# Research log: marine-hull

Newest first. Write the hypothesis and the planned analysis before running an experiment, then the outcome.

## 2026-11-12 · Milestone 6z: month by month along 150 W and 30 W, where does a deck-solar boat go further per day than the 2015 Wave Glider, and where does the ranking flip? (predictions in `experiments/run_flip_map.py`'s docstring, committed in aeffabfa before the 2024 data were fetched)
- Why: 6y's "Next". It compared the two vehicles at five sites in January and July only and found the ranking flips with season and latitude; two months cannot say where the flip sits or how many months a year each vehicle leads, which is what a planner choosing one platform for a year-round line needs. `experiments/fetch_flip_map.py` reads 2024 ERA5 swh and mwp (Open-Meteo `era5_ocean`, CC BY 4.0) and NASA POWER daily all-sky surface shortwave for 26 transect points (150 W and 30 W, 60 S to 60 N every 10 degrees) plus the five corridor sites. 305 of 312 transect point-months kept: 60 S 30 W loses June to December to sea ice. Rules: 6y's 54 boats and functions and 6x's ERA5 law (a_e 0.533, best state s 1.471, worst 0.882), unchanged; winner = central boat (4 m, 30 W, panel 0.20, derate 0.85) sustained speed (A) against the Wave Glider's best state; flip latitude = smallest |lat| on a meridian and side where the Wave Glider wins.
- Outcome, pre-registered (`experiments/results_flip_map.txt`, under 1 s, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests, 154 pass). **P1 held:** in the winter hemisphere's January (north) and July (south) the central boat sustains 0 km/day at all 11 kept points at 40 degrees or more. **P2 held:** within 10 degrees of the equator (A) > 0 in **100%** of 72 point-months. **P3 held:** Spearman(|lat|, months the Wave Glider wins) **0.921** over 26 points. **P4 held:** winter flip latitude **30, 20, 30, 30** (150 W north in January, south in July; 30 W the same). **P5 held:** in the summer peak month the boat wins at 9 of 10 points at 10 to 50 N (July) and 10 of 10 at 10 to 50 S (January). **P6 held:** the Wave Glider's best state wins **40.3%** of transect point-months. **P7 held:** corridor months won by the boat in 2024: Equator-Pacific 12, Florida-Straits 8, Gulf-of-Maine 6, North-Sea 4, Gulf-of-Alaska 3. **P8 held, at the top:** a full 10 kWh pack at the start of each month (B) moves **14.8 pp** of point-months to the boat and none back. **P9 refuted:** dividing the Wave Glider plan by 6x's ERA5/buoy ratio 1.101 changes only **2.6%** of winners, but moves one winter flip latitude by 20 degrees (150 W north in December, 10 to 30, because 10 N in December is a near tie, 67 against 69 km/day).
- Descriptive: months a year the boat wins, mean over the four points at each |lat|: 12 at the equator, 11.75 at 10, 10.75 at 20, 7.5 at 30, 5.5 at 40, 2 at 50. The year-mean of central (A) is 1.5 to 1.9 times the Wave Glider's best-state plan within 10 degrees (85 to 106 against 55 to 64 km/day), about equal at 30 degrees (56 to 70 against 57 to 63) and 0.6 to 0.8 times at 40 (39 to 49 against 64 to 71). The flip latitude swings about 30 degrees through the year: in the north it sits at 30 degrees in January and February and at 50 degrees from April to August, with no flip at all (boat wins to 60 N on 150 W, to 50 N on 30 W) in June; the south mirrors it, 20 to 30 degrees in June and July. Across the 54 boats the share that beats the best-state Wave Glider falls from 67% at the equator to 59% at 20 degrees, 46% at 30, 32% at 40 and 15 to 17% at 50 to 60.
- Reading: the 6y result generalises. A 4 m deck-solar boat with a 30 W payload goes further per day than the 2015 Wave Glider at its best everywhere within about 20 degrees of the equator in every month, and the Wave Glider goes further everywhere poleward of about 40 degrees in its hemisphere's winter half; between, the answer is the season. For a year-round line, the boat leads on the year's mean below about 30 degrees, the Wave Glider above about 35. The pack result says the boat's poor months are energy, not hull: a boat recharged to full at the start of every month (a monthly port call, not a year-long mission) leads on the year's mean to 40 degrees. The flip latitude as defined is fragile to single near-tie points; the winner map and the months-per-latitude counts are the robust outputs, not a single flip number.
- Disclosed after the pre-registration commit: the sun file was renamed from `power_flip_2024.json` to `sun_flip_2024.json` because `tests/test_solar_sites.py` globs `power_*.json`; no rule changed. The 150 W, 60 N point snaps to a coastal ERA5 cell on the Kenai shelf (Wave Glider plan 33 to 50 km/day, well below the other 60-degree points), so its 5 boat-won months are not open-ocean figures.
- Limits: all of 6y's (calm-water solar boat, so the winter storm belts favour the boat more than they should; energy-neutral at the month's mean sun; NASA POWER is a 0.5 by 0.625 degree product; the Wave Glider's own payload power not modelled) and 6x's (one 2015 vehicle sets the law and its best state; ERA5 about 10% high against buoys); one year; two meridians; (B) restarts every month full.
- Next: put both vehicles on the same payload power budget (a published SV3 deck panel area and battery); price added resistance in waves on the solar side at the 40 to 60 degree winter points, where it would push the flip equatorward; a year-long (not monthly-reset) pack campaign along the transect.

## 2026-11-12 · Milestone 6y: with each corridor site's own sun in place of 5c's single 86 km/day, does a deck-solar boat or the 2015 Wave Glider go further per day? (predictions in `experiments/run_wave_vs_sun_sites.py`'s docstring, committed in aef6d913 before the run)
- Why: 6x compared its ERA5 Wave Glider plan with "the 86 km/day deck-solar ceiling", which is the 4 m monohull's 1.0 m/s best-range speed held for 24 h (5c, 6h), the same at every site and season. 5e had already shown the same boat energy-neutral in 7 months at the equator and none at 60 N. NASA POWER daily all-sky surface shortwave for the five corridor sites, January and July 2024, was already on disk (`experiments/data/daily_<site>.json`), so the solar side can use the same site-months as 6x's wave side. Rules: 5e's 54 boats (3/4/5 m, hotel 20/30/40 W, panel 0.15/0.20/0.23, derate 0.70/0.85; 200 kg, calm water); (A) the highest energy-neutral speed at the month's mean insolation, 0 when harvest is below the hotel load; (B) the highest constant speed a full 10 kWh pack (5c's 25% of 200 kg at 200 Wh/kg) carries through the month day by day; (C) (A) at the month's worst 7-day mean; Wave Glider = 6x's plan at the best and worst 2015 state, recomputed by 6x's own functions.
- Outcome, pre-registered (`experiments/results_wave_vs_sun_sites.txt`, under 1 s, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests, 150 pass). **P1 held:** the central 4 m boat (30 W, 0.20, 0.85) sustains **0 km/day** in January at North-Sea (22 W/m2) and Gulf-of-Alaska (14 W/m2), and also at Florida-Straits (136) and Gulf-of-Maine (57). **P2 held:** in July it beats the Wave Glider's best state at **all five** sites (58 to 103 km/day against 43.5 to 54.7). **P3 held:** in January the Wave Glider's best state wins at **4 of 5** (all but Equator-Pacific, 96 against 45). **P4 held:** 86 km/day overstates the central boat in 7 of 10 site-months, and understates it in the other 3 (96 to 103 km/day at the equator in January and at Florida and the Gulf of Maine in July). **P5 held:** over 540 boat-site-months the Wave Glider's best state beats (A) in **61.7%**. **P6 held:** the fastest boat sustains 150 km/day (5 m, 20 W, 0.23, 0.85, Florida-Straits July). **P7 refuted:** the full pack lifts the central boat by **1.17 to 1.64 times** where (A) > 0 (predicted 1.05 to 1.50); the top is Equator-Pacific in July (175 W/m2, 58 to 95 km/day). **P8 held:** the worst week cuts (A) by more than 20% in 3 of 6 site-months, to 0 at North-Sea and Equator-Pacific in July.
- Descriptive: the central boat's harvest equals its 30 W hotel load at **153 W/m2** of daily-mean insolation. Below that it cannot sustain any speed; above it, because propulsion power grows roughly as v^3 at these Froude numbers, speed jumps quickly (175 W/m2 already gives 58 km/day, 247 gives 96). In January no solar boat of the 54 sustains any speed at North-Sea or Gulf-of-Alaska, and the best boat manages 13 km/day at Gulf-of-Maine, where the Wave Glider plans 58 to 75 km/day at its best state and 35 to 45 at its worst. By boat, 5 m hulls with a 20 W load beat the best-state Wave Glider in 70% of the site-months, 4 m with 30 W in 42%, and 3 m hulls with 30 W or more in 2% or none. With a full pack the central boat covers 78 km/day through January at Florida-Straits, where (A) is 0, so near the threshold the battery, not the sun, decides.
- Reading: 6x's "no ocean point reaches the 86 km/day ceiling" is correct as arithmetic but the wrong comparison. The solar boat's distance per day is a threshold function of insolation against hotel load, not a ceiling: in summer and in the tropics a 4 m deck-solar boat at 30 W sustains 1.2 to 2.4 times the 2015 Wave Glider's best-state plan, and in winter above about 40 degrees it sustains nothing while the Wave Glider still plans 58 to 75 km/day. So the ranking flips by season and latitude, not by vehicle. 6x's sentence that a Wave Glider's case against a solar boat is "endurance, weather and payload power, not distance per day" holds in summer and at low latitudes only; in the winter storm belts its case is also distance. One caution against reading that as a free win: the Wave Glider's payload is solar-powered too, and the same 1.15 m2 deck at 22 W/m2 gives about 4 W, so neither vehicle carries a 30 W payload through a North Sea January without a pack.
- Limits: calm-water solar boat (5o onwards price added resistance in waves, which the winter storm belts would raise); daily-mean harvest with no intra-day battery cycle in (A) and (C); NASA POWER is a 0.5 x 0.625 degree satellite-and-reanalysis product, not on-deck measurement, and the panel tilt, soiling and spray sit in the swept derate; one year (2024) and two months; the Wave Glider side carries 6x's limits (one 2015 vehicle, ERA5 about 10% high against buoys); the Wave Glider's own hotel budget is not modelled; boat parameters are 5e's sweeps, not one vendor's datasheet.
- Next: put the Wave Glider's payload power on the same deck-solar budget (a published SV3 panel area and battery, if one can be found) so both vehicles carry the same payload; price added resistance in waves on the solar side at the January storm-belt sites, where the comparison flips; extend both plans to all twelve months with ERA5 and POWER to map the flip latitude by month.

## 2026-11-11 · Milestone 6x: does a Wave Glider's km/day plan from ERA5 transfer to the 2024 buoys, and what does it say over open ocean? (predictions in `experiments/run_waveglider_era5_sites.py`'s docstring, committed in 16e5203f before the 2024 ERA5 data was fetched)
- Why: 6w's reading said 6v's law "can be planned anywhere ERA5 covers, not only at buoys", but that was checked only along the vehicle's own track in open Hawaiian seas. 6v already has a measured plan at the 2024 NDBC buoys (buoy WVHT and APD), so replacing the buoy with ERA5 at each buoy's grid point tests the transfer where a 0.5 degree model does worst (shelf, Gulf, coast). `experiments/fetch_era5_sites.py` reads ERA5 swh and mwp (Open-Meteo `era5_ocean`, CC BY 4.0) for January and July 2024 at the 11 buoys, the programme's five corridor sites and a 10 degree grid (371 ocean points in January, 364 in July). Rules: 6w's ERA5 vehicle law (a_e 0.533, state index s_m from ERA5 at the vehicle; best month June 2015, s 1.471; worst October, 0.882); km/day = 86.4 s mean (Hs/Tm)^a_e.
- Outcome, pre-registered (`experiments/results_waveglider_era5_sites.txt`, under 1 s, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests). The docstring says 22 buoy-months; 6v's table has 20 (9 buoys in January), a miscount in the rules, not a dropped site. **P1 refuted, just:** ERA5 plans a median **1.101 times** the buoy plan (range 0.910 to 1.216). **P2 refuted:** 75% of buoy-months within 15% (predicted 80%). **P3 refuted in January:** Spearman of the buoys' plans **-0.12 in January**, 0.93 in July. **P4 held:** ERA5 site ratios 1.177 (January; 6v 1.123) and 1.644 (July; 6v 1.725). **P5 held:** the July shelf and Gulf buoy-months miss by more (mean |log ratio| 0.132 against 0.081). **P6 held:** no grid point plans above the 86 km/day deck-solar ceiling at the vehicle's best month in either month (maximum 85.6 at 50 N 40 W in January, 85.4 at 50 S 120 E in July). **P7 held:** the fastest point is in the winter hemisphere's 50 degree band in both months. **P8 held:** 32.6% of grid hours lie outside the mission's 5 to 95% q_e range (0.144 to 0.348 m/s). **P9 refuted:** Florida-Straits is lowest of the five corridor sites in July (43.5 km/day) but the Equator-Pacific is lowest in January (44.7; long 8.6 s swell, low Hs/T), and the North Sea is highest in both (74.5 / 54.7).
- Grid, best state: median 63.0 km/day in January and 61.1 in July (10th to 90th 50 to 77 and 48 to 79). By latitude band the winter hemisphere's 40 to 70 degree seas plan 75 (January, north) and 77 km/day (July, south), the tropics 56 to 57, and the summer high latitudes 53 to 70. At the worst state every figure scales by 0.60 (fleet medians 36 / 29 km/day at the buoys).
- Post hoc (`post_hoc()` in the same script, written after reading P1 to P9): the buoy-to-buoy differences in the ERA5/buoy ratio are almost entirely calibration. The ratio implied by each buoy-month's hour-matched median Hs_e/WVHT and Tm_e/APD, with the vehicle-side calibration divided out (Hs_e/Hs_ob 0.960, Tm_e/T_avg 1.285), correlates **0.991** with the observed ratio (residual sd 0.010 against 0.073 raw). The main term is period: ERA5 mwp runs 1.196 times buoy APD over all matched hours, against 1.285 times the vehicle's MOSE average period, so the state index fitted on the vehicle side carries a period bias that does not hold at the buoys.
- Reading: the ERA5 plan transfers to the buoys to about +10% (+8% in mean log), and site-to-site to the size of the July spread: in summer it ranks the buoys as the buoys do and gets the 1.6 to 1.7 site ratio. In winter, when 6v found every buoy within 12% of each other, the ERA5 ranking is noise, because the site-dependent calibration error (sd 7%) is as large as the site differences. So ERA5 can say which season and which ocean, not which of two nearby winter sites. The calibration that matters is the ratio of the model's mean period to the vehicle's own period measure, and it varies with sea type (0.88 to 1.43 at the buoy-months). Over open ocean the plan is 50 to 79 km/day at the 2015 vehicle's best month and nowhere reaches the 86 km/day deck-solar ceiling (the capability wind drone of 6o reached 1.47 times it in January at the buoys). Unless a vehicle is faster than the 2015 one at its best, a Wave Glider's case against a solar boat is endurance, weather and payload power, not distance per day. About a third of the ocean's hours are outside the seas the law was fitted on, mostly the long-swell tropics (low Hs/T) and the winter storm belts (high).
- Limits: one vehicle and mission sets the law and the state index; ERA5 is a reanalysis (no forecast-lead error); the grid uses the nearest 0.5 degree cell to each 10 degree point, so coastal and ice-edge cells are crude; January and July 2024 only; the solar ceiling (86 km/day) is milestone 5c's single figure, not site-specific; the grid mean uses 20 quantile bins (max error 0.29% on the 32 site-months).
- Next: carry the site calibration (Tm_e/APD) into the plan where a buoy exists and test whether it fixes the January ranking out of sample (fit on 2024, test on 2023 buoy files); a second fielded vehicle in calm, short seas (the 33% of open-ocean hours outside the law's range); a site-specific solar ceiling from the corridor sites' NASA POWER files for a like-for-like map of wave against sun.

## 2026-11-11 · Milestone 6w: with ERA5 reanalysis waves at the vehicle's position in place of 6u's on-board oracle, does a model sea state keep the Wave Glider's speed law and its forecast skill? (predictions in `experiments/run_waveglider_era5.py`'s docstring, committed in ec1f8f03 before the run)
- Why: 6u's H3 forecast used the observed target-hour on-board Hs and period (an oracle), and 6v planned km/day only where a buoy exists. A planner has a wave model, not the future sensor record. ERA5 (Hersbach et al. 2020) via Open-Meteo's marine API (`models=era5_ocean`, CC BY 4.0, Copernicus) gives swh, mwp and pp1d hourly on a 0.5 degree grid; `experiments/fetch_era5_waveglider.py` reads it at the 109 grid points the vehicle passed and joins all 3,657 mission hours. A reanalysis carries model error but no forecast-lead error, so it bounds from above what an archived forecast would give. Rules: 6u's S2 hours (3,281), q = Hs/T; a from log u ~ month + a log q; 6u's forecast rules with the ratio law for both the oracle (Mob) and the reanalysis (Me); 6v's monthly plan as a descriptive check.
- Outcome, pre-registered (`experiments/results_waveglider_era5.txt`, about 60 s, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests). Calibration: median Hs_e/Hs_ob **0.960**, Tm_e/T_avg **1.285**, so **P5 held**. **P1 refuted, on the good side:** the within-month correlation of log q is **0.866** (predicted 0.5 to 0.85; the buoy's was 0.20), log Hs 0.887 and log T only 0.587. **P2 refuted:** **a_e = 0.533 (95% 0.447 to 0.618)** against a_ob 0.534 on the same hours (difference +0.001, -0.051 to +0.060). Errors-in-variables predicted 0.494, and there is no attenuation. With ERA5's peak period in place of mwp the exponent falls to 0.329, so the period definition matters. **P3 refuted:** at 24 h Me/M1 is **0.955 (0.812 to 1.135)**. The oracle with the same ratio law gets 0.880 (0.744 to 1.043); 6u's H3 got 0.767 with the free (b, c) pair. **P4 refuted, just:** Me recovers **38%** of the oracle's 24 h gain (predicted 40 to 90%). **P6 held:** at 72 h Me/M1 is **0.845 (0.700 to 1.014)**, against the oracle's 0.772 (0.655 to 0.918), which is **68%** of the oracle's gain. At 6 h neither term helps (1.004, 1.011). Descriptive plan: the ERA5 monthly km/day is within 0.4 km/day of the on-board plan in every month (June 50.4 against 50.2, October 35.5 against 35.2), and both are within 2 km/day of the observed means (August 40.3 against 42.1, the step month).
- Reading: for planning, a 0.5 degree reanalysis replaces the vehicle's own wave sensor at this vehicle's sites. It gives the same exponent and the same monthly km/day, so 6v's law can be planned anywhere ERA5 covers, not only at buoys, using ERA5's mwp (not its peak period). For hour-to-day speed forecasts it is weaker. Nothing helps at 6 h; at a day ahead the reanalysis adds about 4% skill (not significant); at three days it adds about 15%, two thirds of what the oracle gives. The 24 h oracle gain that the ratio law gets is 12%, not 6u H3's quarter, because H3 used the free pair; that sentence in 6u should be quoted with its model. The missing attenuation (lambda 0.92, a_e equal to a_ob) means either the MOSE record is itself about as noisy as ERA5 against the true forcing, or the vehicle responds to the larger-scale sea the model resolves. The data here cannot tell these apart.
- Limits: one vehicle and mission in open Hawaiian and North Pacific seas, where a 0.5 degree model does well; ERA5 is a reanalysis, so real forecast skill at 24 to 72 h will be lower; the ratio law forces b = -c, and the free pair was not pre-registered for ERA5; the grid point is the nearest 0.5 degree cell, with no interpolation; one cell (20.0 N, 156.0 W, 339 hours) sits in the lee of the Big Island, and its hours were left in, as the rules fixed.
- Next: plan km/day from ERA5 at 6v's 2024 buoy sites and compare with 6v's buoy plan, then at open-ocean survey sites with no buoy; for real forecast skill, a vehicle fielded after 2017, when an archived WAVEWATCH III run exists on ERDDAP (`NWW3_Global_Best` starts 2017-01-01).

## 2026-11-11 · Milestone 6v: with 6u's on-board law U = s (Hs/T)^0.53, is a Wave Glider's planned km/day at the 2024 buoys still set more by the vehicle than by the site? (predictions in `experiments/run_waveglider_site_period.py`'s docstring, committed in 9c6975ba before the run)
- Why: 6u's "Next" and its reading: 6r's km/day rows used the buoy-attenuated Hs^0.218 and no period, so they "understate the spread between sites" and must be rerun before the paper quotes them. The 2024 NDBC file already carries APD (average period) every hour, so this is an offline rerun. Rules: vehicle-state index s_m = median of u / (Hs_ob / T_avg)^0.534 over each 2015 month with 100+ on-board hours (June to October; May has no MOSE); km/day = 86.4 x mean of s (WVHT / APD)^0.534 per buoy-month; the free pair (Hs^0.542 T^-0.711) and a sensor-to-buoy calibration (k_H, k_T on hours with the buoy within 150 km) as checks; coverage = share of hours outside the mission's 5 to 95% Hs/T range.
- Outcome, pre-registered (`experiments/results_waveglider_site_period.txt`, under 1 s, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests): s_m 1.248 (Jun), 1.154, 0.851, 0.807, 0.745 (Oct), **state ratio 1.676**. Fleet medians **55.4 (Jan) / 45.2 (Jul) km/day** at the best state, 33.0 / 27.0 at the worst (6r: 52.4 / 48.8 and 32.0 / 29.8). **P1 refuted:** the site ratio rose in July (**1.725**, from 1.335) but fell in January (**1.123**, from 1.203). **P2 held** (1.676 in 1.4 to 1.9). **P3 held:** between-state variance share 0.659 (6r 0.825); between months 0.152 (0.058), buoys 0.119 (0.106). **P4 held:** in July 42036, 42040 and 44013 have 95.2%, 87.5% and 94.5% of hours below the mission's 5th percentile Hs/T (0.184 m/s); 41009 92%. **P5 held:** best buoy-month 59.0 (46013, January). **P6 held:** k_H 1.090, k_T 0.988 (486 hours), +5.4% on both fleet medians. **P7 held:** the free pair is within +0.1% (Jan) and +1.4% (Jul); its site ratios 1.079 / 1.667.
- Post hoc (written after reading P1 to P7, disclosed in the script): median u / (s_m (Hs/T)^a) by mission Hs/T bin is 1.083 (below 0.12, 15 hours), 0.985 (0.12 to 0.15, 20 h), 0.905 (0.15 to 0.184, 128 h), then 0.98 to 1.02 in the bulk and 0.94 above 0.475. The law shows no large bias down to Hs/T 0.12, but on 35 hours; the July Gulf and Nantucket medians (0.095 to 0.113) sit at the bottom of that.
- Reading: once period enters, January's Pacific lead mostly goes: its 2.3 to 2.6 m seas come with 8 to 9 s APD, so Hs/T (0.28 to 0.31) is about the Atlantic and Gulf's (0.25 to 0.30), and every January buoy plans 52 to 59 km/day at the best state. That reverses 6s's "the Pacific lead is wave height", which rested on the buoy-attenuated exponent. In July calm short seas (Gulf, Nantucket, 41009) cut the plan to 32 to 38 km/day against 48 to 55 in the Pacific, so site is now as large a lever as the 2015 vehicle's month-to-month state (1.73 against 1.68), and season moves from 6% to 15% of the variance. The planning advice becomes: in winter carry the vehicle's logged speed and ignore the site; in summer the site's Hs/T matters as much, but the slow-site figures rest on 35 mission hours at comparable heave velocity and should be read as a lower region of uncertain size, not a number. 6u's sentence for the paper (speed a sub-linear function of heave velocity) stands; 6r's "mostly the vehicle" holds in January only.
- Limits: one vehicle and mission (Hawaii, Hs 1.2 to 2.3 m); MOSE "average period" and NDBC APD may differ in definition (median ratio 0.99 near buoys, but measured 100 to 150 km apart and only in Hawaiian seas); the a = 0.534 exponent is 6u's post hoc fit; the state index carries whatever made June 68% faster than October, which 6t and 6u leave partly unexplained; no current, no wind, no payload power.
- Next: a second fielded vehicle in calmer, shorter seas (the Gulf or US east coast) to test the law below Hs/T 0.15, where the July plans sit; an archived WAVEWATCH III forecast in place of 6u's H3 oracle.

## 2026-11-11 · Milestone 6u: with the Wave Glider's own on-board waves and wind in place of a distant buoy, is its weak sea-state response real? (predictions in `experiments/run_waveglider_onboard.py`'s docstring, committed in f35590df before the run)
- Why: every Hs in 6p to 6t came from the nearest NDBC buoy (median 144 km, up to 300 km; up to about 1,000 km post hoc). Under errors-in-variables a noisy regressor shrinks a slope toward 0, so the Hs^0.22 response could be an artefact of the match. The same NOAA ERD ERDDAP server carries the vehicle's own Datawell MOSE wave record (`LiquidR_HBG3_2015_mose_v2`), its weather station and telemetry `total_power`, `sub_heading` and `desired_heading`, none used before (6t's note that the telemetry had no power field was wrong: it was simply not fetched). `experiments/fetch_waveglider_onboard.py` builds 3,657 hourly rows, 3,281 usable with MOSE waves.
- Outcome, pre-registered (`experiments/results_waveglider_onboard.txt`, 80 s, stdlib, deterministic; 6 new tests). **P1 held:** on the 246 6q-training hours with both records, b_ob 0.404 (95% 0.153 to 0.469) against b_buoy 0.092 on the same hours. On all 3,281 hours from June, **b_ob = 0.354 (0.299 to 0.415)**. **P2 refuted, worse than predicted:** the within-month correlation of log Hs on board and at the buoy is **0.20** (predicted 0.3 to 0.8); the attenuation factor is 0.52. **P3 refuted:** at a fixed state the MOSE average-period exponent is **c = -0.71 (-0.80 to -0.61)**, with b rising to 0.54; 6s's null (DPD from a distant buoy, coarsely binned) was the proxy, not the physics. **P4 refuted:** headwind d = -0.0012 per m/s (-0.0044 to +0.0022), so a 5 m/s headwind costs 0.6%. **P5 held:** an on-board oracle Hs alone makes the 24 h persistence forecast worse (1.17 of M1; 0.97 to 1.43). **P6 refuted:** across 7 August on-board Hs fell 24% (2.43 to 1.85 m; wind -10%), and the Hs-and-headwind model explains 27% of the log step, under the third predicted. **P7 refuted:** `total_power` +2.9%.
- Post hoc (`experiments/run_waveglider_onboard_posthoc.py`, written after reading the above; exploratory). H1: log u on log(Hs/T_avg) beats Hs alone by 1,430 BIC, with exponent **0.53**. The free pair is better again by 154 BIC, and b + c = -0.17 (-0.24 to -0.10), so the response is close to, but not exactly, a power of heave velocity. H2: `total_power` follows the sun (median 518k at local 08:00, 640k at 14:00), so it is a battery energy level in unlabelled units. The local-day minimum on 6 August (308k) is the lowest of the mission; the step began at 05 local on 7 August, the 6th-lowest day. Median overnight drain was 5,433 per hour in the week before and 5,195 after (-4%; one night before drained 11,310), so no large load was visibly switched off at the step. H3: an oracle Hs-and-period term (b 0.53, c -0.85 fitted on June and July) cuts the persistence error to **0.946 / 0.767 / 0.674** at 6 / 24 / 72 h (24 h 95% 0.674 to 0.874). H4: the Hs-and-period model explains **30%** of the August week-scale log step, **73%** of the June drop and 52% of its recovery.
- Reading: the buoy match hid most of the sea-state signal. With on-board waves this Wave Glider's through-water speed is a sub-linear function of heave velocity, about (Hs/T)^0.5. That is not the quasi-steady model's linear law (6p's refutation stands), but it is far from the "sea state barely matters" of 6q to 6s. For planning, 6q's advice changes: recent logged speed plus an on-board (or good forecast) Hs and period beats recent speed alone by about a quarter a day ahead and a third at three days. The August step stays mostly vehicle-side (about 70% unexplained, abrupt within the hour, GPS-confirmed in 6t), and the battery low the morning before is a lead for the log, not a cause. The June event that 6t called a reversible event is mostly a calm spell: Hs 1.78 to 1.21 m, period up, wind 5.2 to 2.9 m/s. 6r's km/day rows used the attenuated exponent and no period, so they understate the spread between sites; they need rerunning with (Hs/T)^0.53 before the paper quotes them.
- Limits: one vehicle and mission; the MOSE sits on the vehicle's float, whose motion is the measurement, so a speed-dependent bias in it cannot be ruled out (the encounter period shifts by roughly u/c_p, about 4% at 0.5 m/s in 6 s waves, too small to make c = -0.71 by itself); the H3 forecast uses the observed target-hour sea state (an oracle) and is post hoc; `total_power`'s units are not documented; headwind uses `sub_heading` as the course through the water.
- Next: rerun 6r's buoy plan with (Hs/T)^0.53 from the 2024 NDBC period records; replace H3's oracle with an archived WAVEWATCH III forecast to get an honest forecast skill; ask for the MAGI pilot log about 6 to 7 August 2015.

## 2026-11-11 · Milestone 6t: is the Wave Glider's mid-mission speed loss a step (an event) or a ramp (fouling)? (predictions in `experiments/run_waveglider_step.py`'s docstring, committed before the run)
- Why: 6p to 6s found a vehicle state (about 30% between July and August 2015) that decides this vehicle's speed and km/day plan, with no recorded cause. Fouling grows smoothly; a mode or thruster change, damage or a tangle is a step. The shape tells a planner whether to carry a decay rate or watch for an event. Pre-registered: daily median of log u - 0.218 log Hs (hours <= 300 km from a buoy, from 2015-06-01), models constant, ramp R, step S, step + slope SR and hinge H by BIC, a 5-day moving-block bootstrap of the step.
- Outcome, pre-registered (`experiments/results_waveglider_step.txt`, 7 s, stdlib, deterministic; 4 new tests, 128 pass): the 300 km rule leaves **no qualifying day from 13 June to 18 August** (the vehicle was 900+ km from every buoy in July), so the step's location is not identified: S puts tau at the gap's edge (13 June) and any day inside it fits the same. **P1 refuted:** the ramp beats the step (BIC_R - BIC_S = -6.5); SR is lowest. **P2 refuted** (tau 13 June, outside 15 July to 20 August, and uninformative). **P3 held:** the step ratio is 0.627 (0.57 to 0.70). **P4 held:** from tau on, log speed falls 0.23%/day (95% -0.33 to -0.13). **P5 held as written** (straightness -0.28 across tau) but the tau is an artefact of the gap, so it says nothing about a mode change. I did not foresee the gap when writing the rules; I should have counted qualifying days per month first.
- Post hoc (written after seeing the gap, disclosed in the script): every hour at any buoy distance, raw speed (b = 0) and b = 0.248 (6q's all-distance exponent). **The step model beats the ramp by 15 to 21 BIC**, and S puts the break on **2015-08-08** (bootstrap 95% 5 to 10 August at b = 0.248; 31 July to 14 August raw), ratio 0.70 to 0.74. Hour by hour, through-water speed falls from 0.63 to 0.51 m/s between 14 and 15 UTC on 7 August and stays at 0.44 to 0.48 for days, while the nearest buoy's Hs does not change (1.9 to 2.4 m, though 950 km away). The GPS track drops too (over-ground median 0.567 to 0.433 m/s over the 7 days either side, ratio 0.76, against 0.71 through the water), so it is the vehicle, not the water-speed sensor. After the step, speed keeps falling at 0.20 to 0.27%/day (95% about -0.36 to -0.10), about 7% a month, which is the size a fouling ramp would be; with b = 0.248 the step-plus-slope model is best (BIC 6.6 below S).
- A second week-scale drop, on **12 to 13 June** (7-day ratio 0.67 through the water, 0.61 over ground), **recovered on 20 June** to 0.62 to 0.73 m/s. The August one did not recover in the 88 days left. Hour-scale 24 h dips of the same size (31 May, 26 August) recover within a day. Telemetry samples per hour fall from 12 (May to July) to 11, 10, 9 and 6 (August to November), beginning in the days after the August step; a reporting-rate cut is what a power-saving schedule would look like, but the data do not say why.
- Reading: the "30% drift" of 6p is not mainly fouling. It is mostly one hour-scale event on 7 August (-25 to -30%, persistent) on top of a slow decline of about 0.2 to 0.3%/day; and a similar-sized event in June reversed itself after a week. Events that start within an hour and can reverse fit a changed setting (thruster or mode), a tangle that is later shed, or a fault, better than growth. For planning: 6q's 24 h persistence forecast already absorbs a step within a day; a design study should carry steps of about -30% as discrete risks and a decay of about 7% a month, not one smooth rate.
- Limits: one vehicle and mission; the cause of either event is not in the telemetry (no thruster, mode or power fields in the ERDDAP dataset); the post-hoc block uses buoy Hs from up to about 1,000 km away, which is close to no correction (raw and b = 0.248 agree); the 7 August hour is read off the data after the fact, so its significance is not tested against a null of random drops (the 7-day scan puts it 2nd of 3,227 hours, behind the reversible June event).
- Next: find the MAGI mission's cruise or pilot log (UT Austin, Villareal) for 7 August and 12 to 20 June 2015; a second fielded vehicle when a data host answers; carry "steps plus decay" into 6r's km/day plan as a risk band.

## 2026-11-11 · Milestone 6s: at a fixed vehicle state, does wave period change a Wave Glider's speed, and does it give back the Pacific buoys' lead? (predictions in `experiments/run_waveglider_period.py`'s docstring, committed before the run)
- Why: 6p's P2 found longer period slower at fixed height (residual against DPD -0.260), but pooled over a mission with a 30% vehicle drift; 6q fitted the height exponent at fixed state (month intercepts, b = 0.218) with no period term; 6r's km/day plan used Hs alone and its Pacific buoys, which have the longest swell (median DPD 12.9 s in January against 5.6 to 7.7 s elsewhere), gave the most distance. Same data as 6p to 6r. F2: log u = month intercepts + b2 log Hs + c log DPD on 6q's 416 training hours; 1-day block bootstrap on c; 6q's forecast test with the period ratio added (M2c); 6r's plan with the period term.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_waveglider_period.txt`, under 2 s, stdlib, deterministic; 3 new tests): b2 = 0.224, **c = -0.025 (95% -0.271 to 0.170)**. **P1 refuted:** the sign is as predicted but the interval spans 0 (descriptive: -0.046 on all hours within 300 km, -0.147 at any distance). **P2 held:** b2 0.224 > b 0.218, barely. **P3 held:** M2c/M2 at 24 h 0.991 (0.979 to 1.001); M2c ties M2 at every horizon (MAE 0.035 / 0.047 / 0.052 m/s). **P4 held, trivially:** the Pacific buoys' mean over the fleet median falls from 1.136 to 1.119 (January) and 1.111 to 1.104 (July). **P5 held:** site ratios 1.186 (Jan) and 1.335 (Jul), state ratio 1.639 unchanged. Every buoy-month moves by -0.5% to +1.2%; fleet medians 53.1 / 49.1 km/day at the best state (6r 52.4 / 48.8), 32.4 / 30.0 at the worst.
- Reading: once the vehicle state is held by month, wave period carries no measurable effect on this vehicle's speed, so most of 6p's pooled period residual was the mission drift (Hs and period both rose from June to October while the vehicle slowed), with what remains at far buoys (c -0.15 at any distance) indistinguishable from buoy mismatch. For planning, 6r's rows stand: the Pacific's 10 to 14% lead is from wave height, not offset by long-period swell, and the vehicle state still outweighs site by about two to one. A planner can drop period from a Wave Glider km/day model without loss at this data's resolution.
- Limits: one vehicle and mission; DPD is a dominant-period proxy from a buoy up to 300 km away, and it is coarsely binned at NDBC (four Pacific buoys share a 12.9 s January median), which attenuates any period slope toward 0; 416 fit hours over 47 days give a wide interval, so a period effect of the size 6p's residual implied (|c| near 0.25) is not ruled out at the edge; no directional spread or swell/wind-sea split.
- Next: unchanged from 6r, a second fielded vehicle with co-located waves once a data host answers; with that, a swell/wind-sea split (spectral data) rather than one period.

## 2026-11-11 · Milestone 6r: at the programme's 2024 buoys, is a Wave Glider's planned km/day set by the site or by the vehicle state? (predictions in `experiments/run_waveglider_site.py`'s docstring, committed before the run)
- Why: 6q's reading guessed, by arithmetic only, that site differences are smaller than the 2015 mission drift, so 6p's km/day rows are set more by the vehicle state assumed than by the site. This run checks that on the hourly 2024 NDBC records already in `experiments/data/ndbc_waves_2024.csv`. Data hosts (NDBC, ERDDAP, SECOORA) refused this session, so a second vehicle was not reachable. Model: U = s_m * Hs^b with b = 0.218 (6q), s_m = median of u / Hs^b over each 2015 mission month with at least 100 hours; km/day = 86.4 * mean over the buoy-month's hours of s * Hs^b, at the best and worst month's s.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_waveglider_site.txt`, under 1 s, stdlib, deterministic; 3 new tests): s_m runs 0.361 (May), 0.540, 0.571 (Jul), 0.404, 0.401, 0.348 (Oct), so the state ratio is **1.639** (Jul over Aug alone, 1.41). **P1 held:** the site ratio (best over worst buoy in a month) is 1.203 in January (9 buoys) and 1.335 in July (11), below the state ratio in both. **P2 held:** January over July fleet median at a fixed state is 1.075. **P3 held:** the best buoy-month is 60.7 km/day (46013, January), under the 86 km/day solar ceiling. **P4 held:** b from 0.188 to 0.248 moves the fleet medians by -1.6% to +2.0%. **P5 held:** at the worst state the fleet medians are 32.0 (Jan) and 29.8 (Jul) km/day, inside 6p's power-law range widened by 10%; at the best state they are 52.4 and 48.8.
- Descriptive: over the buoy x month x state grid, 82.5% of the variance in log km/day is between the two states, 10.6% between buoys and 5.8% between months. The Pacific buoys (46011, 46013, 46026, 46054; median Hs 1.35 to 2.55 m) give the most distance, and the Gulf and Nantucket buoys in July (42036, 42040, 44013; median Hs 0.40 to 0.46 m) the least, 41 to 42 km/day at the best state.
- Reading: with the measured Hs^0.22 response, a Wave Glider plan at these sites is about 30 to 61 km/day, and which end it sits at is mostly decided by the vehicle (whatever made July 64% faster than October in 2015), not by where it goes or the season. Site choice is worth up to 20% in winter and 34% in summer, the vehicle state 64% (41% for the July-to-August step alone, which is also above both site ratios). The planning consequence is the same as 6q's: carry the vehicle's logged speed into the plan and treat the site as a correction of a fifth to a third.
- Limits: the state index comes from one 2015 mission in Hawaii; its 64% range includes May, the launch month, whose low speed may be transit or commissioning rather than fouling; the exponent is from that one vehicle and is applied to Atlantic, Gulf and Pacific seas it never sailed; DPD and period are ignored (6p's P2 found longer period is slower at fixed height, which would narrow the Pacific's lead); no current, no payload power.
- Next: unchanged from 6q, a second fielded vehicle with matched waves once a data host answers; a period term at fixed state (6p's P2 residual) to see whether long-period Pacific swell gives back part of the site spread.

## 2026-11-11 · Milestone 6q: once a vehicle-state term carries the Wave Glider's slow drift, does sea state add forecast skill, and does a time trend extrapolate? (predictions in `experiments/run_waveglider_drift.py`'s docstring, committed before the run)
- Why: 6p ended with a 30% speed loss over the mission that hides the sea-state response in pooled data, and a foil model that loses to a constant. A planner forecasting distance made good needs to know which input carries the signal: the vehicle's own recent speed, the sea state or a trend in time. Same data and test set as 6p (hours from 2015-08-01, at most 300 km from a buoy, speed at least 0.1 m/s). Four forecasts at horizons of 6, 24 and 72 h: M0 is the training median (6p's constant); M1 is the median speed over the 24 h before the forecast origin; M2 is M1 times (Hs_target / trailing Hs)^b, with b the within-month exponent fitted on training hours and the observed target Hs used as an oracle wave forecast (which flatters M2); M3 is log u on log Hs plus days since launch, fitted on May to July. A target counts only if its trailing window has at least 6 hours at every horizon, so all four models are scored on the same 761 hours.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_waveglider_drift.txt`, under 1 s, stdlib, deterministic): MAE at 6 / 24 / 72 h was 0.135 for M0, 0.034 / 0.048 / 0.054 for M1 and 0.035 / 0.047 / 0.052 m/s for M2. **P1 held:** at 24 h M1/M0 is 0.354 (block bootstrap, 1-day blocks over 37 days: 0.294 to 0.422). **P2 refuted:** M2/M1 at 24 h is 0.991 (0.860 to 1.163), so even an oracle sea-state forecast adds no measurable skill once recent speed is known. **P3 held:** M3's May to July trend is +3.5% a day in log speed, so extrapolated over Aug to Oct it gives an MAE of 35.9 m/s; a time-since-launch trend is useless as a forecast. **P4 held:** the within-month exponent b is 0.218 on training hours (0.188 on all hours within 300 km, 0.248 at any distance). Doubling Hs raises speed by about 16% at a fixed vehicle state, where the quasi-steady model says 100%. **P5 held:** M1's skill over M0 falls from +0.751 to +0.646 to +0.600 at 6, 24 and 72 h.
- Descriptive, 150 km cut (281 targets): the constant is already good (MAE 0.065). M1's skill falls from +0.52 to +0.23, then to -0.08 at 72 h, and M2 is again no better than M1 (M2/M1 1.04 / 1.10 / 1.03). Near-buoy hours drift less, so persistence's edge fades within three days there.
- Reading: for this vehicle the forecastable part of speed is a slowly varying vehicle state (fouling, mode or thruster use; the data do not say which), not sea state. A 24 h trailing median predicts the next day's hourly speed to about 0.05 m/s, a third of the constant's error. The sea-state response is real but small (U proportional to Hs^0.2 within a month), and it sits inside the hour-to-hour noise. For the programme this means a Wave Glider's distance per day should be planned from its recent logged speed. A wave forecast will barely change the plan, while a hull-cleaning or drift model could. By the exponent alone (arithmetic, not rerun on the 2024 buoys), a site with 50% higher Hs gains about 8% in speed, while the drift between June-July and Aug-Oct was 30%. So 6p's km/day rows are probably set more by the vehicle state assumed than by the site; rerunning them with Hs^0.22 is a cheap next step.
- Limits: one vehicle and one mission; the oracle Hs is from a buoy up to 300 km away, so a better-matched wave record might add skill that this one cannot show; 37 test days make the bootstrap intervals wide (M2/M1 cannot rule out a 14% gain or a 16% loss); M1 needs telemetry from the last day, which a planner has but a design study does not.
- Next: the same test on a second vehicle with on-board or co-located wave data (SECOORA ERDDAP deployments, buoy-matched); a drift model on speed residuals (step changes against a smooth fouling ramp) if a cleaning or recovery date can be found for the 2015 mission.

## 2026-11-11 · Milestone 6p: does a quasi-steady foil model predict a fielded Wave Glider's speed from buoy sea state? (predictions in `experiments/run_waveglider.py`'s docstring, committed before the run)
- Why: the programme compares sun (5c to 5i) and wind (6h to 6o) as free propulsion but had no wave-powered body, though track 23 lists one (milestone 10, `marine-wave`). NOAA SWFSC ERD's ERDDAP serves a fielded Wave Glider's telemetry (Liquid Robotics "Honey Badger", MAGI mission, UT Austin, Hawaii to 30 N, 20 May to 4 Nov 2015; "may be used and redistributed for free"), with through-water speed, so current is not in it. NDBC 2015 standard-met files give wave height and period. `experiments/fetch_waveglider.py` matches each hour (6+ samples) to the nearest buoy with a wave record within 1 h; `src/marine_hull/waveglider.py` is a two-body quasi-steady foil model (float follows heave, rigid tether, passive pitch to a stop, JONSWAP sea) with one fitted ratio r = wing area / body CdA. Because every angle depends on U/w only, it predicts U = Hs * f(Tp): linear in height at fixed period.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_waveglider.txt`, 47 s; 6 new tests, 118 pass): 1,245 hours within 300 km (median 0.44 m/s, 37.7 km/day; Hs median 1.78 m, DPD 10 s). **P1 refuted:** Spearman(speed, Hs) 0.117 (predicted >= 0.3). **P2 held:** at fixed height, longer period is slower (residual against DPD -0.260). **P3 refuted:** held out on Aug to Oct, model MAE 0.165 m/s (bias +0.123) against 0.138 for the training median and 0.194 for a power law U = 0.387 Hs^0.669; no foil cell of the 18-cell sweep (theta_stop 20 to 40, AR 1 to 4, CL_max 0.8 to 1.2) beats the constant (best 0.153). At 150 km the constant wins by more (0.069 against 0.193). **P4 refuted:** median speed at Hs >= 2.5 m over Hs <= 1.5 m is 1.054 in the data, 1.931 in the model (mean-height ratio 2.33). **P5 held:** at the programme's 2024 buoys the fitted model gives medians of 50.1 km/day (January, 9 buoys) and 36.5 (July, 11), the power law 42.5 and 32.9, all below the 86 km/day solar ceiling and 6o's capability wind drone (126 and 80).
- Post hoc (not pre-registered, all distances): monthly median speed 0.39 (May), 0.60, 0.64, 0.46, 0.47, 0.41 m/s (Oct) while monthly Hs rises 1.31 to 2.07 m. Within months speed tracks Hs (Spearman +0.26, +0.52, +0.02, +0.45, +0.76, +0.26); divided by its month's median, the height ratio becomes 1.18 and the pooled Spearman +0.37. So a slow loss of about 30% between July and August, of unknown cause (fouling is the usual suspect for a months-long Wave Glider mission; the data carry no record of it, nor of thruster use or mission mode), is as big as the whole sea-state effect, and hides it in pooled data.
- Reading: the quasi-steady linear-in-Hs shape is wrong for this vehicle; even drift-corrected, speed rises about 18% from 1.5 m to 2.5 m seas against the model's 90%. Saturation mechanisms the model omits (sub lag and tether slack, float not following short waves, stall, wing pitch limits beyond a single stop) are candidates; which one was not tested. For the programme's question, a wave-powered glider makes roughly 33 to 50 km/day at the 2024 buoys, about half the solar ceiling and well below a wind drone in winter, but it spends no battery on propulsion, so per kWh it is off the scale the other bodies share; the comparison it supports is distance per day and payload power, not range per kWh.
- Limits: one vehicle, one mission, one season; nearest buoy up to 300 km away (median of kept hours 144 km); DPD stands in for the peak period; hourly means of 5-minute speeds; no foil geometry sourced (the fit absorbs it); the 2024 buoy runs use a model already refuted in shape, so the power law row is the fairer one.
- Next: a second fielded Wave Glider with on-board wave measurements (SECOORA's FAU HBOI 2016 and BOEM 2017-18 deployments are on erddap.secoora.org; their variables list current and speed but no waves, so they still need buoy matching); a model with sub lag and a tether to test the saturation; a fouling or drift term fitted on time since launch.

## 2026-11-10 · Milestone 3u: what form factor does the measured SUBOFF bare hull have once its wetted area comes from the hull equations? (predictions in `experiments/run_suboff_bare.py`'s docstring, committed before the run)
- Why: 3t could only bound the bare-hull (1+k) because the AFF-1 wetted area was missing (DTIC blocked). The bare hull is defined by closed-form equations (Groves, Huang and Chang 1989), coded in two public GitHub implementations that agree on every constant; `src/marine_hull/suboff.py` integrates them (frustum rule, cosine spacing): 0.69921 m3 and 5.98826 m2, against the report's 0.699 and 5.988 as quoted second-hand (arXiv:2503.19674, Table 1, not opened: arXiv and Zenodo refused this session). That gives a measured bare-hull form factor, the thing the programme sweeps (k 0.1 to 0.3) for REMUS-class AUVs, and a test of Hoerner's textbook formula 1 + 1.5 (d/l)^1.5 + 7 (d/l)^3, which is how a designer would set it without a tank.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_suboff_bare.txt`, under 1 s; 5 new tests, 112 pass): AFF-1 implied (1+k) 1.103, 1.169, 1.178, 1.186, 1.172, 1.141 over 3.05 to 9.25 m/s (nu 1.0e-6). **P1 held:** mean above 5 m/s 1.169 (1.143 to 1.179 over nu 0.95 to 1.15e-6), inside 1.05 to 1.20. **P2 refuted:** Hoerner gives 1.071, 8.4% below the measurement at nu 1.0e-6 (9.1% at 0.95e-6; only at 1.15e-6, i.e. about 13 C, is it within 7%, at 6.3%). **P3 held:** the 3.05 m/s point is 5.6% below the >5 m/s mean, the same shortfall as AFF-8 in 3t, so it belongs to the hull (and its tripwire), not the appendages.
- Reading: on its own wetted area SUBOFF's bare hull has k about 0.17, against Hoerner's 0.07: the formula under-predicts form drag here by about 0.10 in k, or 8% of total resistance. Part of the gap is the bow tripwire's own drag, which the measurement includes and the formula does not; the share was not separable from these data. The appendages (sail, four fins) add 5.8% wetted area (0.348 m2 from 3t's 6.3367 m2) but about 17% resistance, so on AFF-8's area (1+k) rises from 1.169 to 1.299. For the programme: Hoerner at a REMUS-class d/l 0.103 gives 1.057; scaled by SUBOFF's measured/Hoerner ratio it is 1.154, inside 4c's swept 1.1 to 1.3 and near its low end before appendages, so 4c's box was not optimistic. The R-speed exponent is 1.868 measured against 1.838 for the ITTC-57 line.
- Limits: one hull at Re 1.3e7 to 4e7, tripwires on, nu assumed (fresh water, temperature not in the digitised table); REMUS-scale Re (3e5 to 5e5) is still not tested; the "scaled Hoerner" REMUS figure carries a SUBOFF-to-REMUS transfer that is an assumption, not a measurement; the equations are read from two codes that may share an origin, and the reference area is second-hand, though the integral matches it to 4 digits.
- Next: a low-Re (below 1e6) body-of-revolution drag set (Myring-type or REMUS tow data) to test the Reynolds range the AUV cases actually use; a tripwire-drag estimate to split the 0.10 gap.

## 2026-11-10 · Milestone 3t: does the programme's submerged viscous law (ITTC-57 x (1+k) x S) match measured SUBOFF tow-tank resistance? (predictions in `experiments/run_suboff_check.py`'s docstring, fixed before the run)
- Why: 3s found no slender-catamaran curve, but Zenodo answered, and record 22692726 (CC-BY-4.0, F. Hermet, Simvia) carries the digitised DTMB tow-tank resistance of the DARPA SUBOFF (Liu & Huang 1998, Table 14), a submerged body with tripwires, so a measured test of the AUV cases' viscous law with no wave term. The record's CFD is not used.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_suboff_check.txt`): AFF-8 (S 6.3367 m2 from the record, L 4.356 m, nu 1.0e-6): implied (1+k_eff) 1.217, 1.294, 1.299, 1.307, 1.297, 1.296 over 3.05 to 9.15 m/s. **P1 borderline:** 1.30 sits at the bottom of the predicted 1.3 to 1.7 (the 3 m/s point is 1.22). **P2 refuted as stated:** spread 7.0%, but all of it is the slowest point; above 5 m/s the spread is 1.0%. **P3 held:** the bare hull carries 85.3 to 85.7% of AFF-8 resistance. Speed exponent of R: measured 1.896, ITTC-57 law 1.838 over the range.
- Reading: above 5 m/s the line plus one constant factor reproduces the measured speed scaling to about 1%; the 3 m/s shortfall (Re 1.3e7) is the one place it departs. Appendages (sail, fins) add about 17% to bare-hull resistance, so the bare-hull (1+k) is at least 1.11 times S8/S_bare, consistent with the programme's lowest AUV form factor (k = 0.1) and not with 0.3 unless the bare area is much smaller than 6.3 m2. I did not get the bare-hull wetted area (DTIC blocked), so this is a bound, not a fit.
- Limits: a 4.4 m, 0.5 m-diameter military hull at Re 1e7 to 4e7, against REMUS-class Re of about 3e5 to 5e5 (L 1.85 m at 1.5 m/s), where the line and laminar runs matter more; nu assumed (spread of 3% in k_eff over 0.95 to 1.15e-6); digitised table.
- Next: bare-hull wetted area and a low-Re (below 1e6) body-of-revolution drag set, to test the REMUS-scale Reynolds range directly.

## 2026-11-10 · Paper step: DRAFT.md section 3 item 1 now carries milestone 3r (a write-up step, no new experiment)
- Why: the draft covered 3o to 3q and 3s but not 3r's range check. Data hosts (zenodo, crossref) refused this session, so no sourcing step was possible; no prediction: nothing was run.
- Outcome: one sentence added with 3r's numbers, copied from the 3r entry above (best range unchanged to the km with the c15 branch; insensitivity, not validation).
- Next: unchanged (a slender displacement-catamaran tank curve below Fn 0.3 once a data host is reachable).

## 2026-11-10 · Milestone 3s: can the Hull Vane workbooks or the Hespérides tank set supply the slender-hull curve 3q and 3r asked for? (a sourcing step, no model run; no prediction registered)
- Why: 3q and 3r both end "a slender displacement-catamaran curve below Fn 0.3". Zenodo and Crossref answered this session (arXiv and MDPI did not: MDPI 403), so I opened the candidates instead of waiting for a host. Nothing was fitted, so nothing here is a result about hull resistance.
- Hull Vane record (Zenodo 18459868, CC-BY-4.0): the two summary workbooks (`HV Experimental Data.xlsx`, `Hull Vane Paper Tables_Numerical_Experimental.xlsx`) hold only speed, trim, resistance (kg and N) and CT for the same five speeds (1.7 to 4.1 m/s, Fn 0.33 to 0.79) already in `experiments/data/catamaran_bare_hull_tank.csv`, for the bare hull and eight vane variants. No body plan, beam, draft or displacement. So 3q's "geometry inferred from two numbers" cannot be replaced from this record, and its speed range stays above Fn 0.3. Closed.
- Hespérides record (Zenodo 12793313, CC-BY-4.0, supplement to Gutierrez Romero et al., J. Mar. Sci. Eng. 10(2), 246, 2022, doi 10.3390/jmse10020246, DOI resolves in Crossref, paper itself not opened): raw Catman ASCII, 600 Hz, from a research-ship model in the CEHINAV tank, with the ice-coverage cases. Only the C00 (no blocks) runs are clean water: two speeds (4.80 and 8.00 kn, Feb 2020; the file names do not say whether kn is ship or model scale). The geometry is a binary Maxsurf `.msd` (strings only), the Readme gives no scale, particulars or channel units. A ship-form hull (not slender; L/B near 5 to 6 from memory, unchecked) at two speeds could not separate form factor from wave term, so it would not have settled what 3q and 3r need even with a body plan. Closed for this programme unless the paper's particulars table is reachable.
- Not tried: the Maxsurf file's offsets (would need a parser for a proprietary format), the DTMB 5415 and Series 60 literature curves (no open numeric table found on Zenodo; searches returned unrelated records).
- Next: unchanged. A slender displacement hull with printed offsets and a CT table below Fn 0.3 (a thesis or an open-access JMSE/Ocean Engineering table) is still the missing input; searching Zenodo by the keywords used here (catamaran, Wigley, USV, towing tank, resistance) does not find one.

## 2026-11-10 · Milestone 3r: does 3p's c15 slender-hull branch move the 3 to 5 m range verdict? (run, then written; no prediction registered)
- Why: 3p coded the c15 rule; hulls with L^3/V above 512 now take a different value. The programme's 4 m catamaran demi-hulls (0.1 m3) and 5 m monohull sit above 512, so the question is whether any earlier Holtrop-based range or ranking moves. Data hosts are unreachable this session (zenodo, arxiv, crossref time out), so this is an offline model check, not a validation.
- Method (`experiments/run_holtrop_c15_range.py`, `results_holtrop_c15_range.txt`): Wigley-proportioned hulls, L/B 10, 0.2 m3 monohull or 2 x 0.1 m3 catamaran (demi-hull resistance doubled, no interference), 200 kg, 10 kWh, 30 W hotel, eta 0.55 x 0.85, best range over 0.3 to 3 m/s; old c15 (-1.69385 everywhere) against the 3p rule.
- Outcome: best range is identical to the km in all six cases (monohull 831 / 863 / 849 km at 3 / 4 / 5 m; catamaran 782 / 771 / 725 km). Catamaran/monohull range ratio at equal length 0.94 / 0.89 / 0.85, unchanged by c15. The best-range speed is 0.91 to 1.07 m/s (Fn about 0.13 to 0.17), where the c15 term sits inside exp(-0.1/Fn^2) and is negligible. Where it would act, on a 5 m demi-hull total resistance changes -0.4% (Fn 0.20), +1.8% (0.30), +3.7% (0.35), -4.6% (0.40): a few percent, in sign-changing directions, only above the programme's operating speed.
- Reading: the range and ranking results do not depend on the c15 branch. Because both the sign and size of the shift are tied to a rule read from a third implementation (3p), nothing here validates the branch; it only shows the programme is insensitive to it.
- Limits: Wigley proportions assumed for all hulls, no interference, no tank data for slender hulls, c15 unverified against the 1984 paper.
- Next: unchanged from 3q (a slender displacement-catamaran tank curve below Fn 0.3 once a data host is reachable).

## 2026-11-10 · Milestone 3q: does a Michell + Havelock wave term fix 3b's catamaran miss? (run, then written; no prediction registered)
- Why: 3b's placeholder wave term fell 35% to 43% short at Fn 0.33 to 0.58 on the Zenodo 18459868 catamaran tank curve. Michell with Havelock two-hull interference (`michell.py`, milestone 2) was the proposed replacement. Data hosts are refused this session, so the repository's own five-point CSV was the only curve available.
- Method (`experiments/run_catamaran_michell.py`, `results_catamaran_michell.txt`, `tests/test_catamaran_michell.py`): two Wigley demi-hulls, L = 2.812 m from Fn, total wetted area fixed to the 1.709 m2 implied by the tank CT; the unknown beam is swept (L/B 8, 10, 12; draft then solved from the area, 0.08 to 0.12 m) with demi-hull spacing s/L 0.15, 0.25, 0.40 and k in {0, 0.1}: 18 cases.
- Outcome: the model under-predicts the tank CT in every case at every speed: median -50% (Fn 0.33), -36%, -41%, -31%, -29% (Fn 0.79) at k = 0; -46% to -24% at k = 0.1. Best single case is -22%. No case is within 20% at any speed. Spacing and L/B move it by a few points only.
- Reading: Michell, even with interference, does not rescue the miss. The tank hull is semi-planing (trim 0 to -2.6 deg, 3b), so most of the residual is dynamic lift, trim and sinkage, which a thin-ship Wigley model lacks; the geometry here is also inferred, not the real body plan. Consequence for the programme's results is unchanged: they sit at Fn about 0.16, well below this data. What it rules out is using the Michell model, or the displacement model generally, for Fn above about 0.3 on semi-planing catamarans.
- Limits: five points, one hull, geometry inferred from two numbers, Wigley shape is not this hull's shape, bare hull with trim and sinkage the model lacks, water type assumed.
- Next: a slender displacement-catamaran curve below Fn 0.3 (also the c15 branch of 3p); the Hull Vane paper's body plan if reachable.

## 2026-11-09 · Milestone 3p: the c15 three-way rule is coded, and a typo in a public Holtrop implementation is ruled out (a cross-read of a third implementation, no 1984 paper opened)
- Why: 3k-3o asked for a second published 1984 worked example; none is reachable (the Archibald repo carries only the 1982 p. 170 case). Its 1984-term functions were read instead (read only, not executed: running third-party code was blocked in this session) against `holtrop.py`.
- Differences found, term by term: (1) **c15**: Archibald codes the three-way rule (-1.69385 below L^3/V 512; -1.69385 + (L/V^(1/3) - 8)/2.36 up to 1726.91; 0 beyond); `marine_hull` used -1.69385 for every hull, a gap its own comment named. Now coded (`c15_coefficient`). (2) **m1**: Archibald has 0.014047 where `marine_hull` has 0.0140407. Recomputing the 1982 example by hand gives m1 = -2.1274 only with 0.0140407 (0.28783 - 0.28615 - 0.74820 - 1.3809), the printed value, so the other is a typo that their test tolerance (2e-3, plus a T+0.1 shift) hid; ours stays. (3) CA: Archibald uses 0.00675 (L+100)^(-1/3) - 0.00064, a different correlation allowance from ours (the 1982 form, which reproduced the printed CA in 3k); not changed, flagged.
- Outcome (`experiments/run_holtrop_c15.py`, `results_holtrop_c15.txt`, 2 new tests): for a swept hull (L 60 m, Fn 0.30, Cp 0.60) RW is unchanged up to L/V^(1/3) 8 and rises 2% (8.5), 4% (9), 8% (10), 13% (11), 17% (12), where c15 reaches 0. Every earlier result (Wigley, Delft yachts, 1982 example) has L^3/V below 512 and is byte-identical on rerun, so no earlier conclusion moves.
- Limits: the rule is read from a third implementation's docstring, which cites the 1984 paper; the paper was not opened. The sweep is illustrative geometry, not a measured vessel, and no tank data for a slender hull checks the new branch yet.
- Next: a slender-hull tank curve (catamaran demi-hull, L/V^(1/3) above 8) to test the new branch; settle which CA the 1984 paper prints.

## 2026-11-09 · Milestone 3o: a second Wigley tank point at 4 m (Ju 1983) agrees with 3n's near-zero form factor (a sourcing step plus one script; no prediction registered)
- Why: 3n's reading (Wigley k about 0 at a 1 m model) rested on one lab's curve (Kajitani via Ahmed et al.). Zenodo answered this session, and Wang et al. (OPEC 2017, Zenodo 1169033, CC BY-NC-ND, read only) quote an independent tank point: Ju (1983), Wigley L 4.0 m, B 0.4, T 0.25 (L/B 10, L/T 16, the same hull at 4x), Fn 0.267, U 1.67 m/s, Aw = 0.661 L (2D+B) = 2.3796 m2, CT = 4.16e-3. Ju (1983) was not opened, so the value is second-hand.
- Outcome (`experiments/run_wigley_ju.py`, `results_wigley_ju.txt`, `tests/test_wigley_ju.py`): the quoted speed equals Fn sqrt(gL) (1.672 m/s) and the quoted area is within 0.04% of the exact Wigley surface (2.3807 m2), which also confirms 0.1488 m2 as the area basis for the 1 m hull (x16 = 2.381). Michell + ITTC 1957 gives Cw = 0.976e-3 and implied **k = -0.06 (10 C) to +0.01 (25 C)**, the paper not stating the water temperature; -0.04 at 15 C. Same Fn on the 1 m hull: CT ratio 1 m/4 m is 1.40 against 1.52 for the ITTC CF ratio.
- Reading: two labs and two model sizes put the Wigley hull's form factor above ITTC plus Michell at about 0 (-0.06 to +0.01 here, -0.04 at 1 m). That supports 3n's reading that the 2 m and 4 m rows of 3m (k +0.18, +0.44) were artefacts of the guessed length; a form factor of 0.1 to 0.3 for this very slender hull is not supported. It is one Fn, where Michell's wave term is small (Cw about 17% of CT), so it constrains CF + k more than the wave model.
- Limits: second-hand CT, one speed, temperature unknown, Michell is thin-ship theory, and the other hulls in the survey rungs are fatter than Wigley, so their k need not be near 0.
- Next: Ju's full curve (a source with several Fn) or the Kajitani summary; a second worked example for the 1984 Holtrop terms.

## 2026-11-09 · Milestone 3n: the primary Table 5 (Ahmed et al. 2015) opened; it settles the CFD model length and shows the CT reference area (a sourcing step plus one script)
- Why: 3m read the Kajitani six-point Wigley curve second-hand and could not tell the model length, so implied form factor ranged -0.13 to +0.58. Paper section 3 item 1 asked for the primary table. jtse.utm.my answered this session (HTTP 200); the journal archive lists Vol. 2 No. 1 (2015), and the open-access PDF (article 35, 6 pages) downloaded and was read with `pdftotext`. Ahmed, Yaakob, Rashid and Elbatran, "Determining Ship Resistance Using Computational Fluid Dynamics (CFD)", *J. Transport System Engineering* 2(1), 20-25, 2015 (found by browsing the journal's issue; Crossref did not index it). Its tank values are cited there to Kajitani et al. (1983) and are not re-measured by the authors.
- What the primary table shows (`experiments/run_wigley_ahmed_primary.py`, `results_wigley_ahmed_primary.txt`, `tests/test_ahmed_table5.py`; values typed from the PDF): (a) the six CT(exp) values equal the 3m transcription exactly. (b) Table 1 gives the model as Lpp 1.00 m, and the velocity column equals Fn sqrt(g x 1 m) to 0.0002 m/s: the paper's CFD is at **L = 1 m**. It does not say the tank model was 1 m; the tank CT is quoted without a length. (c) Force and CT in the CFX columns imply a constant reference area of 0.0770 m2, i.e. 0.1539 m2 for the full hull (rho = 1000): neither the 0.135 m2 in Table 1 nor the analytic 0.1488 m2. (d) At L = 1 m, Michell + ITTC 1957 implies k = -0.04 (range -0.13 to +0.05) against the tank CT and -0.08 (-0.22 to +0.02) against the paper's own CFX CT; CFX is on average 2.4% below the tank CT (max 5.8%).
- Reading: if the CFD and tank hulls are both about 1 m, the Wigley hull needs essentially no form factor above ITTC plus Michell, and the 2 m and 4 m rows of 3m (k +0.18, +0.44) are unlikely. That is a reading, not a measurement: the Kajitani model length is not in this paper. It agrees with the 0.06 median implied k on keeled yachts at Fn 0.125 (3i).
- No prediction was recorded before this script ran; it is a source check.
- Limits: the CT(exp) column is still second-hand (Ahmed et al. quote Kajitani; the 1983 summary was not opened); CFX figures are a third-party simulation; one hull; six points; the area ambiguity (0.1488 / 0.1539 / 0.135 m2) is unresolved, and a 0.135 m2 basis would raise every CT-based coefficient by 10% to 14%.
- Next: the Kajitani 1983 summary or ITTC Wigley data for model length and area basis; a second worked example for the 1984 Holtrop terms.

## 2026-11-09 · Milestone 3m: is `michell.py` right, and what form factor does a six-point Wigley CT curve imply? (an independent-integral check and a first curve)
- Why: paper section 3 item 1 asks for a small-hull resistance *curve*, not one point. Data hosts are refused this session (zenodo, arXiv, Crossref, ideas.repec all returned proxy 403); only github.com clones work. Archibald's MIT validation script (`examples/01 - Validation/michell_wigley.py`, read from a clone) carries the six Kajitani et al. (1983) (Fn, CT) points of Ahmed, Yaakob, Rashid and Elbatran, *J. Transport System Eng.* 2(1), 2015, Table 5, for the L/B = 10, L/T = 16 Wigley hull. That is a second-hand transcription, not the table.
- Plan (`experiments/run_wigley_kajitani.py`): (a) compare `wave_resistance_wigley` with a dense-grid trapezoid evaluation of the original integral (lambda = 1 + w^2 form, written here; the idea is Archibald's, no code copied) at ten Fn from 0.15 to 1.0; (b) implied form factor k = (CT - Cw)/CF - 1 at the six Fn, with CF at model length 1, 2 and 4 m (the source does not give the model size).
- **Prediction (written after seeing the CSV points, before the first run):** (a) agreement within 0.5% at every Fn (prior 85%). (b) implied k positive at every point for L >= 2 m and between 0 and 0.5 (prior 50%): Michell is known to overshoot humps, so a negative k at some Fn at L = 1 m would not surprise me.
- **Outcome (2 s, deterministic; `experiments/results_wigley_kajitani.txt`; `tests/test_michell_fine_integral.py`):**
  - (a) held: largest difference 0.01% over Fn 0.15 to 1.0. Two independent evaluations of the same integral agree; this tests the numerics, not the physics. (The earlier 800-step theta convergence check in milestone 2 is now backed by a second method.)
  - (b) held at L = 2 m and 4 m, not at 1 m: implied k +0.07 to +0.30 (mean +0.18) at 2 m, +0.30 to +0.58 (mean +0.44) at 4 m, and -0.13 to +0.05 (mean -0.04) at 1 m. The answer moves by 0.5 across the unknown model size, so a single "implied k" is not a finding; it is a bracket. Archibald reports the same 1 m case as a form factor of 0.87 to 1.06 (k -0.13 to +0.06), which matches.
  - The implied k is not steady in Fn at any size (e.g. 4 m: 0.30 at Fn 0.408, 0.58 at 0.354), as expected where thin-ship theory exaggerates humps and hollows. A realistic Wigley k is about 0.1 to 0.3 (a sweep range already in `run_yacht_formfactor.py`, not a measurement here), which is consistent with a model of roughly 2 m.
- Limits: the (Fn, CT) points are second-hand; the model length, tank temperature and the area CT is based on are unknown (I used the exact wetted area 0.1488 m2; Ahmed et al. list 0.135 for the 1 m hull, 9% lower, which would raise every CT-based coefficient); six points; Michell is thin-ship theory and the curve is the only validation of the physics here. Does not close paper section 3 item 1 (that wants the primary table and a stated error), but it replaces "one point" with "six points and a bracket".
- Next: obtain the primary table (owner supplies it, or a run with a reachable host); a second worked example for the 1984 Holtrop terms.

## 2026-11-08 · Milestone 3k: does marine_hull's Holtrop-Mennen reproduce the 1982 paper's worked example? (a check against the paper's printed numbers)
- Why: since milestone 3c the coefficients were typed from memory and agreed with public implementations, never with the paper. The p. 170 worked example (205 m ship, 25 kt, every intermediate printed) is transcribed in the MIT-licensed Archibald validation script (julesricheux/Archibald, `examples/01 - Validation/holtrop_1982_example.py`, read through a git clone this session). That is a second-hand copy of the paper, not the paper. Plan (`experiments/run_holtrop_1982.py`): feed the example's inputs, with its published bulb and transom factors c2, c5 and wetted area, and compare 1+k1, RW, CA, RA with the printed values.
- **Prediction (before running):** 1+k1, RW and RA each within 3% of print (prior 60%). Refuted if any is off by more.
- **Outcome: refuted, and it found a bug.** First run: 1+k1 +2.5% (1.185 against 1.156), RW **-15.4%** (471 against 557 kN). Causes: (1) **lambda's branch was inverted** in `holtrop.py`: it used 0.03 L/B when L/B >= 12 and 0.36 below, the paper's 0.6513 needs 0.03 L/B when L/B < 12 (here L/B = 6.4). (2) The form factor coded is the 1984 one (the same formula Archibald uses, 1.1851), whereas the 1982 paper prints c13 {0.93 + c12 (B/LR)^0.92497 (0.95-Cp)^-0.521448 (1-Cp+0.0225 lcb)^0.6906} = 1.156; the module's docstring said 1982.
- After the fix and with `k_form="1982"`: Fn +0.01%, 1+k1 +0.04%, RW -0.02% (556.98 against 557.11 kN, low-speed form m2), CA +0.14%, RA -0.63%, and RF(1+k1)+RW+RA+RB within **-0.06%** of the printed sum. With the m4 low-speed form RW is -0.55%: the example favours m2 (Cp^2) over m4, which settles the 3d/3e/3f disagreement in favour of the default, though a 0.5% gap is thin evidence by itself. The default `k_form` stays "1984" so earlier results stay comparable.
- Effect on earlier milestones (re-run, results files regenerated): Wigley tank point CT +19% to +24% on the regression area (was +17% to +22%), +33% to +39% on the true area (was +31% to +37%); Delft yachts at Fn 0.125 to 0.2, the survey regime, are unchanged (median Rr over-prediction 1.32 to 2.16 times), because lambda only sets the wave phase; at Fn 0.2 to 0.3 the share within 20% moved to 42% to 82% (was 44% to 79%). The 3j catamaran verdict (H2) uses Michell and ITTC, not this module, and does not move. The H3 reading (partly supported at low Fn) stands.
- Limits: the printed numbers come from Archibald's transcription, not from the paper; the example is one large, full-form ship (L/B 6.4, outside the small-hull regime); bulb and transom factors c2, c5 were passed in, not computed; RB, RTR, RAPP and the wake are not part of this module; the CA formula differs from Archibald's (ours matches the print to 0.14%).
- Next: item 1 of paper section 3 (a tank curve); a second published worked example (the 1984 paper's, if a host serves it) for the revised terms.

## 2026-11-07 · Milestone 6zf: does fitting the ridge to the smoothed wind, as a correction to the trailing mean, clear T = 0? (closes 6ze's "Next")
- Why: 6ze's ridge forecast (fitted to the point wind, correction to the last observation) lost 0.8 points to 6z's trailing-mean persistence. `experiments/run_sail_ridge_smooth.py` refits on January to the 18-minute-smoothed future wind, as a correction to the 60-minute trailing mean, and shrinks the correction by alpha = 0.5 or 1.0 (all else as 6ze: 11 stations, 88 July windows, tau 240 s).
- Prediction (written before the run, in this session): the alpha = 1 forecast stays below T = 0 (prior 65%); alpha = 0.5 lies within +-1 point of T = 0 (prior 60%).
### Outcome (`experiments/results_sail_ridge_smooth.txt`; about 4 minutes on 4 cores)
- **Both held.** alpha = 1 minus T = 0 is -1.2 points (bootstrap 95% of median -2.2..-0.4); alpha = 0.5 minus T = 0 is -0.3 points (-1.0..+0.3), i.e. indistinguishable from the trailing mean. Tacks per day: 46 (alpha 1), 44 (alpha 0.5), 42 (T = 0). The ceiling is +10.9 points as in 6ze (replication).
- Reading: smoothing the target did not rescue the fitted forecast; shrinking it to the trailing mean only converges on T = 0. Pensacola still loses 3.9 to 4.5 points. Fitted coastal forecasts with 4 to 8% RMSE skill do not beat a trailing mean for tack planning, so this branch closes; paper section 3 can state that.
- Limits: 8 windows per single-station readout; linear ridge; one tau; coastal CO-OPS stations only; the mark bearing is the window's mean direction (hindsight). Not run here: homepage rebuild, citation checks (no new citations).
- Next: the remaining input is offshore upwind sensing or radar nowcasts, which need data not in `experiments/data`; otherwise return to paper section 3.

## 2026-11-07 · Milestone 6ze: does a ridge wind forecast with 6 to 8% RMSE skill move the sail score at tau 240 s? (closes 6zd's "Next"; prediction written before the run)
- Why: 6zc/6zd found 4 to 8% vector-RMSE skill over persistence at some coastal stations, and no neighbour adds more. 5d/6z priced forecasts by an assumed decay a(L) = exp(-L/T); here a forecast fitted on real data goes through `tack.sim_replan_dead` (tau 240 s, hourly replan, 24 h windows, every 4th, polar and corridor as 6z). `experiments/run_sail_ridge_forecast.py` fits, per station, a ridge correction to persistence on 6 lags of (u, v) at leads 6, 12, 18, 30, 60, 120 min on January, interpolates linearly in (u, v) between leads and holds the 120 min value beyond; scored on the July windows only (January is in-sample and not reported as a result). Compared in the same windows against: hold-last-observation, `lead_forecast` T = 0 (trailing 60-min mean persistence, 6z's baseline), T = inf (perfect 15-min-smoothed truth, the ceiling) and the header-follower.
- **Prediction:** (1) ridge minus T = 0 median share difference is under +1.0 point (prior 70%). (2) ridge minus hold-last-observation lies within +-1.5 points (prior 60%). (3) the ceiling (T = inf minus T = 0) is about +8.7 points as in 6z (replication, prior 90%). Refuted: (1) a difference of +1.0 or more with the bootstrap interval excluding 0; (2) outside +-1.5; (3) outside 7 to 10.
- Limits known in advance: coastal CO-OPS stations only; linear forecast; the mark bearing is the window's circular-mean direction (a hindsight quantity, as in all earlier sail milestones); one polar, one corridor width; one tau.

### Outcome of 6ze (`experiments/results_sail_ridge_forecast.txt`; about 1 minute on 4 cores; 11 stations, 88 July windows, tau 240 s)
- **Prediction: (1) held, (2) held, (3) refuted.** (1) Ridge minus T = 0 is **-0.8 points** (median; bootstrap 95% -2.0..-0.3), so under +1.0 and in fact negative: the fitted forecast sails worse than 6z's trailing-mean persistence (0.742 against 0.772 of the bound; header-follower 0.775). (2) Ridge minus hold-last-observation is -1.3 points (-2.1..-0.7), inside +-1.5 but excluding 0. (3) The ceiling (T = inf minus T = 0) is +10.9 points (7.4..13.3), above the 7 to 10 band: 6z's +8.7 used a different station set and window count (96 windows), so this is a replication on a different 88-window set and the ceiling is between 9 and 11 points rather than a fixed 8.7.
- Reading: a 6 to 8% RMSE skill advantage over persistence does not become sail score. The ridge forecast is an unsmoothed point forecast, so it raises tacks per day (50 against 42) and the dead-time DP chases noise; 6z's persistence baseline is a 60-minute trailing mean of 15-minute-smoothed wind, which is steadier than the last observation (hold is 1.9 points under it). At Pensacola, where 6zd found the highest skill rising with lead, the ridge forecast loses 4.2 points to T = 0 (8 windows). At San Francisco it loses 0.8 (8 windows). Gap to the ceiling is still 9 to 11 points that no coastal-station predictor tried here closes.
- Limits: 8 windows per single-station readout, so the station rows are indicative; the forecast is scored on rates after the polar, with the hindsight mean-direction bearing; linear ridge, fitted to the point wind rather than to the smoothed wind the DP consumes; one tau.
- Next: fit the ridge to the 15-minute-smoothed target (and shrink toward the trailing mean as a function of lead) and see whether it then clears T = 0; if not, this branch closes and paper section 3 states that fitted coastal forecasts do not beat a trailing mean for tack planning.

## 2026-11-07 · Milestone 6zd: does a neighbouring coastal station 15 to 70 km away add the wind-forecast skill a single point lacks? (closes 6zc's "Next"; prediction written before the run)
- Why: 6zc found a buoy's own history adds under 4% over persistence and said a spatial predictor needs an upwind neighbour. The CO-OPS 6-minute series in `experiments/data` hold two usable pairs: San Francisco and Alameda (about 15 km, January and July) and Pensacola and Dauphin Island (about 70 km, July only). `experiments/run_wind_pair_skill.py` fits a ridge correction to persistence on 6 lags of (u, v) at the target alone, then with 6 lags at the neighbour added, at leads 6, 12, 18, 30, 60, 120 min. 15 km pair: fit January, test July, both directions. 70 km pair: fit the first half of July, test the second half (with a 4 h gap), both directions.
- **Prediction:** (1) target-only skill replicates 6zc: under +0.05 at 30 min (prior 75%). (2) For the 15 km pair the neighbour adds +0.05 or more at 30 min (prior 40%, the pair is inside one bay with a shared sea breeze, but 15 km at 5 m/s is a 50 min advection). (3) For the 70 km pair it adds under +0.05 at 30 min (prior 70%). Refuted: (1) target-only at 30 min of +0.05 or more; (2) added skill under +0.05 for both 15 km directions; (3) added skill of +0.05 or more at 70 km in either direction.
- Limits known in advance: two pairs only, both inside a bay or sound; no radar or upwind sensor offshore; one fit month for the 15 km pair, half a month for the 70 km pair (seasonal and within-month drift); linear predictor.

### Outcome of 6zd (`experiments/results_wind_pair_skill.txt`; under 10 s, deterministic; about 7,400 July test rows per lead for the 15 km pair, 3,700 for the 70 km pair)
- **Prediction: (1) refuted, (2) refuted, (3) held.** (1) Target-only skill at 30 min is +0.076 at San Francisco and +0.055 at Pensacola, over the +0.05 bar (Alameda +0.019 and Dauphin Island +0.045 are under it), so 6zc's "nothing beyond persistence" does not hold everywhere: 6 to 8% skill exists at some coastal stations, and at Pensacola and Dauphin Island it rises with lead (+0.11 at 120 min). (2) The neighbour adds +0.006 (San Francisco) and +0.009 (Alameda) at 30 min, under the +0.05 bar in both directions. (3) At 70 km it adds +0.009 and +0.008 at 30 min, under the bar in both directions.
