- Consistency with 5e: margin 0 and the T=0, 30, 60, 120 rows at margin 0 agree exactly with 5e's (same windows, same code). Together the two grids (0.02 to 2) show monotone loss with margin, so a fixed proportional margin is closed off.

## 2026-11-04 · Milestone 5e: does a switch margin rescue the low-skill rows of 5d? (pre-registration)
- Why: 5d's "Next". At forecast skill T = 0 and 30 min the hourly-replanned policy tacks 150 to 250 times a day and loses to (T=0) or barely beats the header-follower. `tack.sim_replan` gains a `margin` argument (as `sim_policy` has): tack only if switching beats staying by margin x the best model progress of one 60 s step (a tack costs tau/dt = 0.5 step). `experiments/run_sail_margin.py` -> `results_sail_margin.txt`: T = 0, 30, 60, 120 min x margin 0, 0.5, 1, 2; every 8th window (half of 5d's), same data, polar, corridor, tau as 5c/5d.
- **Predictions (before running):** M1 margin 0 reproduces 5d's tacks/day within 10% (implementation check; the window subset differs, so medians may shift a little). M2 at T = 0 a margin of 1 raises the result above the header-follower (persistence plus a margin beats following headers), and tacks/day fall by at least half. M3 at T = 120 no margin changes the result by more than 1 point (a skilled forecast needs no damping). M4 at T = 30 the best margin recovers at least 5 points of share of the 5c gain over margin 0. Prior: M2 45%, M3 65%, M4 55%.

- Outcome (`results_sail_margin.txt`, 48 windows per month, deterministic, run about 15 minutes; medians as share of the bound, difference from the header-follower in points):
  - **A switch margin does not help; it hurts at every forecast skill.** Jan / Jul, T = 120: margin 0 +3.4 / +3.3, 0.5 -0.3 / -0.1, 1 -1.9 / -2.0, 2 -2.5 / -2.9. T = 60: +2.5 / +2.9, -0.4 / -0.6, -2.2 / -2.4, -2.6 / -2.6. T = 30: +1.5 / +2.3, -0.7 / -1.3, -2.0 / -2.5, -2.5 / -2.7. T = 0: -1.0 / -2.2, -1.7 / -2.7, -2.6 / -3.9, -2.6 / -3.8.
  - M1 held in July (T=0 248 against 5d's 246 tacks/day) and failed in January (166 against 148, 12% over; T=30 178 against 157), which I read as the every-8th-window subset, not an error. M2 **refuted**: at T = 0 margin 1 is 1.6 to 1.7 points below margin 0, not above the header-follower, and tacks fall 34% (Jan) and 44% (Jul), not half. M3 **refuted**: at T = 120 margin 0.5 costs 3.7 / 3.4 points. M4 **refuted**: at T = 30 the best margin is 0, since every positive margin lowers the result.
  - Reading: the extra tacks at low skill are not chatter to be damped. The value table already prices a tack (tau = 30 s, 0.5 step) and its decision to tack is on average right; a margin on the model's best step progress (about 60 s of progress, far above the real tack cost) blocks tacks that were worth making. Tacks per day fall only to ~100 to 160, still not the 5c level. If a low-skill forecast is to be rescued it needs a smarter rule (a margin scaled to the tack cost, hysteresis in time, or a forecast-uncertainty-aware value), not a larger fixed margin. A negative result that closes the branch "a fixed margin repairs low-skill replanning".
- Limits: margin is in units of the model's best one-step progress (a margin of 0.5 is already about the whole tack cost); only 0, 0.5, 1, 2 tried, none between 0 and 0.5; 48 windows per month (every 8th), bootstrap treats windows as independent; the forecast error model is 5d's synthetic blend, not an archived forecast; no archived-forecast host was tried this session.
- Next: margins of 0.05 to 0.25 (finer around zero); a minimum dwell time instead of a margin; archived HRRR or GFS winds at a station if a host answers.

## 2026-11-04 · Milestone 5d: how much of 5c's +5 point gain survives a forecast whose error grows with lead time? (pre-registration)
- Why: 5c's "Next". `tack.sim_replan` replans hourly with `tack.lead_forecast`: model rate at lead L = a(L) x truth smoothed to 15 min + (1-a(L)) x trailing-hour mean (persistence), a(L)=exp(-L/T), T = 0 (persistence), 30, 60, 120, 240 min, inf (5c's knowledge). Script `experiments/run_sail_lead.py` -> `results_sail_lead.txt`; every 4th window, same data, polar, corridor and tau as 5c. This is a synthetic error model, not an archived NWP forecast (no such host tried yet).
- Outcome (`results_sail_lead.txt`, deterministic, 96 windows per month, 5 tests in `test_tack_policy.py` pass; run takes about 12 minutes):
  - L1 held: T=inf with hourly replanning equals the one-shot policy (it is the same model each hour; 0.924 Jan, 0.929 Jul). L4 held: monotone in T in both months.
  - L2 held: persistence (T=0) is below the header-follower (-0.2 Jan, -2.6 Jul points) and tacks 148 to 246 times a day, so a forecast with no skill beyond now is worse than following headers.
  - L3 held, narrowly: at T=120 min the policy keeps 51% (Jan) and 58% (Jul) of 5c's gain (+3.0 and +3.2 points over the header-follower, bootstrap 95% 2.4 to 3.9 and 3.0 to 4.1). T=30 keeps 20% and 16%; T=60 38% and 45%; T=240 68% and 62%.
  - Reading: the value is not a cliff. Half of the +5 points needs forecast skill (error e-folding time) of about 2 hours, roughly 20% needs 30 minutes, and anything with less skill than 30 minutes loses to the header-follower. A 15-minute-scale forecast at 1 to 3 hour lead is what a boat would need, which is the range where short-range NWP and local persistence are weak; that is a claim about what to test next, not a result.
- Limits: the error model is exponential blending of truth with persistence, not an archived forecast (it has no bias, no timing error, no shear between stations); the same blend applies to all wind scales; replan interval fixed at 60 minutes and no tack margin in the policy (the chatter at low T would shrink with one, not tried); everything from 5a and 5c stands.
- Next: archived HRRR or GFS winds at a station (if a host answers through the proxy) scored on the same windows; a switch margin for the low-skill rows; spatial wind fields.

- Predictions (written before the run): L1 T=inf with hourly replanning keeps at least 90% of the 5c one-shot gain (implementation check). L2 persistence (T=0) keeps under 25% of the gain. L3 T=120 keeps under 60%. L4 gain is monotone in T.

## 2026-11-04 · Milestone 5c: the 12-point gap was mostly an edge-rule artefact, and the rest is slow-wind information (corrects 5a and 5b)
- Why: 5b's "Next" (is the planner or the information the limit?). Writing a full-day value-table policy (`tack.dp_values`, `tack.sim_policy`: no finite horizon, so no terminal-value problem) exposed a mismatch: 5a's clairvoyant DP clamps y at the corridor edge and keeps full progress with no tack, while the header-follower and edge-tacking controllers must tack there. `dp_values(edge_tack=True)` applies the same rule. Script `experiments/run_sail_policy.py` -> `results_sail_policy.txt` (every 2nd window, 180 per month, tau 30 s, same data, polar, corridor and header margin as 5a).
- Predictions: **not pre-registered in the strict sense**: I wrote them after a one-station July smoke test (15 windows) showed the edge-forced DP at 0.935 and the 15-minute-smoothed policy at 0.934, so they were partly known. E1: the edge rule lowers the clairvoyant by at least 2 points. E2: the policy on exact wind (w = 1) reaches at least 98% of the edge-forced DP (an implementation check). E3: the policy on 15-minute-smoothed wind closes at least half of the remaining header-to-DP gap. E4: on 120-minute-smoothed wind it closes under 20%.
- Outcome (deterministic; tests `tests/test_tack_policy.py`, 4 pass, whole suite 81 pass):
  - E1 held by a wide margin: the edge-forced clairvoyant is 92.6% (Jan) and 92.7% (Jul) against 99.7% for 5a's. **Of the 11.6 to 12.7 points 5a and 5b called headroom, 7.1 and 6.9 were the edge rule**; the header-follower's gap to the honest clairvoyant is 4.5 (Jan) and 5.8 (Jul) points.
  - E2 held: the w = 1 policy reaches 100% of the edge-forced DP (0.926 and 0.928).
  - E3 held: on 15-minute-smoothed wind the policy closes 95% (Jan) and 93% (Jul) of that gap, +4.8 and +5.2 points over the header-follower (bootstrap 95% over windows, treated as independent: 4.3 to 5.5 and 4.7 to 6.1). 5-minute smoothing changes nothing; 30 minutes keeps 83% and 74%; 60 minutes 39% and 37%; 120 minutes -5% and 11% (E4 held).
  - So **5b's reading is wrong in its main claim**: the minute-scale gusts are not what the headroom was; its planners (finite horizon, zero terminal value, first-decision replanning on block means) were poor, as its own limit 1 said. A full-day value table built on wind smoothed to 15 minutes, executed on the true wind, beats the header-follower by about 5 points at the same number of tacks per day (88 to 106 against 89 to 100).
- What it does not show: the controller knows the *whole day's* 15-minute-scale wind exactly. A real forecast has error that grows with lead time, and the 60- and 120-minute rows (which lose 60 to 100% of the gain) show how fast the value depends on the scale of knowledge, not on its lead. The next question is value against forecast lead and error, with archived HRRR or GFS winds if a host answers through the proxy. Everything else in 5a stands (uniform wind, time-penalty tacks, 6-minute data from US harbours, a first-order polar). The y grid of 25 m lets a policy exceed the table value by under 0.1% on a synthetic test.
- Corrections to earlier text: 5a's "the clairvoyant reaches 99.7%" and "knowing the next shift is what limits the boat" and 5b's "no forecast recovers any of the 12 points" are superseded by this entry (README and paper draft updated).
- Next: a forecast with lead-dependent error (persistence of the smoothed wind blended with truth) fed to the full-day policy; archived NWP winds; spatial fields.

## 2026-11-04 · Milestone 5b: does a wind forecast close the 12-point gap between the header-follower and the clairvoyant? (negative result)
- Why: 5a's "Next". Receding-horizon controller `tack.sim_mpc` (value iteration over (tack, y) on `step`-minute blocks of a forecast, executes the first decision, replans every `step` minutes, optional switch margin against chattering). Scripts: `experiments/run_sail_forecast.py` (persistence, 30-min-smoothed true future, and raw true future, horizons 30 to 120 min, 5-min blocks), `run_sail_forecast_res.py` (perfect 1-min forecast for 30 min, block 1/3/10/30 min, every 3rd window), `run_sail_forecast_fullday.py` (perfect forecast for the whole remaining day, blocks 5/15 min, every 6th window). Same polar, corridor, tau 30 s and windows as 5a. Margin chosen on January, July is the holdout.
- Predictions (written before the results were read, after a one-window smoke test showed the margin-free planner tacking 303 times a day, which is why the margin exists): F1 persistence does not beat the header-follower by 1 point or more; F2 a perfect 30-min-smoothed forecast with a 60-min horizon closes at least 40% of the gap (about 4.8 points); F3 horizon beyond 60 min adds under 2 points.
- Outcome (`results_sail_forecast.txt`, `_res.txt`, `_fullday.txt`; deterministic; tests `tests/test_tack_mpc.py`, 3 pass):
  - F1 held: persistence is 1.0 point below the header-follower in both months (July 0.856 against 0.876; bootstrap -1.3 to -0.8).
  - F2 refuted: the smoothed perfect forecast is also 1.0 point below it (July 0.855). The raw perfect 1-min forecast over 60 or 120 min, planned on 5-min blocks, ties it (July 0.868 against 0.876, -0.2 to +0.2 on the difference after rounding in the file). No variant closes any of the gap.
  - F3 held trivially: the result is identical to three decimals at 30, 60 and 120 min horizons, so the horizon is not what matters here.
  - Resolution check (`_res`): planning on finer blocks makes it worse, not better (July, perfect 30-min forecast: 1-min blocks 0.815, 3-min 0.859, 10-min 0.880, header 0.887). A perfect forecast of the whole remaining day on 5-min blocks gets 0.837 and on 15-min blocks 0.867 (July, header 0.874), clairvoyant 0.996.
- Reading: with this planner and these data, **no forecast of the wind's slow shifts, however good and however long, recovers any of the 12 points**. The clairvoyant's 99.7% comes from information at the scale of single 6-minute samples (gusts and noise, interpolated to 1 minute), which no forecast service supplies, and a boat cannot act on usefully (a 30 s tack lost against a few-minute gust). The earlier limit 1 of 5a is therefore the main story: the 12-point "headroom" is mostly not attainable. The header-follower's 87 to 89% is close to what a realistic controller can have on this model.
- Limits and honest caveats: (1) the finer-block planners doing worse than coarse ones with a perfect forecast shows the planner is not optimal (finite horizon with zero terminal value, myopic first-decision replanning, forecast block means that hide the within-block rates the true boat sees); a better planner (terminal value from a day-scale DP, rolling-horizon with commit) could close some gap, so this is a negative result for this controller family, not a proof that no forecast helps; (2) the "forecasts" are the observed series, not archived forecasts: no NWP product, and no forecast error model beyond persistence; (3) everything else from 5a (uniform wind, uniform polar, time-penalty tacks, 6-minute data) stands; (4) the margin was chosen on January per variant and is 0.3 for all but the 30-minute and full-day blocks.
- Next: a terminal-value planner (use the day-scale DP value as the horizon's terminal reward) to see whether the planner, not the information, is the limit; an archived-forecast comparison (HRRR or GFS) if a host answers through the proxy; spatial wind fields.

## 2026-11-04 · Milestone 5a: wing-sail drone polar, and what tack control loses on real wind series (pre-registration)
- Why: PROJECTS.md track 23, milestone 5 (`marine-sail`), as a step inside this programme (the marine-hull draw says "next free: marine-sail"; no `sail`/`polar` project exists on `origin/main`). Question: how much upwind progress does a small wing-sail drone give up to tacking, and does following wind shifts recover it?
- Model (`src/marine_hull/sail.py`, stdlib): finite-wing lift with a stall roll-off and flat-plate limit, sail trimmed for maximum net force, force balance against a Wigley hull (ITTC 1957 friction x form factor + the Michell wave resistance already in this package) and a keel's induced drag at leeway. Speed capped at Froude 0.5 (displacement-hull validity). Not modelled: heel, reefing, sea-state drag. Every drone parameter is swept (no vendor polar is used).
- Data: NOAA CO-OPS observed 6-minute wind, Jan and Jul 2024, 15 coastal stations (`experiments/fetch_coops_wind.py`, files kept under `experiments/data/`). Wind is taken as spatially uniform over the 20-odd km a 24 h leg covers, so this is a time-variation (shift) study, not a weather-routing study; isochrone routing on a spatial field is not done here.
- Predictions (written before any run):
  - S1: the best upwind true wind angle at 5 m/s lies between 35 and 55 degrees over the whole parameter sweep, and an analytic check holds: with hull drag near zero the apparent wind angle at equilibrium equals atan(D/L) at the sail's best L/D.
  - S2: on 24 h windows with a +-1.5 km corridor about a mark dead to windward and a 30 s tack loss, corridor-edge tacking reaches at least 85% of the no-corridor, no-tack-cost bound (median); a header-following controller with a margin chosen on January beats it by at least 3 percentage points in the July holdout; the clairvoyant dynamic programme sits within 5 points above the header-follower.
  - Refuted if: the header-follower's July gain over corridor tacking is under 1 point, or the clairvoyant gap exceeds 10 points, or the best angle leaves 35 to 55 degrees.
- Outcome (`experiments/run_sail_polar.py` -> `results_sail_polar.txt`, `experiments/run_sail_tack.py` -> `results_sail_tack.txt`; deterministic; tests `tests/test_sail.py`, 7 pass):
  - S1 partly refuted. Over 200 random drones (sail 3 to 8 m2, AR 2 to 4.5, CLmax 1.0 to 1.5, keel span 0.6 to 1.4 m, hull drag x0.8 to 1.6, length 4/5/6 m) the best upwind true wind angle at 5 m/s has median 40 degrees (10th to 90th 34 to 46) but ranges 30 to 52: 180 of 200 draws fall in 35 to 55, 20 do not. The analytic check holds: with hull drag near zero the model's apparent wind angle is 4.43 degrees, equal to atan(D/L) at the sail's best L/D of 12.92. Default drone: VMG 1.99 m/s at 5 m/s wind (boat speed 2.52 m/s at 38 degrees); above about 8 m/s the Froude cap (0.5, 3.5 m/s) binds and the best angle falls to 28 degrees at 12 m/s.
  - Tack control, 24 h windows from 12 station-months each of Jan and Jul (358 and 360 windows used), +-1.5 km corridor, share of the no-corridor no-tack-cost bound, tau 30 s, July holdout: corridor-edge tacking median 0.837 (10th to 90th 0.564 to 0.899), header-following (margin 0.3, chosen on January) 0.876, clairvoyant DP 0.997. Header minus corridor +3.2 points (bootstrap 95% interval over windows, treated as independent: 2.7 to 3.7; header better in 327 of 360 windows and positive in all 12 stations). At tau 0 / 60 s the gain is +3.7 / +2.6 points.
  - S2 verdicts: corridor at least 85%: refuted narrowly (83.7% in July, 82.5% in January). Header gain at least 3 points: met, with an interval that touches 2.7. Clairvoyant within 5 points of the header-follower: refuted, the gap is 12.1 points at tau 30 s (the pre-registered refutation was over 10).
- Reading: against the same bound, a boat that only watches the wind in front of it loses about 12% of upwind progress to tacking (tau 30 s), and following headers recovers a quarter of that. The clairvoyant reaches 99.7%, so the corridor and the tack loss are not what limits the boat; knowing the next shift is. How much of that 12-point gap a real forecast can close is the open question.
- Limits: (1) the clairvoyant also exploits the noise in the 6-minute samples (gusts), which no forecast gives, so 12 points overstates what is attainable; (2) wind is uniform in space, the mark is dead to windward of the window's mean wind, and currents, waves and leeway drift are absent; (3) tack loss is a pure time penalty (30 and 60 s are swept, not sourced); (4) the polar is a first-order model whose inputs are swept, not a vendor or measured polar, and the hull is a displacement Wigley form limited to Froude 0.5; (5) the 12 stations are US harbours and piers with their local sea-breeze and land effects, not open water; (6) the header margin was picked on only one month of the same stations, so July is a temporal not a spatial holdout; (7) the DP bins y at 25 m and interpolates, so it is an upper bound up to that error (tests).
- Next: a forecast-skill step (give the controller a persistence or NWP-style forecast of the next 30 min and see how much of the 12 points it recovers; same data, no new fetch); spatial wind fields and isochrone routing (needs a gridded product such as ERA5 or GFS, check the proxy); wave-added resistance upwind.

## 2026-11-04 · Milestone 7p: 4h and 4i folded into the paper draft (no experiment run); persistence step blocked
- Why: 4i's "Next". Added the CO-OPS pooled figures and the calm/moderate/strong split to section 2.3 of `paper/DRAFT.md`, every number copied from `results_current_coops.txt` and `results_current_coops_regimes.txt`, with their limits.
- Blocked: the stall-persistence step needs the raw 6-minute series; `api.tidesandcurrents.noaa.gov` returned `CONNECT tunnel failed, 403` through this session's egress proxy (it was reachable in the 4h run), so nothing was fetched. Retry from a session where the host answers.
- Limits: prose only; no new numbers.
- Next: persistence of stalling from the raw series (needs NOAA host); a shelf product outside ports.

## 2026-11-04 · Milestone 4i: 4h's coastal-current test split by each station's own median speed
- Why: 4h's "Next": the pooled 61% stall figure mixes calm bay stations with tidal channels. Groups come from the data (station median speed: calm <= 0.2 m/s, moderate 0.2 to 0.5, strong > 0.5), not from hand labels of the stations.
- Prediction (written before the run): glider stalling is under 35% worst case in the calm group, over 60% in the moderate group, and over 80% in the strong group; the glider keeps a majority of energy wins only in the calm group.
- Outcome (`experiments/run_current_coops_regimes.py`, `results_current_coops_regimes.txt`, deterministic, about 1 min). Calm, 18 stations: stalls 31.6% worst case, wins 67.1% (random heading: 17.9% and 81.1%). Moderate, 30 stations: stalls 66.6%, wins 32.5% (43.5% and 55.4%). Strong, 16 stations: stalls 84.0%, wins 15.6% (64.6% and 34.6%). Station-weighted, the three groups reproduce 4h's pooled 61.1% worst-case stall. Prediction held, including the one-sided claim: the glider wins a majority at random heading in calm and moderate groups but not in the strong group, and in worst case only in the calm group.
- Reading: the glider's energy advantage is conditional on the site, and a buyer can read the site class off one number, the local median current, against the glider's 0.2 m/s. Only the 18 calm stations (28%) leave the 4e/4f advantage mostly intact.
- Limits: grouping by the same data it is then evaluated on; thresholds 0.2 and 0.5 chosen for the glider speed and round numbers, not tuned, but not sensitivity-checked; one month, US ports only (see 4h); no tests added (reuses 4h's tested functions; the pooled figure is reproduced by hand above, not by a test).
- Next: persistence of stalling from the raw series; a shelf product outside ports; fold 4h and 4i into `paper/DRAFT.md`.

## 2026-11-04 · Milestone 7o: Steinberg et al. citation finalised (no experiment run)
- Why: 7n's "Next": three sources had no DOI.
- What: Crossref lookup found Steinberg et al., MTS Journal 44(2), 46-55, 2010, doi:10.4031/mtsj.44.2.3 (author list checked against the record); added to `PROPOSAL.md` Related work. Two sources remain without a final DOI or URL (Ocean Science os-2019-36 author response; MARTECH 2025 paper 17).
- Limits: the paper was read in 4e from the PDF; this entry only fixes the reference. No numbers changed.
- Next: the two remaining sources; shelf currents from a tide model or ADCP.
## 2026-11-04 · Milestone 4h: 4g's current test on observed coastal currents (NOAA CO-OPS), where OSCAR understates them
- Why: 4g's "Next" and limit: OSCAR is smoothed and understates shelf and tidal currents, where survey work happens. Source: NOAA CO-OPS PORTS observed currents (`datagetter?product=currents`), 31 days of 6-minute readings (Jan 2024) at 64 of the 85 stations in the `currents` list that returned at least 1,000 readings (the rest returned nothing for that month); the speed is the median over depth bins at each reading. The 35 MB raw series is not kept; `experiments/data/coops_currents.csv` holds each station's speed at percentiles 0 to 100 step 5, rebuilt by `fetch_coops_currents.py`.
- Prediction: not written before the run (I looked at the station list and the data format first); from the 4g result I expected the glider to stall in well over half the readings, since many stations are tidal channels.
- Outcome (`experiments/run_current_coops.py`, `results_current_coops.txt`, deterministic, under 1 s): speed median 0.303 m/s, 75th 0.593, 90th 0.957, 95th 1.207; above 0.2 m/s in 63.5% of readings and above 0.5 in 31.2% (OSCAR: 30.1% above 0.2). Worst case: glider cannot hold the track in 61.1% of readings and has lower energy per ground metre than the AUV in 38.0% of triples (OSCAR 29.6% and 69.0%). Random heading: 41.6% stalled, 57.4% glider wins (OSCAR 16.5% and 82.5%). Only 18 of 64 stations have a median speed at or below 0.2 m/s.
- Reading: in the places CO-OPS measures, the glider's energy advantage from 4e/4f is much smaller and its track-holding failure is the common case, not the exception. The "glider wins" share is still a majority at a random heading, but only because the AUV also stalls (it cannot hold a track against more than 1.5 m/s; the stalled pair counts as no win).
- Limits: the stations are US ports, ship channels, bridge piers, bay entrances and rivers (for example Cape Cod Canal, Houston Ship Channel), chosen for navigation, not for where a 100 to 500 kg survey craft would work, so they overstate a typical shelf survey's current; one month of one winter; the bin-median speed is not the speed at a glider's depth; stations are weighted equally though they sit in different regimes; the percentile summary drops the order of readings, so persistence (how long a glider is stalled) is not measured; no pre-registered prediction. A test checks the data file's shape only.
- Next: a per-regime split (open bay entrance against river and channel); persistence of stalling from the raw series (kept out of the repo); a shelf product (HF radar or a tide model) for surveys outside ports.

## 2026-11-04 · Milestone 4g: 4f's cross-current sweep weighted by a sourced global surface-current distribution (OSCAR)
- Why: 4f swept the cross-current 0 to 0.5 m/s with no source, and 4f-days ended with "a current model, since 0.2 m/s is below typical ocean currents". Source: OSCAR 1/3 degree, 5-day, 15 m surface velocity (NASA/JPL, via NOAA CoastWatch ERDDAP `jplOscar`), 13 dates of 2013, every 4th degree, 60S to 60N, ocean points, weighted by cos(latitude). Raw samples kept in `experiments/data/oscar_{u,v}.csv`.
- Prediction (written before the run): the share of ocean where the glider cannot hold a track is between 20% and 40% in the worst case (whole current cross-track), and the glider keeps lower energy per ground metre than the AUV in a clear majority of triples at a random heading.
- Outcome (`experiments/run_current_oscar.py`, `results_current_oscar.txt`, deterministic, under 2 min): weighted speed median 0.126 m/s, 90th 0.389, 95th 0.533; 30.1% of the ocean above 0.2 m/s and 16.3% above 0.3. Worst case: glider stalls in 29.6%, wins energy per ground metre in 69.0% of triples. Random heading: stalls 16.5%, wins 82.5%. Prediction held.
- Reading: with a sourced current climatology the glider's energy advantage over the AUV survives in most of the open ocean, but a quarter to a third of the ocean (the strong boundary currents and equatorial belt) is out of reach for a 0.2 m/s glider on a transect that crosses the flow. 4f's "stalling, not gradual loss" result now has a map-weighted frequency.
- Limits: OSCAR is smoothed (1/3 degree, 5-day, geostrophic plus Ekman), so it understates eddy and tidal currents, especially on shelves and near coasts, where survey work happens; 13 dates of a single year and a 4 degree subsample, so seasonal and interannual spread is not resolved; no heading data for a real mission, hence the two bounds; no tests added (reuses tested functions, and the 4f table is reproduced at matching speeds).
- Next: coastal currents (ADCP or tide-model speeds) for shelf surveys; a per-basin split; fold into `paper/DRAFT.md`.

## 2026-11-03 · Milestone 4f: mission days on one 1.5 kWh battery, glider against AUV at sourced hotel loads
- Why: 4e's "Next" asked for a time-weighted comparison, since the glider wins on energy per metre but moves at 0.2 m/s. Plan (`experiments/run_endurance_days.py`): same 1.5 kWh battery (REMUS 100 vendor figure, 4c) for both; glider as 4e (hotel 0.0954 W, dive electronics 0 or 1351 J, cd0/B/pump swept, 90 cases); AUV at 8 W (Tethys design estimate), 104 and 175 W (REMUS implied), three hulls, best-range speed. Outputs: range, days to empty.
- Prediction (written before the run): the glider has the longer range in every case but needs more than 10 times the AUV's days at 104 W and above, so the choice is a deadline question, not a range one.
- Outcome (`results_endurance_days.txt`, deterministic, under 1 s): glider range median 2,919 km (10th to 90th 1,213 to 4,883) and 164 days (41 to 386) with no dive electronics; 2,265 km and 127 days with 1,351 J. AUV: 363 to 445 km in 5.0 to 5.1 days at 8 W; 64 to 69 km in 0.5 days at 104 W; 41 to 43 km in 0.3 days at 175 W. The glider out-ranges the AUV in 90 of 90 cases at every hotel load, and takes longer in 90 of 90. Prediction held on range. Days: ratios of the glider's median days (164) to the AUV's are about 33 at 8 W, 330 at 104 W and 550 at 175 W (hand arithmetic from the table; the glider's own 10th to 90th spread, 41 to 386 days, gives 8 to 77 at 8 W), so the 'more than 10 times' part held for the median and failed at the glider's fast end against the 8 W AUV.
- Reading: on one battery the glider buys about 7 to 70 times the median range for about 33 to 550 times the median days. A survey with a deadline under about a week picks the AUV at any of its hotel loads; a persistent-observation job picks the glider.
- Limits: 1.5 kWh is the REMUS pack, far below a Seaglider's lithium pack, so absolute glider days are not a fielded figure; only the ratios carry; at 104 W and 175 W the AUV best-range speed sits at the 1.5 m/s grid ceiling, so its ranges are lower bounds on its own optimum (datasheet maximum 2.3 m/s); no current, steady flight; no tests added (reuses tested functions).
- Next: a measured AUV hotel budget; a current model, since 0.2 m/s is below typical ocean currents.

## 2026-11-03 · Milestone 4e: glider against AUV at sourced AUV hotel loads (8 W Tethys design estimate; 104 to 175 W REMUS implied)
- Why: 4d's "Next": a sourced AUV hotel load. Searched for a measured REMUS/Bluefin budget and found none. What exists: Bellingham et al. (MBARI, AUV 2010, the Tethys paper, text extracted from the PDF) estimate a hotel load of 8 W as achievable for a CTD, oxygen, backscatter, fluorescence, nitrate and velocity payload at a duty cycle, and measured about 12 W of propulsion at 0.96 m/s. Steinberg et al. say REMUS100 total consumption is O(10 W) with 22 h endurance, which contradicts 1.5 kWh and 12 h (125 W), so I did not use it. The 8 W is a design estimate by the vehicle's builders, not a measured budget.
- Prediction: not pre-registered; from 4d's break-even median of 0.36 to 0.54 W, I expected 100% glider wins at every sourced AUV hotel (8 W and above).
- Outcome (`experiments/run_auv_hotel_sourced.py`, `results_auv_hotel_sourced.txt`, 270 cases per row, deterministic): the glider has the longer best range in 270 of 270 cases at AUV hotel 8, 12, 104 and 175 W, for both dive-electronics settings (0 and 1351 J). 5 W already gives 100%. Prediction held; the result is the same as 4d's at 5 W, so this milestone adds the sources, not new sensitivity.
- Reading: H4's glider range advantage holds against every AUV hotel load I could source, including the lowest-power long-range AUV design. It flips only below about 0.4 to 1.7 W of AUV hotel load, which no propeller AUV payload with navigation reaches.
- Limits: still energy per metre, not time (the glider moves at 0.2 m/s); the glider side is a Seaglider load on Slocum aerodynamics (4d); the 8 W is a design estimate; no measured REMUS or Bluefin hotel budget was found; no new tests (reuses tested functions).
- Next: a measured AUV hotel budget (vendor integration manuals, KTH field-trial power data); time-weighted comparison (energy per metre against mission duration).

## 2026-11-03 · Milestone 4d: glider against AUV with a measured Seaglider load budget (hotel 0.095 W, dive electronics 0 to 1.35 kJ)
- Why: 4c's last "Next" asked for surface-phase energy. Searching for it turned up something more useful: Table 1 of the Ocean Science preprint os-2019-36 author response (opened as a PDF and read) breaks down one measured Seaglider dive (sg564, dive 227, Iceland Sea, 5 Nov 2015, 1000 m, 11 h): total 5410 J (137 mW mean), buoyancy engine 1633 J, GPS+Iridium 1014 J, controller 2028 J, attitude 414 J, sensors 321 J. So the whole non-buoyancy load of a measured glider is 3777 J / 39,600 s = **0.095 W**, below the 0.7 to 5 W hotel range every earlier glider step (4a to 4c) swept. It is a Seaglider; the model keeps Slocum aerodynamics (4b), so this is the sourced load of a sibling vehicle, not of the modelled one.
- Prediction: not written before the run (I saw the table first and ran it directly; recorded here so nobody reads it as pre-registered). What I expected from the arithmetic: the glider wins at AUV hotel 1 W in a clear majority of cases.
- Outcome (`experiments/run_seaglider_budget.py`, `results_seaglider_budget.txt`, 270 cases per row, deterministic): AUV hotel is now swept in absolute watts, since 'x glider hotel' is meaningless at 0.095 W. With e_cycle 0: glider farther in 63.0% / 83.3% / 94.4% / 100% at AUV hotel 0.5 / 1 / 2 / 5 W; break-even AUV hotel median 0.36 W (10th to 90th 0.16 to 1.41). With the inflection+surface electronics 1351 J (1172+179) per dive: 44.1% / 75.6% / 92.2% / 100%; break-even median 0.54 W (0.28 to 1.69). The AUV never wins at zero hotel.
- Reading: with a measured glider load, H4's glider advantage holds once the AUV carries more than about 0.5 W of hotel load; 4c's much weaker advantage came from a 14 to 35 kJ per-cycle dive energy (Slocum worst-case bench test at 1000 m) against 1.35 kJ here, a factor of 10 the two vehicles' different buoyancy engines may or may not explain. The deciding unsourced input is now the AUV's hotel load, not the glider's.
- Limits: Seaglider load with Slocum aerodynamics; one low-power, slow dive (vertical 5 cm/s), which the authors chose as an example, so typical loads are probably higher; the table's buoyancy-engine energy is partly double-counted against the model's pump term, which is why the cycle energy is bracketed rather than added; the AUV hotel range is unsourced; no tests added for this script (it only reuses tested functions).
- Next: a sourced AUV hotel load (REMUS/Bluefin hotel in the vehicle datasheets), which now decides the ranking.

## 2026-11-03 · Milestone 4c: glider against AUV with the dive energy taken from a Slocum G2 bench test
- Why: 4b swept the glider's dive energy over {0, 5, 20 kJ} per cycle, unsourced; 7e/7f left it the deciding input and found no figure. The SOCIB bench test of Slocum G2 batteries (MARTECH 2025, paper 17, two pages, opened and read) gives a measured high-drain phase: 3 min at 6.43 A, regular battery range 15 V to 12 V, modelling the deep inflection of a 1000 m dive under the harshest expected conditions. Electrical energy 6.43 A x 12/13.5/15 V x 180 s = 13.9 / 15.6 / 17.4 kJ per inflection.
- Prediction (before running, `experiments/run_slocum_socib.py`): the glider still wins in 0% of cases at equal hotel load. I also predicted the break-even AUV hotel multiple would fall below 4b's median 4.87; that was a sign error on my part, since a costlier dive makes the glider worse and so raises the hotel multiple the AUV can carry before it loses.
- Outcome (`results_slocum_socib.txt`, 2,430 cases per row, deterministic, seconds): one inflection per cycle (13.9 to 17.4 kJ): glider farther in 0% at equal hotel, 19.7% at AUV hotel x5 (4b: 51.6%), 99.9% at x20; break-even multiple median 7.19 (10th to 90th 4.19 to 11.62; 4b 4.87). Two inflections per cycle (27.8 to 34.7 kJ): 0%, 3.4%, 87.5%; break-even median 11.26 (6.16 to 21.12). The AUV beats the glider even at zero hotel in 0 cases either way. The first part of the prediction held; the second was wrong in direction.
- Reading: with a measured dive energy the glider needs an AUV whose hotel load is about 7 to 11 times its own to win on range, against 4.9 in 4b. H4's glider advantage is therefore smaller than 4b suggested at 0.2 m/s, and rests on the hotel gap (glider 0.7 to 5 W is itself swept).
- Limits: the bench profile is a worst case, for 1000 m dives, while the model's depth is 500 m and e_cycle is spread over it as in 4b (so the per-metre cost at 500 m is probably overstated); 'one versus two inflections per cycle' is bracketed, not known; the paper reports 490/565 'Ah' capacity, a unit I could not reconcile and did not use; glider hotel, pump efficiency and buoyancy still swept; surfacing GPS/Iridium energy still unsourced.
- Next: a surface-phase (GPS, Iridium) energy from the Teledyne Webb manual or the Ocean Science 2019 glider energy budget (telemetry 1014 J, 19% of a 5410 J total, seen only as a search excerpt).

- Why: 3i's "Next". The milestone-1 box used k 0.10 to 0.40 (central 0.25); the Delft yacht tank data bound k at about 0.03 to 0.15. Reran the identical 6,561-case box with k_form and k_cat in {0.03, 0.08, 0.15} (`experiments/run_lowk.py`, `results_lowk.txt`, deterministic, about 1 s).
- Outcome: the catamaran out-ranges the monohull in 0 of 6,561 cases (milestone 1: 27, 0.4%), none at equal length; range ratio cat/mono min 0.74, median 0.85, max 0.97 (was 0.72, 0.85, 1.02). Monohull best range 600 to 1,216 km, median 876 km (was 836), best speed median 1.10 m/s (was 1.05). Lower form factors help both hulls about equally, so H2's verdict is unchanged and slightly firmer: the earlier 27 wins came from the k = 0.4 monohull against a k = 0.1 catamaran, a combination the tank bound makes implausible.

## 2026-11-02 · Milestone 3i: what form factor do the Delft yacht tank points imply at survey Froude numbers? (exploratory; no prediction logged before the run)
- Why: 3g found Holtrop-Mennen over-predicts residuary resistance 1.3 to 2.2 times at Fn 0.125 to 0.2, and 3c said its excess is the form factor, not waves. The tank table can say how big the form factor really is: at Fn 0.125 wave making is smallest, and Rr (total minus ITTC-1957 friction) divided by the friction resistance is an upper bound on k.
- Outcome (`experiments/run_yacht_formfactor.py`, `results_yacht_formfactor.txt`, instant, deterministic): implied k at Fn 0.125 has median 0.061 to 0.064 over 22 hulls (10th to 90th percentile 0.03 to 0.13, max 0.18; Cm 0.7 to 0.9), 0.17 at Fn 0.15. HM's k1 for the same hulls has median 0.29 to 0.36. The implied bound is below HM's k1 for 22 of 22 hulls and below the programme's central k = 0.25 for all 22.
- Reading: on these hulls the 3g over-prediction is explained by HM's form factor, which is 3 to 5 times the tank-implied bound. The programme's swept k box (0.25 central) is conservative for slender hulls, consistent with 3a. It also means hull rankings at survey speed that use k = 0.25 or HM k1 favour low-form-drag (long, slender) hulls less than the tank data would.
- Limits: keeled sailing yachts, so appendage and hull form drag are mixed; RF uses HM's wetted-area regression (Cm swept, true area unknown); ITTC-1957 friction in the original table not verified; mirror file unverified (3h); the bound ignores any wave drag at Fn 0.125, so true k is lower still.
- Next: rerun the milestone-1 range box with k from 0.03 to 0.15 to see whether the H1 spread and H2 verdict move; a keel-less fuller hull table.

- Why: 3g's "Next" asked to check the mirror file against the paper; the paper host is blocked, so this checks what can be checked offline. Prediction (before running): 22 hulls x 14 Froude numbers (0.125 to 0.45, step 0.025), no negative Rr, and Rr rising with Fn within every hull; any violation would mean a corrupted mirror.
- Outcome (`experiments/check_yacht_data.py`, `results_yacht_data_check.txt`, instant): 308 rows, 22 distinct hull forms, each with all 14 Froude numbers; no negative Rr; 0 of 286 hull-steps where Rr falls as Fn rises; Rr 0.01 to 62.42 per mille. Prediction held.
- Reading: the mirror is structurally intact and physically monotone, which makes a silent corruption less likely. It does not show the values equal the paper's; 3g's H3 reading is unchanged and the mirror stays flagged unverified.

## 2026-11-02 · Milestone 3g: Holtrop-Mennen residuary resistance against 308 Delft yacht-series tank points (exploratory; not pre-registered)
- Why: H3 had one tank point (3c). The Delft Systematic Yacht Hull Series table (Gerritsma, Onnink and Versluis 1981; UCI "Yacht Hydrodynamics", CC BY 4.0; 22 hulls, Fn 0.125 to 0.45, residuary resistance per unit weight) is a public numeric table. The data host was blocked, so the file came from a public GitHub mirror (`aamini/evidential-deep-learning`, `neurips2020/data/uci/yacht/`); I did not check it against the 1981 paper. Copy: `experiments/data/yacht_hydrodynamics.data`.
- Method (`experiments/run_holtrop_yacht.py`, `results_holtrop_yacht.txt`): Rr is total minus ITTC-1957 friction, so the HM counterpart is RF*k1 + RW (no correlation allowance, as at model scale). Midship coefficient, waterplane coefficient and model length are not in the table, so I swept Cm 0.7 to 0.9, Cwp 0.65 to 0.85, L 1 and 2 m (my ranges). The hulls are sailing yachts with keels and rudders: the comparison is on the hull-form terms only.
- Result: at Fn 0.2 to 0.3 HM is within 20% of the tank value for 44% to 79% of points (median ratio 0.78 to 1.24 across the sweep; 0.98 to 1.04 at Cm 0.8-0.9, Cwp 0.75). At Fn 0.125 to 0.2, the survey regime, HM **over-predicts residuary resistance by a median 1.3 to 2.2 times** in every swept case (central case: 0.45 against 0.09 per mille at Fn 0.125, 0.62 against 0.24 at 0.15, 1.05 against 0.78 at 0.2). Above Fn 0.4 HM stops being valid and falls to a third of the tank value at Fn 0.45 (the low-Fn form was extrapolated by the code, as the paper's range ends there).
- H3 reading: partly supported for the low-speed regime (error well above 20% there, in relative terms) and not supported at Fn 0.2 to 0.3. Absolute size is small: the gap at Fn 0.15 is 0.4 per mille of weight, which matters mostly at the lightest-loaded survey speeds. It points the same way as 3c: HM's extra drag at low Fn is its form term, so rankings built on it at survey speeds err on the conservative side for slender hulls.
- Limits: yacht hulls with appendages, not survey boats; free parameters swept not measured; mirror copy unverified; Cm and Cwp unknown; no seeds (deterministic).
- Next: a survey-boat-like hull (fuller, no keel) tank table; check the mirror against the original paper.

## 2026-11-01 · Milestone 3f: a third public implementation breaks the tie on the Holtrop-Mennen low-speed wave form (reading only, no experiment)
- Why: 3e left m2 against m4 open: Albert-Gil computes m2 but uses m4 below Fn 0.4, B4kif uses m4, and neither the 1982 nor the 1984 paper could be opened (tudelft and sciencedirect refuse the proxy; arXiv has no paper on the method, a search for "Holtrop" returns only nuclear physics).
- Evidence: the US EPA's `Marine_Emissions_Tools` (MIT licence), `ShipPowerModel/R/calcHMWaveMakingRes.r`, read not run, nothing copied, labels its Fn < 0.55 branch as the 1982 paper and codes it as `exp(m1 Fn^-0.9 + m2 cos(lambda Fn^-2))` with `m2 = c15 Cp^2 exp(-0.1 Fn^-2)`, and uses `m4 = c15 0.4 exp(-0.034 Fn^-3.29)` only in the Fn >= 0.55 (1984) branch. Its m1, c15 (three-way rule on L^3/volume) and lambda match the constants in `holtrop.py`.
- Reading: two of three implementations use m2 below Fn 0.4 in the form the programme defaults to, and in the Albert-Gil code the m4 use sits next to an unused m2, the pattern of a slip. This favours the default (`low_form="m2"`), but three transcriptions are still not the paper; the 3e wording stands that the choice moves CW by at most about 12% and CT by 2.4%, and H3 stays neither supported nor refuted. One structural difference: the EPA code applies the low-speed form up to Fn 0.55 with no blend, whereas `holtrop.py` blends from Fn 0.4; irrelevant at the programme's survey speeds (Fn about 0.16) and at the tank point (0.267).
- Limits: nothing run; coefficients still unverified against the paper; the EPA code is for ships of tens to hundreds of metres.
- Next: a small-hull tank curve; the paper itself if a host opens.

## 2026-11-01 · Milestone 3e: is 3d's "bug" a bug? Two public implementations use the m4 form below Fn 0.4 (prediction written before the run)
- Why: 3d called the use of m4 below Fn 0.4 a transcription error, because the Albert-Gil MATLAB code computes an unused m2. A second public implementation, B4kif/holtrop-mennen-kcs-resistance (Python, no licence stated, read not run, only the formula structure noted, nothing copied), also uses m4 = c15 0.4 exp(-0.034/Fn^3.29) in its Fn <= 0.4 branch and has no m2. Two implementations agreeing is weak evidence (they may share a lineage), but it means my memory of "the published form uses m2" is not enough to call the other a bug: from memory, the 1982 paper may use the m4-style expression there and the m2 expression (c15 Cp^2 exp(-0.1/Fn^2)) came with the 1984 revision. Not settled: neither paper was opened.
- Prediction (written before running): swapping forms moves CW at the tank point by under 10% and CT by under 3%, so H3's reading (+15% to +22%, straddling the 20% line) does not depend on the choice.
- Method: `low_form` argument in `holtrop.py` ("m2" default, "m4"); `experiments/run_holtrop_forms.py`, stdlib, instant; one new test.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_holtrop_forms.txt`): at the tank point CW is 1.022e-3 (m2) against 0.944e-3 (m4), -7.6%; CT on HM's own area 4.995e-3 against 4.918e-3 (+20.1% against +18.2% over the tank 4.16e-3), CT ratio 0.985. Over Fn 0.10 to 0.40 the wave ratio m4/m2 is 1.00, 1.00, 0.95, 0.88, 1.13, 1.02, 0.95, the total ratio between 0.978 and 1.024. Prediction held.
- Reading: the choice is worth at most about 12% of wave resistance and 2.4% of total over this range; H3 stays neither supported nor refuted (the m2 form sits right on the 20% line, m4 just under it). The 3d wording "error found and fixed" overstated: it is an unresolved form choice, default unchanged, both kept.
- Limits: one hull; Wigley's L^3/volume = 360 only; the 1982 and 1984 papers still unopened, so which form belongs to which year is memory, flagged as such; the second implementation's licence is unstated, so nothing was copied.
- Next: open the 1982 paper or a worked example to settle it; a small-hull tank curve.

## 2026-11-01 · Milestone 3d: a shared transcription error in the Holtrop-Mennen wave term, found and fixed (not pre-registered)
- Why: 3c's coefficients were "checked by eye" against a public implementation. Re-reading that implementation (Albert-Gil/Ship-Propulsion-Power-Simulator, MIT, read not run) for a mechanical comparison showed it computes `m2 = c15 Cp^2 exp(-0.1/Fn^2)` and never uses it: its Fn < 0.4 wave resistance uses `m4` (the Fn > 0.55 exponent, with the 0.4 factor). `holtrop.py` copied that. In the published method (Holtrop 1984 form) the low-speed branch uses `m2`, the high-speed branch `m4`, and the blend between Fn 0.4 and 0.55 uses the low-speed term evaluated at Fn = 0.4, not at the current Fn. I am fairly but not fully sure of this from memory of the standard form; the unused `m2` in the reference is the evidence, the 1982/1984 papers were still not opened (hosts 403 through the proxy; a second independent implementation, efsolefter/holtrop-mennen, is an empty repository).
- Fix: `rw_a(fn)` uses m2; the blend calls `rw_a(0.4)`. New test pins CW_HM at the tank point (1.02e-3). Not coded: the c15 rule for L^3/volume above 512 (the Wigley is 360).
- Effect (`experiments/results_holtrop.txt`, regenerated): the HM wave coefficient at the tank point rises from 0.94e-3 to 1.02e-3 (+8%); CT on HM's own area becomes 4.85 to 5.08e-3 (+17% to +22% over 4.16e-3, was +15% to +20%), +31% to +37% on the true area (was +29% to +36%). Over Fn 0.10 to 0.40 the HM/Michell(k=0.25) ratio moves by at most 0.03 at Fn <= 0.35 (0.20 to 0.35: 0.98, 1.05, 0.90, 1.20) and 0.03 at Fn 0.40. Conclusions of 3c stand: H3 neither supported nor refuted (+17% to +22% straddles the 20% line), survey-speed rankings unmoved.
- Lesson: two transcriptions agreeing is weak evidence when one copied the other; an unused variable in the reference was the tell, found only by diffing structure, not constants.
- Limits: as 3c; formulas still not checked against the paper; one hull.
- Next: a fuller small-hull tank curve; open the 1984 re-analysis to settle m2 and the blend.

## 2026-11-01 · Milestone 3c: Holtrop-Mennen on the Wigley tank point, and against the programme's own model (exploratory; not pre-registered)
- Why: H3 and milestone 3 had no Holtrop-Mennen code at all after a week of blocked data hosts. `src/marine_hull/holtrop.py` implements the 1982 bare-hull terms (ITTC friction times 1+k1, wave resistance below Fn 0.4 with the blend to 0.55, correlation allowance; bulb, transom and appendages zero). `experiments/run_holtrop.py` applies it to the standard Wigley (Cb 0.444, Cp = Cm = Cwp 2/3, L/B 10) at the one published tank point (Ju 1983 via Wang et al., CT 4.16e-3 at Fn 0.267), then compares it with Michell + ITTC over Fn 0.10 to 0.40. I ran the script while writing it, so no prediction was recorded first; read every number below as exploratory.
- Source of the coefficients: typed from memory and compared by eye, line by line, with an MIT-licensed public implementation (Albert-Gil/Ship-Propulsion-Power-Simulator). I tried to also run that code numerically on the Wigley and the sandbox refused (executing external code), so there is **no numerical cross-check**, and the 1982 paper itself was not opened. A transcription error shared by both would not show. Tests (`tests/test_holtrop.py`) only check internal consistency (components sum, friction equals the ITTC line, wave term rises with Fn, continuity at Fn 0.4).
- Result (`experiments/results_holtrop.txt`): at the tank point HM gives CT = 4.77 to 5.00e-3 using its own wetted-area regression (2.06 m2 against the true 2.38 m2), i.e. +15% to +20% over 4.16e-3 across 10 to 25 C, and 5.38 to 5.64e-3 (+29% to +36%) if the true surface is used with the same coefficients. The HM wave coefficient there is 0.94e-3 against Michell's 0.96e-3, so the excess is the form factor 1+k1 = 1.15 and the correlation allowance CA = 0.80e-3 (about 20% of the tank CT) on a hull whose tank residual is already matched by k = 0.
- Over Fn 0.10 to 0.40 HM is 6% to 42% above Michell + ITTC with k = 0, and between -8% and +20% against the central k = 0.25 (wave ratio HM/Michell 0.02 at Fn 0.10, 0.15 at Fn 0.15, 0.7 at Fn 0.25). At the programme's survey speeds (Fn about 0.16) HM is within 3 to 6% of the k = 0.25 model, so range rankings built on it are not moved by swapping in HM; the difference is mostly CA and k1, not waves.
- H3 reading: on this one slender hull HM is +15% to +20% (own area, the like-for-like HM number) or about +30% (true area): at the 20% line, not robustly across temperature. H3 (more than 20% error) is **neither supported nor refuted**: one point, one hull, second-hand tank value, unverified coefficients.
- Limits: as above; bare hull only; Wigley is not a survey boat; no small-hull tank table obtained.
- Next: open the 1982 paper or an HM worked example to settle the coefficients; a Fn 0.15 to 0.3 tank curve of a fuller small hull.

## 2026-11-01 · Milestone 7g: is Iridium messaging the glider's deciding fixed energy? (exploratory; one sourced input, arithmetic only)
- Why: 7e left a glider mission overhead as the deciding unsourced input; 7f found no Wh-per-surfacing figure. A second search returned an excerpt of the Spray glider description page: Iridium link energy about 40 J for the first kilobyte and 30 J per further kilobyte. The page itself could not be opened (`spray.ucsd.edu` does not resolve from this session), so the figure is taken from the search excerpt and is for a Spray, not a Slocum.
- Method (`experiments/run_iridium_overhead.py`, stdlib, instant, 3 tests): energy = N x (40 + 30 (kB - 1)) J for N surfacings and kB per surfacing; both swept, unsourced. Compared with 7e's thresholds (100 Wh swaps 0.7% of glider/AUV cases, 250 Wh 2.3%, 500 Wh 6.0%).
- Outcome (`experiments/results_iridium_overhead.txt`): 100 surfacings of 5 kB cost 4.4 Wh; 1,000 of 5 kB cost 44 Wh; reaching 100 Wh at 5 kB takes about 2,250 surfacings. Only a very high-volume case (1,000 surfacings of 20 kB or more, 169 Wh) crosses 7e's first threshold.
- Reading: for plausible mission counts Iridium messaging alone is far below the overhead that would flip the glider/AUV order. Whatever overhead decides H5 for the glider, it is not the link energy; surface GPS, the pump priming, ballasting and hotel load while surfaced remain unsourced and are the remaining candidates.
- Limits: one figure from a search excerpt for a different vehicle; surfacing counts and message sizes are not sourced; no GPS or surface hotel energy; not a flip count, only a comparison with 7e's thresholds.

## 2026-10-31 · Milestone 7e: H5 for the glider against the propeller AUV: what fixed-energy gap flips the order? (not pre-registered)
- Why: 7d's "Next". The glider and the AUV have different energy structures, so the equal-overhead proportionality argument of 7c needs its own check. `experiments/run_h5_glider.py` (stdlib, seconds) reuses run_glider.py's full 19,683-case grid (52 kg, 500 m dives), takes each vehicle's best-range energy per metre, and a 10 kg pack at 150 to 250 Wh/kg (1.5 to 2.5 kWh). Run in one go with no predictions logged first: exploratory.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_h5_glider.txt`): the glider has the lower energy per metre in 12,803 of 19,683 cases (65.0%), e_g/e_a median 0.62 (5% to 95%: 0.17 to 2.57). With equal overhead there are 0 rank swaps across the 150 to 250 Wh/kg window, as 7c's argument says. A glider fixed-energy penalty of 100 Wh (7% of the smallest pack) swaps the order in 130 cases (0.7%), 250 Wh in 445 (2.3%), 500 Wh in 1,181 (6.0%), 1,000 Wh in 3,969 (20%). Among cases with the glider ahead, the median overhead that would flip it at 150 Wh/kg is 931 Wh of 1,500.
- Reading: unlike mono against catamaran (7d: 3 swaps in 2,187 cases at best), a glider/AUV ranking is sensitive to a fixed per-mission energy, because the glider's energy per metre is small and the AUV's hotel load is large. Whether such a gap exists (ballasting, surfacing, GPS fixes, pump priming) is unsourced here; H4's published figures do not give it. H5 stays refuted at equal overhead and is conditional on an unsourced term otherwise.
- Limits: swept, unsourced constants; the 65% includes grid corners that favour the AUV (low hotel multiple); overhead is a number I chose; one seed-free deterministic grid, no confidence intervals needed but also no real data.
- Next: source a glider mission overhead (surfacing, GPS, ballast) from a vendor datasheet or paper; milestone 3 curve at survey speed.

## 2026-10-31 · Milestone 7d: can a hull-specific fixed mission energy flip the mono/cat ranking? (not pre-registered)
- Why: 7c's "Next". Range is linear in pack energy for every hull, so ranking cannot change with specific energy unless the hulls carry different fixed energy E0 (launch, recovery): range = (E - E0)/e_km, flip at E* = c d/(c - m) for catamaran extra overhead d (Wh) and km/Wh figures m (mono), c (cat). `experiments/run_h5_overhead.py` (stdlib, seconds), 2,187 cases, 150 to 250 Wh/kg of a 50 kg pack. Written and run in one go: I logged no predictions first, so treat the outcome as exploratory.
- Outcome (`experiments/results_h5_overhead.txt`): the catamaran out-ranges the monohull at equal overhead in only 9 of 2,187 cases (0.4%), so there is almost nothing to flip. Rank swaps: 0 at d = 0, 3 (0.1%) at d = 100 Wh, 0 at 250 Wh and above (past the window, the cat has already lost by 7.5 kWh). The closed form agrees with brute force in every row (new test).
- Reading: within this model H5 stays refuted for mono against catamaran, even with a hull-specific fixed energy; a swap needs a near-tie in km/Wh and an overhead gap tuned to the 7.5 to 12.5 kWh window. The only pairs with real flip potential are the ones with close km/Wh (the nine cat-ahead cases), which the sweeps of run.py can list.
- Limits: swept unsourced constants; overhead is a single number I chose, not sourced; two hull types; the 9 cases are not examined individually.
- Next: milestone 3 curve at survey speed when a data host allows; H5 across glider/AUV (different energy structure) is the better remaining test.

## 2026-10-31 · Milestone 7c: H1 and H5 tested as the proposal states them (planned before running)
- Why: 7b found neither was ever run as written. `experiments/run_h1_h5.py` (stdlib, seconds). H1: monohull 200 kg, 4 m, 30 W hotel, 200 Wh/kg; 27 combinations of c_s (2.6 to 3.4), k_form (0.10 to 0.40), wave_scale (0 to 1). H5: mono against catamaran over run.py's box (2,187 cases), pack 150 to 250 Wh/kg, (a) battery 25% of 200 kg, (b) 5 kWh fixed so a better pack is lighter.
- **Predictions (before running):** (1) H1 refuted: best range spreads by 10% or more across the 27 combinations (prior 85%). (2) Best speed spreads by 15% or more (prior 55%). (3) The propulsion share of power at the best speed is within 0.30 to 0.40 everywhere (prior 70%; theory for v^3 drag gives 1/3). (4) H5 (a) has 0 rank swaps, by proof: range is linear in pack energy for every hull at fixed mass (prior 99%). (5) H5 (b) has swaps in under 2% of cases (prior 70%). Refuted: any one missed.

### Outcome (`experiments/results_h1_h5.txt`)
- **H1 refuted as stated.** Best range 763 to 921 km, a spread of 20.6%; best speed 1.00 to 1.20 m/s (20.0%). Prediction (1) and (2) held. One at a time, c_s moves range by 9.0%, k_form by 8.1% and wave_scale by 0.8%, so each hull constant alone is under the 10% line and together they are over it. The "set by the hotel load" half holds: the propulsion share of power at the best speed is 0.33 to 0.37 (3 held), the 1/3 of v^3 drag (new test `tests/test_best_speed_share.py`).
- **H5 refuted, and not a finding about hulls.** (a) 0 swaps in 2,187 cases; the cat/mono ratio does not change at all (4 held). (b) 0 swaps; the ratio moves at most 0.13% because a lighter hull displaces less (5 held). Within this model a battery specific energy cannot flip a mono-versus-catamaran ranking; a flip needs a term that is not proportional to pack energy (a fixed energy per mission, a payload that scales with pack mass, or a mass-limited hull). Not tested against glider, SWATH or planing rungs, whose ranges are also linear in pack energy here.
- Limits: swept, unsourced constants; two hull types for H5; one mass and length for H1.
- Next: H5 with a term that breaks the proportionality (a fixed mission energy or a pack-volume limit); milestone 3 curve when a data host is reachable.
