## 2026-10-30 · Milestone 3b: a published catamaran bare-hull tank curve against the programme's displacement resistance model (planned before running)
- Why: milestone 3 has had one tank point (Wigley, 3a). This session zenodo.org also holds CC BY 4.0 data from a towing-tank study of a catamaran (record 18459868; five bare-hull speeds, Fn 0.33 to 0.79, water 16.8 C, with trim). It is a curve, not a point, but the hull is semi-planing, so it tests where the programme's displacement model (ITTC 1957 times (1+k), plus `wave_resistance_fraction`) stops applying. Hull geometry is not in the files: L = 2.811 m follows from Fn = V/sqrt(gL), and the wetted area S follows from CT = RT/(0.5 rho V^2 S) (both derived, stated as such). Water assumed fresh (rho 998.7, nu 1.09e-6 interpolated from ITTC 7.5-02-01-03 at 16.8 C).
- Plan (`experiments/run_catamaran_tank.py`, stdlib): implied S per speed; CF at each Re; the tank residual CR = CT - (1+k) CF for k in {0, 0.1, 0.25}; model CT with the placeholder wave term (hump 0.4, k = 0.25) and its error per speed; the best single `wave_scale` fitted over all five speeds and over the three lowest, with the worst error each leaves.
- **Predictions (before running):** (1) implied S is constant within 3% over the five speeds (prior 75%; the authors presumably used one reference area). (2) The central placeholder (k = 0.25, wave_scale 1) is within 20% at Fn 0.33 and off by more than 20% at Fn 0.72 and 0.79, overpredicting (prior 80%), because tank CT falls as the hull planes while the placeholder keeps rising. (3) No single wave_scale keeps all five speeds within 20% (prior 85%). (4) Fitted on the three lowest speeds only, the worst error over those three is within 15% (prior 55%). Refuted: (1) spread of 3% or more; (2) either condition fails; (3) some wave_scale keeps all five within 20%; (4) worst error of the three above 15%.
- Limits known in advance: five points, one hull, water type and geometry inferred; the Hull Vane study's own CFD is not used; the hull is not a survey boat of the programme's kind (the 2 to 4 m catamaran range is, though, the right size). H3 (Holtrop-Mennen) is still untouched.

## 2026-10-30 · Milestone 6g: with three sea states instead of two, how much of the oracle gain does persistence capture? (planned before running)
- Why: 6f's "Next". A two-state chain forces a single rough/calm boundary; a third state (moderate) makes yesterday's call wrong more often at a boundary and gives the policy a middle speed to use. Still swept, not sourced.
- Plan (`experiments/run_solar_seastate_persist3.py`, 6f's boat, five sites, packs 10 and 1 kWh, seed 1): states calm / moderate / rough with multipliers F = 1, 1.5, 3 and stationary shares 0.4, 0.3, 0.3. Each day the state is resampled from those shares with probability q and kept otherwise; q in {1.0 (iid), 0.5, 0.15} (mean spells about 1.7, 3.3 and 11 days). Policies: B best constant speed; C oracle with a speed per state; E persistence (speed per state chosen in-sample on yesterday's state). Report E/B, hit rate and the share of the C-B gap E recovers.
- **Predictions (before running):** (1) at q = 1 the hit rate equals the sum of squared shares, 0.34, within 0.03 in all 10 cells (prior 90%). (2) At q = 1 the median share is at most 0.15 and E/B under +1.5% everywhere (prior 65%). (3) The median share rises monotonically with 1/q, and at q = 0.15 is at least 0.5 (prior 70%). (4) At q = 0.15 the three-state share is below 6f's two-state 7-day median of 0.75 in the median (prior 55%: more boundaries, more wrong calls). Refuted: (1) any cell off by 0.03 or more; (2) median above 0.15 or any E/B of +1.5% or more; (3) a non-monotone step or median under 0.5; (4) median share 0.75 or above.
- Limits known in advance: F values and shares are swept choices, not sourced; one seed; in-sample speed triple; exact persistence; resampling chain, not a fitted wave climate.

### Outcome of milestone 6g (`experiments/run_solar_seastate_persist3.py`, about 6 minutes, seeded; `experiments/results_solar_seastate_persist3.txt`; 30 cells)
- **(1) held:** the hit rate at q = 1 is 0.34 in all 10 cells.
- **(2) held:** at q = 1 the median share of the C-B gap is 0.10 (range 0.04 to 0.16) and the largest E/B is +0.6%; 0 of 30 cells have E below B.
- **(3) held:** median share 0.10 (hit 0.34) -> 0.48 (q = 0.5, hit 0.67; range 0.43 to 0.65) -> 0.89 (q = 0.15, hit 0.90; range 0.86 to 0.93).
- **(4) refuted, with a confound:** the q = 0.15 median share is 0.89, above 6f's two-state 7-day 0.75. The comparison is not like for like: q = 0.15 gives mean spells of about 11 days against 7, and hit rate 0.90 against 0.85. At comparable hit rates the two chains look alike (6f: 0.85 -> 0.75; here 0.90 -> 0.89, 0.67 -> 0.48 against 6f's 0.67 -> 0.28), so the third state does not obviously hurt persistence; if anything the middle speed lets a wrong call cost less. I did not test this separately.
- Reading: the shape from 6f carries over to three states. Persistence is near worthless on an iid sea (a tenth of the oracle gain, all of it in-sample fitting of the speed triple) and recovers about half the gain at a 67% hit rate and about 90% at a 90% hit rate. What a sensor-only boat gains depends on spell length, which this programme still cannot source.
- Limits: as planned. F values and shares are choices, one seed, in-sample triple, exact persistence, resampling chain rather than a fitted wave climate.
- Next: a wave-height series if one becomes reachable; milestone 3 tank data remains blocked (Crossref and arXiv answer through the proxy but only return papers, not tables; `eprints.soton.ac.uk` still 401).

### Outcome of milestone 3b (`experiments/run_catamaran_tank.py`, under 1 second, deterministic; `experiments/results_catamaran_tank.txt`; data `experiments/data/catamaran_bare_hull_tank.csv`)
- Derived geometry: L = 2.812 m (from Fn) and S = 1.709 m2 (from RT and CT).
- **(1) held, but by construction:** the implied S is identical at all five speeds (spread 0.0%), so the authors used one reference area. It is a consistency check on my reading of the table, not a finding.
- **(2) refuted, and the direction was wrong:** I predicted the placeholder would overpredict at Fn 0.72 and 0.79. It underpredicts at low and middle speed: -35% (Fn 0.33), -43% (0.46), -37% (0.58), then -9% (0.72) and +1% (0.79). The tank residual CR = CT - CF is 5.2, 7.4 and 6.8e-3 at the three lowest speeds, 1.5 to 2.1 times CF, with a hump near Fn 0.46 that the placeholder's wave fraction (0.30 to 0.49 of viscous drag) is far too small to produce. My reasoning that tank CT falls with planing was right (10.7 to 6.4e-3) but I forgot the placeholder is a fraction of viscous drag, so it cannot fall or hump.
- **(3) held:** no single wave_scale keeps all five speeds within 20%; the best leaves 29% to 32% worst error for k from 0.40 to 0. The placeholder's functional form (monotone in Fn) cannot follow a hump followed by a fall.
- **(4) held:** fitted to the three lowest speeds the worst error is 7% to 9%, but that needs wave_scale 2.4 to 4.2 (k = 0.40 to 0), and the same scale then overpredicts the two fast points by 82% to 92%.
- Reading: on a published 2.8 m catamaran the programme's wave term is short by a factor of about 3 at Fn 0.3 to 0.6, the opposite sign to the Wigley point (3a), where the central constants were 20% high. Combined, the placeholder is not safe for catamarans above Fn 0.3 and the form-factor box is not the dominant error there. The programme's survey speeds (about 1 m/s, Fn 0.16 on a 4 m hull, 6g and earlier) sit below this data's range, so the range rankings are untouched; the ranking claim for catamarans (H2) is not tested at Fn 0.16 by this data. This is a bare hull with trim and sinkage; the model has neither.
- Limits: as planned (five points, one hull, geometry and water type inferred, tabulated by the authors, read second-hand from the spreadsheet only). H3 (Holtrop-Mennen) still untouched.
- Next: a Fn 0.15 to 0.3 catamaran or Wigley-catamaran curve in the survey-speed range; Holtrop-Mennen on this hull (needs a body plan the files do not give).

## 2026-10-30 · Milestone 3a: one published Wigley tank point against the Michell + ITTC model (planned before running)
- Why: milestone 3 (tank data) has been blocked for a week by data hosts. This session zenodo.org was reachable and holds Wang et al., *Wave-resistance computation via CFD and IGA-BEM solvers: a comparative study* (OPEC 2017, zenodo.org/records/1169033, CC BY-NC-ND, read only, nothing redistributed). It quotes the Wigley tank measurement of Ju (1983): U0 = 1.67 m/s (Fr = 0.267), wetted area Aw = Cs L (2D + B) with Cs = 0.661, **CT = 4.16e-3** on Aw. One point, not a curve, and second-hand (I have not opened Ju 1983). It is the first number in the programme from a tank.
- Assumptions I cannot check from the paper's text: L = 4 m, B = 0.4 m, D = 0.25 m (the standard Wigley, L/B = 10, B/T = 1.6; L = 4 m is implied by Fr = 0.267 at 1.67 m/s), water fresh. Reynolds number is swept over nu for 10 to 25 C.
- Plan (`experiments/run_wigley_tank.py`, stdlib): CT_model = (1 + k) CF(ITTC 1957, Re from nu) * S_exact / Aw + Rw_Michell / (0.5 rho U^2 Aw), rho = 999.1; report CT for k in {0, 0.10, 0.25, 0.40} (the programme's swept box), the implied k that matches CT = 4.16e-3, and the same with the Ju wetted area Cs L (2D + B) against the exact Wigley surface.
- **Predictions (before running):** (1) with the programme's central form factor k = 0.25 the model CT is within 20% of 4.16e-3 (prior 55%; this is the H3 test in miniature, and H3 says the regression-style error exceeds 20%, which would here be a refutation of "Michell is fine"). (2) The implied k lies inside the swept box 0.10 to 0.40 (prior 60%; thin-ship theory is known to overpredict Cw near Fr 0.27 which would push implied k down, possibly below 0.10). (3) Michell Rw is greater than the experimental residual CR = CT - CF(1+0) share, i.e. over-predicts wave drag (prior 65%). Refuted: (1) |error| >= 20%; (2) implied k outside 0.10 to 0.40; (3) Michell below the residual.
- Limits known in advance: a single speed; secondary source; geometry assumed; no sinkage and trim; fresh-water temperature unknown.

### Outcome of milestone 3a (`experiments/run_wigley_tank.py`, under 1 second, deterministic; `experiments/results_wigley_tank.txt`)
- Check on the assumed geometry: the exact surface of the 4 m, 0.4 m, 0.25 m Wigley is 2.381 m2 and Ju's formula 0.661 L (2D + B) gives 2.380 m2, so the geometry is consistent with the quoted wetted area (now a test).
- **(1) refuted, by a small margin:** at the central form factor k = 0.25 the model CT is +22% above the tank value at 15 C (+25% at 10 C, +20% at 20 C, +18% at 25 C). The result is not robust to the unknown water temperature across the 20% line, but the sign is the same at every temperature: the programme's central hull constants over-predict this hull's resistance by about a fifth.
- **(2) refuted:** the form factor that makes the model match the tank is k = -0.03 at 15 C (-0.06 to +0.01 over 10 to 25 C), i.e. zero within the scatter of the unknown temperature, below the swept box 0.10 to 0.40. With k = 0 the model is within +1 to +5% at 10 to 20 C and -1% at 25 C. The Wigley is a thin, fine hull, for which a small form factor is the textbook expectation; the programme's 0.10 to 0.40 box is a value for fuller 4 to 5 m hulls and was never tested against a measurement.
- **(3) mixed:** Michell Rw gives Cw = 0.963e-3. The tank residual CT - CF is 0.778e-3 (10 C), 0.862e-3 (15 C), 0.936e-3 (20 C), 1.003e-3 (25 C), so Michell over-predicts the residual by 24%, 12% and 3% and under-predicts by 4%. At the nominal 15 C it is high, as predicted, but with one point and an unknown temperature this cannot be called a finding.
- Reading: first contact with a tank number. Michell plus the ITTC line with no form factor reproduces this one Wigley point to a few per cent; the programme's central k = 0.25 does not. For the Wigley hull the sweep box overstates resistance, so its absolute range figures for slender hulls are conservative by up to about 20% on this evidence, and the ranking conclusions (which compare hulls under the same k) are unaffected in sign. Form factor and wave term trade off at a single speed, so this does not validate Michell separately: one speed cannot separate them. A curve over Fr 0.2 to 0.4 could.
- Limits: as planned (one point, second-hand, geometry assumed but cross-checked by the wetted area, no sinkage or trim, water temperature unknown). H3 (Holtrop-Mennen) is untouched.
- Next: find the Wigley Cw/CT curve over Froude number (Ju 1983, Kajitani 1983 or the ITTC workshop data) in a reachable host; a fuller (non-Wigley) small hull for H3.

## 2026-10-30 · Milestone 6f: with a genuinely iid sea state, what does the persistence forecast capture, and how does it grow with spell length? (planned before running)
- Why: 6e's "Next". Its spell-1 cells are a deterministic alternation (hit rate 0.00) and must not be quoted. At p = 0.5 the `markov` chain with mean rough-spell 2 days has stay = enter = 0.5, which is iid, so spell 2 is the honest no-persistence baseline; spell 3 fills the curve between it and 7.
- Plan (`experiments/run_solar_seastate_persist_iid.py`, 6e's script with SPELLS = 2, 3, 7 days; same boat, sites, packs, p = 0.5, F = 3, seed 1): report E/B, hit rate and the share of the C-B gap E recovers.
- **Predictions (before running):** (1) at spell 2 (iid) the hit rate is within 0.03 of 0.50 in all 10 cells (prior 90%, binomial sd about 0.01 over 3650 days). (2) At spell 2 the median share of the C-B gap is at most 0.15 and E/B is under +1.5% in every cell (prior 60%; yesterday carries no information, so E can only gain by in-sample fitting of the pair). (3) The median share rises monotonically 2 -> 3 -> 7 days (prior 85%). Refuted: (1) any hit rate off by 0.03 or more; (2) median share above 0.15 or any E/B of +1.5% or more; (3) any non-monotone step.
- Limits known in advance: as 6e (p = 0.5, F = 3, one seed, in-sample pair, exact persistence, two-state chain).

### Outcome of milestone 6f (`experiments/run_solar_seastate_persist_iid.py`, 17 seconds, seeded; `experiments/results_solar_seastate_persist_iid.txt`; 30 cells)
- **(1) held:** every spell-2 (iid) cell has a hit rate of 0.50 to two decimals.
- **(2) held:** the median share of the C-B gap that persistence recovers at spell 2 is 0.06 (range 0.00 to 0.10) and the largest E/B is +0.6% (Equator and Florida, 1 kWh pack); 0 of 30 cells have E below B. The small positive part is in-sample fitting of the speed pair, not forecast skill (hit rate 0.50).
- **(3) held:** median share 0.06 (spell 2, hit 0.50) -> 0.28 (spell 3, hit 0.67; range 0.18 to 0.39) -> 0.75 (spell 7, hit 0.85; range 0.68 to 0.80). The 7-day cells reproduce 6e exactly.
- Reading: replaces 6e's artifact cells. Persistence is worthless on an iid sea, recovers about a quarter of the oracle gain at 3-day spells and three quarters at 7-day spells, so whether a boat's own wave sensor suffices depends on how long rough spells last, which this programme cannot source (no climatology reachable). Quote the spell-2 row as the no-information baseline; ignore 6e's spell-1 rows.
- Limits: as planned; p = 0.5, F = 3, one seed, in-sample pair, exact persistence, two-state chain.
- Next: a three-state chain or real wave-height series if one becomes reachable; milestone 3 tank data remains blocked.

## 2026-10-30 · Milestone 6e: does a no-parameter persistence forecast (tomorrow = today) capture the oracle back-off gain? (planned before reading the output)
- Why: 6d's skill s was a free parameter tied to no real product. Persistence needs none: the call for day t is the true state of day t-1. Its hit rate is set by the spell length, so it is the realistic floor a boat with no external forecast (only its own wave sensor) could use.
- Plan (`experiments/run_solar_seastate_persist.py`, 6d's script with the skill sweep replaced; same boat, sites, p = 0.5, F = 3, spells 1 and 7 days, packs 10 and 1 kWh, seed 1): policy E uses the pair (v_calm, v_rough) chosen in-sample on persistence calls. Report E/B, hit rate and the share of the C minus B gap E recovers. The script was executed once before these predictions were written, but its output had not been read.
- **Predictions:** (1) at spell 7 days E recovers at least 0.6 of the C-B gap in at least 14 of 20... corrected: in at least 7 of the 10 spell-7 cells (prior 65%: hit rate about 1 - 2/7 = 0.71 at p = 0.5, which 6d put near s = 0.4 to 0.5, so I expect only 0.3 to 0.5, but persistence errors are one-day lags rather than random, which costs less; I lean on 6d's s = 0.5 share of 0.35 as the central guess and put 0.6 as the refutable line). (2) At spell 1 day E is below B in at least 5 of 10 cells (prior 60%: a single-day spell makes yesterday anti-informative, hit rate about 0 to 0.1, and the pair could invert but cannot go below B unless the in-sample pair excludes constants; the pair grid contains the constants, so E >= B always: prediction restated as E within 0.5% of B in every spell-1 cell, prior 70%). Refuted: (1) fewer than 7 of 10; (2) any spell-1 cell off by 0.5% or more.
- Limits known in advance: Markov days with p and F swept; one seed; the pair is chosen in-sample; no real wave-sensor lag.

### Outcome of milestone 6e (`experiments/run_solar_seastate_persist.py`, 10 seconds, seeded; `experiments/results_solar_seastate_persist.txt`; 20 cells)
- **(1) held:** at a 7-day spell persistence recovers 0.68 to 0.80 of the C-B gap in all 10 cells (median 0.75), above the 0.6 line, so my central guess of 0.3 to 0.5 was too low: E/B is +3.6 to +6.4% with the 10 kWh pack and +4.1 to +11.4% with the 1 kWh pack. My arithmetic for the hit rate was also wrong: with p = 0.5 and 7-day spells in both states the day-to-day flip chance is 1/7, so the hit rate is 0.85, not 0.71. Rough-spell persistence is what makes a sensor-only boat nearly as good as the oracle, and no forecast skill parameter is needed.
- **(2) refuted, and the cause is a model artifact:** at spell 1 day E equals C (share 1.00 in all 10 cells, E/B up to +5.5%) while the hit rate is 0.00. With p = 0.5 the chain `markov` builds for spell 1 is a deterministic alternation (stay = 0, enter = 1), so yesterday's state predicts today's perfectly inverted and the in-sample pair simply swaps the two speeds. This is not a result about real seas: the spell-1 cells in 6c and 6d are a deterministic day/night-style cycle, and read as an iid baseline they overstate how predictable the weather is. It also means E >= B everywhere only because the pair grid contains the constants (0 of 20 cells below B).
- Reading: for 7-day spells, a boat that only reads yesterday's sea recovers about three quarters of the perfect-foresight gain (hit rate 0.85), which is more than 6d's s = 0.75 curve (0.58) suggests because persistence errors fall on spell boundaries, where being wrong costs one day, while 6d's errors were independent. Only the 7-day cells should be quoted; the 1-day cells measure the artifact.
- Limits: p = 0.5 and F = 3 only; the spell length is swept; one seed; in-sample pair; persistence is exact (no sensor noise or delay); real sea states are not a two-state chain.
- Next: replace the two-state chain with a three-state one or a real wave-height series if a climatology becomes reachable (milestone 3 tank data remains blocked); fix the spell-1 chain to be iid (mean spell 2 at p = 0.5) before quoting any spell-1 figure.

## 2026-10-30 · Milestone 6d: how much of the oracle back-off gain survives a forecast of finite skill? (planned before running)
- Why: 6c's "Next". C knows each day's sea state exactly, so C/B (3 to 15%) is an upper bound. A real forecast is partly right. Skill is swept, not sourced: no forecast-verification data for small-craft sea state is reachable through the proxy.
- Plan (`experiments/run_solar_seastate_forecast.py`, 6c's boat, sites, F = 3, p = 0.5, spell 1 and 7 days, packs 10 and 1 kWh): policy D picks its speed from a forecast that equals the true state with probability s and otherwise is a climatological draw (rough with probability p); energy use follows the true state. s in {0, 0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 1}; (v_calm, v_rough) chosen in-sample for each s (D at s = 1 is C). Report the share of the C minus B gap that D recovers.
- **Predictions (before running):** (1) at s = 0 D is within 0.3% of B in every cell (prior 80%; the pair grid contains the constant speeds, so D(0) >= B, and a random forecast gives nothing to exploit). (2) At s = 0.5 D recovers at least half of the C-B gap in at least 15 of the 20 cells (prior 55%: the gain should be roughly linear in s, a concave curve would push it above). Refuted: (1) any cell off by 0.3% or more; (2) fewer than 15 cells.
- Limits known in advance: errors are independent across days (real forecast errors cluster); one seed for the truth and one for the forecast; the pair is chosen in-sample; skill is a free parameter.

### Outcome of milestone 6d (`experiments/run_solar_seastate_forecast.py`, about 8 minutes, seeded; `experiments/results_solar_seastate_forecast.txt`; 20 cells)
- **(1) refuted, narrowly:** the largest |D(0) - B| / B is 0.325%, against the 0.3% line (median share of the gap recovered at s = 0 is 0.00, maximum 0.12 of a gap that is itself under 4 km/day). D(0) never falls below B.
- **(2) refuted, clearly:** at s = 0.5 only 1 of 20 cells recovers half the C-B gap (median share 0.35, range 0.08 to 0.50). The curve is convex, not linear: median share 0.11 at s = 0.25, 0.35 at 0.5, 0.58 at 0.75, 1.00 at 1. With p = 0.5 the forecast's hit rate is s + (1 - s)/2, so s = 0.5 is already 75% of days called correctly, and it buys about a third of the oracle's gain.
- Reading: the oracle gain of 3 to 15% (6c) is mostly unreachable by a mediocre forecast. A forecast right on 75% of days recovers about a third of it (roughly 1 to 4% of km/day); it takes about 87% correct (s = 0.75) to recover more than half. The best constant speed B stays the sensible default unless a forecast of sea state at that skill exists.
- Limits: as planned. Independent forecast errors, one seed each for truth and forecast, in-sample pair, p = 0.5 and F = 3 only, and s is not tied to any real forecast product.
- Next: tie s to a real forecast verification figure if one becomes reachable; milestone 3 tank data remains blocked.

## 2026-10-29 · Milestone 6c: do persistent rough spells and a small pack change what a back-off policy gains over the best constant speed? (planned before running)
- Why: 6b's "Next" and its stated caveat: iid rough days understate spells, and a 10 kWh pack (25% of a 200 kg boat at 200 Wh/kg) buffers a bad week. No climatology is reachable, so spell length is swept, not sourced. Not a prediction of any real sea.
- Plan (`experiments/run_solar_seastate_spells.py`, same boat, five sites, daily NASA POWER series): rough/calm two-state Markov chain with stationary rough share p in {0.3, 0.5} and mean rough-spell length in {1 (iid), 3, 7} days; F = 3; pack 10 kWh and 1 kWh (the 1 kWh pack cannot ride out a week). Policies A, B, C as in 6b (C in-sample oracle on each day's state). Report C/B and B/A per cell.
- **Predictions (before running):** (1) with the 10 kWh pack, the C/B gain at a 7-day spell is within 1.5 points of the iid gain in every cell (prior 55%): the pack buffers spells. (2) With the 1 kWh pack, C/B at a 7-day spell exceeds the iid C/B by at least 1 point at the Equator and Florida at p = 0.5 (prior 50%). (3) The site ordering holds under every policy and cell (prior 90%). Refuted: (1) any cell off by more than 1.5; (2) either site under 1 point; (3) any reorder.
- Limits known in advance: C knows the state of each day exactly (no forecast skill); one seed; F and p and spell length are swept, not sourced; no added-resistance curve.

### Outcome of milestone 6c (`experiments/run_solar_seastate_spells.py`, about 4 minutes, seeded; `experiments/results_solar_seastate_spells.txt`)
- Correction to the plan: the chain with mean spell length 1 is single-day spells (rough days never follow rough days), not iid; iid at p = 0.5 would be a 2-day mean spell. The comparisons below use spell = 1 as the baseline, and 6b's iid numbers sit between spell 1 and 3.
- **(1) refuted:** with the 10 kWh pack the C/B gain still rises with spell length, by up to 2.8 points from 1 to 7 days (Equator p = 0.5: 5.4% to 8.2%; p = 0.3: 5.2% to 7.3%), so the pack does not fully buffer a week of rough water.
- **(2) held:** with the 1 kWh pack and p = 0.5, the 7-day gain exceeds the 1-day gain by 9.4 points at the Equator (5.5% to 14.9%) and 7.1 at Florida (3.7% to 10.8%).
- **(3) held:** the site ordering holds under A, B and C in every cell.
- Reading: persistence helps the day-by-day back-off and hurts the constant speed that was tuned to the mixture, because the one speed must trade off long calm spells against long rough ones. The pack is the buffer: with 10 kWh the back-off gain is 3 to 8%, with 1 kWh it reaches 15% where sun is plentiful. B/A shrinks with spell length (Equator p = 0.5: 15.3% to 10.8% with the big pack), so the best constant speed matters less and a regime-aware policy more.
- Limits: as planned. C has perfect knowledge of each day's state; a real forecast would capture only part of the gain; spell length, p and F are swept, not sourced; one seed; the Markov chain has no seasonality.
- Next: a forecast of finite skill on the Markov chain (the upper bound C becomes a curve in skill); milestone 3 tank data remains blocked.

## 2026-10-29 · Milestone 6b: if rough days are intermittent, how much does a speed policy that backs off on rough days gain over the best constant speed? (planned before running)
- Why: 6a's "Next". 6a showed that keeping the calm best speed in a constant f = 2 sea loses 12 to 27%, but real seas alternate. No wave climatology is reachable (data hosts refused by the proxy, as in 5m), so the step sweeps the share of rough days p and the rough multiplier F rather than reporting a site's sea. Not a prediction of any real sea.
- Plan (`experiments/run_solar_seastate_policy.py`, same 5 m boat, hotel 10 W, eta_pv 0.20, derate 0.85, five sites, the daily NASA POWER series): each day is rough with probability p (seeded iid draws, seed 1, same draws for all policies), f = F on rough days and 1 on calm days; p in {0.1, 0.3, 0.5}, F in {2, 3}. Policies, speeds on a 0.5 to 2.0 m/s grid: (A) the calm-sea best constant speed; (B) the best constant speed for the mixture; (C) an oracle that knows each day's f and uses the best pair (v_calm, v_rough), chosen in-sample. Report km/day of each and the gains C/B and B/A.
- **Predictions (before running):** (1) C beats B by under 3% in every cell (prior 55%): the km/day-versus-speed curve is flat near its optimum (5j). (2) A loses at least 5% to B in the p = 0.5, F = 3 cells at every site (prior 60%). (3) The site ordering holds under every policy and cell (prior 90%). Refuted: (1) any cell at 3% or more; (2) any site under 5%; (3) any reorder.
- Limits known in advance: iid days (real rough spells persist, and a pack carries energy across them, which favours C); C is in-sample and has perfect foresight, so it is an upper bound on a forecast-driven policy; f is a free multiplier; one decade, one cell per site.

### Outcome of milestone 6b (`experiments/run_solar_seastate_policy.py`, about 1 minute, seeded; `experiments/results_solar_seastate_policy.txt`)
- **(1) refuted:** the oracle pair (C) beats the best constant speed (B) by 3% or more in 9 of 30 cells and by up to 6.8% (Equator, p = 0.5, F = 3). Elsewhere 0.4 to 2.6% at moderate p; at p = 0.1 it is 0.4 to 2.4%. Gains grow with p, F and sunshine (largest at the Equator and Florida, smallest at the Gulf of Alaska, 0.6 to 3.9%).
- **(2) held:** at p = 0.5, F = 3 the mixture-tuned constant speed beats the calm-best one by +13.0% (Equator), +14.5% (Florida), +13.7% (Gulf of Maine), +9.0% (North Sea), +5.4% (Gulf of Alaska); the Alaska case is 0.4 points above the line. At p = 0.1 the calm-best speed is within 0.8% of B in every cell.
- **(3) held:** the site ordering holds under all three policies in every cell.
- Reading: if rough days are 10% of days, ignoring them costs under 1%; at half the days with a tripled resistance, re-tuning the one cruise speed recovers 5 to 15%, and a day-by-day back-off adds up to 7% more, mostly where sun is plentiful. The caveat that matters: C is in-sample with perfect foresight and iid days, so 6.8% is an upper bound on a forecast-driven policy; persistent rough spells draining a pack would favour backing off more, and this run cannot size that.
- Limits: as planned. F and p are swept, not sourced; the wave climatology is still missing.
- Next: a persistence model (Markov rough spells) with a forecast of finite skill, if a climatology can be sourced; otherwise milestone 3 tank data.

## 2026-10-29 · Milestone 6a: how much does a sea-state resistance multiplier cost the deck-solar boat in km/day, and does it move the best speed or the site ordering? (planned before running)
- Why: milestone 6 (sea states) is the largest effect the programme has not touched, and the draft's `paper/DRAFT.md` says there is no sea-state loss. Added resistance in waves for a 4 to 5 m hull has no published curve I could cite here (strip-theory and Kwon-type speed-loss formulas are fitted to ships far above this size), so I do not invent one: the step sweeps a multiplier `f` on the hull resistance (friction, form and wave terms together) and reports where the conclusions break. This is a sweep because nothing sourced exists, not a prediction of any real sea.
- Plan (`experiments/run_solar_seastate.py`, same boat and daily NASA POWER series as 5m, 4 m and 5 m, five sites): power-side inputs at the 5m medians (hotel 10 W, eta_pv 0.20, derate 0.85 after 5l/5m ranges), hull at the central constants; `f` in {1.0, 1.25, 1.5, 2.0, 3.0}; best constant speed (0.5 to 2.0 m/s) and km/day per cell; the ratio to f = 1; the speed that is best at f = 1 run at f = 2 (regret).
- **Predictions (before running):** (1) at 5 m, km/day at f = 2 is 55 to 80% of f = 1 at every site (prior 60%). (2) the best speed falls by at least 0.2 m/s from f = 1 to f = 2 at every 5 m site (prior 55%). (3) the site ordering holds at every f (prior 90%). Refuted: (1) any site outside 55 to 80%; (2) any 5 m site shifts under 0.2; (3) any reorder.
- Limits known in advance: `f` is a free multiplier, not a sea state; it is constant over the decade although real seas vary by season and site (so the high-latitude sites, which have the worst seas and the least sun, would lose more than this treats them); no added mass, slamming, heave-induced propeller ventilation or drift.

### Outcome of milestone 6a (`experiments/run_solar_seastate.py`, a few seconds, deterministic; `experiments/results_solar_seastate.txt`)
- **(1) held, at the edge:** 5 m km/day at f = 2 is 79% of f = 1 at all five sites (80% is the refutation line; the 4 m boat is also 79%). f = 1.5 gives 87%, f = 3 gives 68 to 69%. The loss is almost site-independent because every site is drag-limited once the multiplier is applied.
- **(2) held:** the 5 m best speed falls 0.3 m/s (Equator, Florida, Gulf of Maine), 0.2 (North Sea, Gulf of Alaska) from f = 1 to f = 2; 1.6 to 1.3, 1.6 to 1.3, 1.5 to 1.2, 1.4 to 1.2, 1.3 to 1.1.
- **(3) held:** the site ordering holds at every f in both boats.
- **New, not predicted:** keeping the calm-water best speed in a rougher sea costs a lot: running the f = 1 speed at f = 2 loses 12.1% (5 m Gulf of Alaska) to 27.1% (5 m Equator) of the km/day the boat could make at its own best speed, 16 to 28% for the 4 m boat. That is 2 to 3 times the hull-constant regret of 5j (up to 9%), so a speed policy that backs off in rough water is worth more than any of the hull-constant choices. It is largest where the sun is plentiful because there the calm-water best speed is highest.
- Reading: a resistance multiplier of 2 costs about 21% of the km/day and lowers the best cruise speed by 0.2 to 0.3 m/s, and does not reorder the sites; the shape of these conclusions survives, the absolute numbers fall. This says nothing about which f a real sea gives.
- Limits: as planned. f is a free multiplier, constant over time and across sites; real high-latitude seas would raise f exactly at the sites with the least sun, so the true site ordering could change and this run cannot say; a reported f would need a sea-state climatology (wave-height series, not fetched here) and an added-resistance curve for a 4 to 5 m hull.
- Next: source a wave climatology for the five cells and a small-hull added-resistance relation (tank data, same blocker as milestone 3), then replace the constant f by a seasonal one; test a policy that cuts speed when the wave height rises.

## 2026-10-29 · Milestone 5m: what interval does km/day carry when every power-side input is drawn jointly from its range? (planned before running)
- Why: 5k/5l varied inputs one or two at a time; 5l's "Next" asked for a sourced derate. NASA POWER (`power.larc.nasa.gov`) was refused by the session proxy, so no new temperature series could be fetched and the derate cannot be rebuilt from site temperatures here. Instead the derate is split into factors with ranges and drawn jointly. Ranges (the first two from 5l's snippet-level sources, PDFs unopened; the rest my choices, not sourced): hotel 5 to 20 W (Wave Glider sheet average), eta_pv 0.17 to 0.22, temperature factor 0.93 to 1.00 (coefficient -0.29 %/K from the Maxeon datasheet snippet times 0 to 25 K above 25 C cell temperature), soiling and spray 0.90 to 0.99, mismatch, wiring and MPPT 0.92 to 0.97. Hull constants at the 5k centre.
- Plan (`experiments/run_solar_mc.py`, 5 m and 4 m, five sites, 500 seeded joint uniform draws, seed 1): per draw the best constant speed (0.5 to 2.0 m/s grid) and km/day. Report median and 5th to 95th percentile of km/day and best speed per site, and the share of draws where the site ordering (Equator > Florida > Gulf of Maine > North Sea > Gulf of Alaska) holds.
- **Predictions (before running):** (1) the 5 to 95% km/day band is within +-25% of the median at every 5 m site (prior 60%). (2) The 5 m median best speed is 1.1 to 1.6 m/s at every site (prior 80%). (3) The site ordering holds in at least 95% of draws at 5 m (prior 85%). Refuted: (1) any band edge beyond 25%; (2) any median outside 1.1 to 1.6; (3) under 95%.
- Limits known in advance: uniform draws over ranges I picked (no source gives a distribution); the unsourced factors dominate the spread; the hull constants are fixed; daily steps; one cell per site.

### Outcome of milestone 5m (`experiments/run_solar_mc.py`, 2 min, seeded; `experiments/results_solar_mc.txt`)
- **(1) held:** the 5 to 95% km/day band is -7/+6% (Equator) to -10/+9% (Gulf of Alaska) of the median in the 5 m boat; the 4 m boat is wider, up to -15/+13% (Gulf of Alaska).
- **(2) held, at the edge:** 5 m median best speed 1.6 (Equator, Florida), 1.5 (Maine), 1.4 (North Sea), 1.3 (Alaska) m/s, inside 1.1 to 1.6. Medians are higher than 5l's because the drawn hotel (5 to 20 W) is below the 5k centre.
- **(3) held:** the site ordering holds in 500 of 500 draws in both boats.
- Reading: with the power-side inputs drawn jointly from published-ish ranges, 5 m km/day is about 133 / 125 / 97 / 77 / 69 km/day (Equator to Alaska), each known to roughly +-10%. The joint interval is narrower than the one-at-a-time swings of 5k suggest, because the draws are interior to the ranges and the ends rarely coincide. It says nothing about the hull constants (5j: another +-10%) or the unsourced wave term.
- Limits: as planned. Uniform draws over ranges I picked; the temperature, soiling and electrical factors are my ranges, not sourced; the temperature series could not be fetched (NASA POWER refused by the proxy), so site-specific cell temperatures are not modelled; the 5l sources were not opened.
- Next: open the Wave Glider and module datasheets for citation; hull constants still need milestone 3 (tank data).

## 2026-10-29 · Milestone 5l: does the best cruise speed and km/day survive swapping the unsourced hotel load and panel efficiency for published figures? (planned before running)
- Why: 5k's "Next": hotel load and panel yield dominate km/day. Sources found this session (search snippets only; the vendor PDF host was not reachable from the session, so the PDFs were NOT opened, and each needs opening before it goes in a paper): Liquid Robotics/Boeing Wave Glider product sheet, "average continuous power 5 W to 20 W"; a Renewable Energy 2022 simulation paper (ideas.repec.org/a/eee/renene/v188y2022icp911-921.html), about 10 W continuous available to payloads; SunPower Maxeon 3 rigid module datasheets, module efficiency 22.1 to 22.6% and power temperature coefficient -0.29 %/degC, cell 23.1 to 24.3% (enfsolar cell datasheet 1740). A rigid module is an upper end for a curved deck, so eta_pv 0.22 is an optimistic anchor and 0.17 stays as the flexible/mismatch case. The derate (soiling, wiring, mismatch, temperature) stays unsourced except temperature: 25 K above 25 degC at -0.29 %/K is -7.3%, so derate 0.85 is not contradicted but not measured either.
- Plan (`experiments/run_solar_sourced.py`, same boat, daily NASA POWER series, 4 m and 5 m, five sites, no source changes): hotel in {5, 10, 20, 30} W x eta_pv in {0.17, 0.22}, derate 0.85, hull at the central constants. Report best speed and km/day per cell, and the ratio against the 5k centre (30 W, 0.20).
- **Predictions (before running):** (1) at a sourced hotel of 10 W the 5 m best speed is 0.3 m/s or more lower than at 30 W at the energy-limited sites (Gulf of Maine, North Sea, Gulf of Alaska) (prior 55%): less fixed load shifts the best-range speed down. (2) km/day at 10 W and eta 0.22 is at least 1.5 times the 5k centre at those three sites in the 5 m boat (prior 60%). (3) The site ordering (Equator > Florida > Gulf of Maine > North Sea > Gulf of Alaska) is unchanged in every cell (prior 85%). Refuted: (1) any of the three sites shifts under 0.3; (2) any ratio under 1.5; (3) any reorder.
- Limits known in advance: the 5 to 20 W figure is a Wave Glider average over a mission, not this boat's propulsion-excluded hotel load, and includes sleeping payloads; a drive computer and a motor controller sit on top; rigid module efficiency is not a curved-deck figure; unopened PDFs.

### Outcome of milestone 5l (`experiments/run_solar_sourced.py`, a few seconds, deterministic; `experiments/results_solar_sourced.txt`)
- **(1) refuted, and my sign was wrong.** I wrote that less fixed load would lower the best speed; it raises it (a lower hotel load makes the same speed cheaper relative to harvest). Going from 30 W to 10 W (eta 0.17) shifts the 5 m best speed by +0.1 (Gulf of Maine), +0.2 (North Sea) and +0.2 (Gulf of Alaska) m/s, under the 0.3 line in magnitude too.
- **(2) refuted:** km/day at 10 W and eta 0.22 is 1.21x (Gulf of Maine), 1.23x (North Sea) and 1.28x (Gulf of Alaska) the 5k centre in the 5 m boat, under 1.5x. The 4 m boat gains more (North Sea 1.47x, Gulf of Alaska 1.57x) because its harvest is smaller relative to its load.
- **(3) held:** the site ordering is unchanged in all 16 cells (hotel x eta x boat length).
- Reading: swapping the unsourced hotel load for the Wave Glider's published 5 to 20 W average, and eta for a rigid-module 0.22, raises 5 m km/day at the three energy-limited sites by 10 to 28% at 10 W (eta 0.17 to 0.22) against 5k's centre and moves the best speed by at most 0.2 m/s. The 5k centre (30 W, 0.20) was pessimistic against the sourced figures, so the absolute km/day in earlier milestones are conservative, not optimistic, on those two inputs. The best speed stays 1.1 to 1.5 m/s, so 5i's "cruise at 1.1 to 1.5, not 1.0" survives sourcing.
- Limits: as planned; the sourced hotel figure is a different vehicle's mission average, not this boat's, and its PDFs were not opened here; rigid-module efficiency is an upper end for a curved deck; the derate stays unsourced; the wave constants are still unsourced (milestone 3).
- Next: a sourced derate (soiling, mismatch, temperature) and a propulsion-excluded hotel load for a small USV (e.g. a published drive-computer and radio budget); milestone 3 for the hull.

## 2026-10-29 · Milestone 5k: which of the six inputs moves the best cruise speed and the km/day most? (planned before running)
- Why: 5j's "Next". 5j varied only the hull constants and found they matter more than the speed policy; the hotel load, panel efficiency and derate are unsourced too. Plan (`experiments/run_solar_sensitivity.py`, same boat, daily NASA POWER series, 4 m and 5 m, five sites, no source changes): one-at-a-time swing of each input from the central boat (c_s 3.0, k_form 0.25, wave 1, hotel 30 W, eta_pv 0.20, derate 0.85) to a low and a high value: c_s 2.6/3.4, k_form 0.10/0.40, wave scale 0.5/1.0 (above 1 untested), hotel 20/45 W (about -33%/+50%), eta_pv 0.17/0.23, derate 0.75/0.95. For each: best constant speed (0.5 to 2.0 m/s, 0.1 steps) and km/day at that speed. Rank inputs by the km/day swing (high minus low, % of central) and by the best-speed shift.
- **Predictions (before running):** (1) the hotel load is the largest km/day swing at the energy-limited sites (Gulf of Maine, North Sea, Gulf of Alaska) in the 5 m boat (prior 65%). (2) At the Equator the largest km/day swing is a hull constant (c_s or k_form), not a power-side input (prior 75%): the pack is never the limit, so speed is set by drag. (3) Eta_pv and derate give identical swings per percent (they enter as a product) and the 5 m best speed moves under 0.2 m/s across their range at every site (prior 60%). Refuted: (1) a different input tops any of the three sites; (2) a power-side input tops the Equator; (3) any best-speed shift of 0.2 m/s or more.
- Limits known in advance: the ranges are my choice, not measurements (so the ranking is conditional on them, and I say which are wider); one-at-a-time ignores interactions; wave scale low value 0.5 is a shrink only; daily steps; one cell per site.

### Outcome of milestone 5k (`experiments/run_solar_sensitivity.py`, a few seconds, deterministic; `experiments/results_solar_sensitivity.txt`)
- Disclosure: I reworded the wave-scale phrase in the plan after the run because it was garbled (the script's 0.5 to 1.0 range was already what ran; predictions untouched).
- **(1) held:** hotel load has the largest km/day swing in the 5 m boat at Gulf of Maine (-21.8% for 30 to 45 W against 20 W), North Sea (-25.8%) and Gulf of Alaska (-30.2%).
- **(2) refuted:** hotel also tops the Equator (5 m: -19.9%; 4 m: -38.2%), not a hull constant. My reasoning that an unconstrained pack leaves drag in charge ignored that at 1.0 to 1.5 m/s the 30 W hotel is still a large share of the load, and the km/day swing at the Equator comes from the speed the boat can afford, which scales with surplus power.
- **(3) refuted by one case:** eta_pv and derate act as one product (the per-percent swings agree to about 10%: e.g. 5 m Equator +16.2% for +/-15% in eta_pv against +13.4% for +/-12% in derate), but the 5 m best speed shifts by 0.2 m/s at Gulf of Alaska (1.1 to 1.4 across the eta_pv range), meeting the refutation line. Everywhere else the shift is 0.1 m/s.
- **Ranking, all ten cases:** hotel (19 to 50%) > eta_pv (16 to 30%) > derate (12 to 23%) > c_s (8.6 to 9.3%) > k_form (7.9 to 8.4%) > wave scale (0.3 to 1.2%). The ranges are my choices, so the order of hotel against eta_pv depends on them; the gap between the power-side inputs and the hull constants is 2 to 5 times in every case and does not depend on which ends of the ranges I picked within a factor of two.
- **The best speed is robust; km/day is not.** Every input moves the best speed by 0.0 to 0.2 m/s, yet moves km/day by up to 50%. That agrees with 5j (hull constants move the best speed by 0.3 m/s and km/day by about 10%) and sharpens it: the thing to measure first is the vehicle's hotel load and panel yield, not its hull. The wave term is nearly irrelevant to km/day at this speed range, so the Michell scale worry from 5i/5j matters little for the energy answer.
- Limits: ranges chosen, not measured (hotel 20 to 45 W is asymmetric about 30 W); one-at-a-time, no interactions; 0.1 m/s speed grid, so shifts of 0.1 are at the resolution; daily steps; one cell per site; wave scale only shrunk.
- Next: source the hotel load (a published USV or AUV payload and computer budget) and the panel yield (a datasheet module efficiency plus a soiling and temperature derate), as those now dominate; milestone 3 remains blocked on tank data but matters less for km/day than 5j suggested.

## 2026-10-29 · Milestone 5j: does the best constant speed of a deck-solar boat survive the unsourced hull constants? (planned before running)
- Why: 5i's "Next" and the open doubt that every absolute speed rests on `c_s`, `k_form` and the wave scale, none sourced (milestone 3 is blocked on tank data). Plan (`experiments/run_solar_hull_uncertainty.py`, `solar.py` and `hull.py` unchanged): the 5i boat (30 W hotel, eta_pv 0.20, derate 0.85, 200 kg, 10 kWh pack), 4 m and 5 m, five sites, all 3,653 days of NASA POWER daily data. Hull box = the milestone-1 box: c_s {2.6, 3.0, 3.4} x k_form {0.10, 0.25, 0.40} x wave scale {0, 0.5, 1} = 27 hulls. For each hull: best constant speed on a 0.5 to 2.0 m/s grid (0.1 steps) and its km/day. Then: (a) spread of the best speed across the 27 hulls; (b) regret of running the central-hull best speed (c_s 3.0, k 0.25, wave 1) on each other hull, as % of that hull's own best; (c) the single speed that minimises worst-case regret over the box and its worst regret.
- **Predictions (before running):** (1) the best speed spans at least 0.3 m/s across the box in the 5 m boat at 3 or more of 5 sites (prior 70%). (2) Running the central-hull best speed costs under 5% on every hull in the box, every site and boat (prior 75%): the km/day-versus-speed curve is flat near its optimum. (3) The worst-case regret of the minimax speed is under 3% in every site-boat case (prior 70%). Refuted: (1) fewer than 3 sites; (2) any case at 5% or more; (3) any case at 3% or more.
- Limits known in advance: the box is the same unsourced range as milestone 1, not a measurement; wave scale 0 removes wave-making entirely, which is unphysical above Fr 0.25; speeds above 1.5 m/s sit where Michell scale 1 is least trustworthy (5i); daily steps; one cell per site, one decade; no round-trip loss or depth of discharge.

### Outcome of milestone 5j (`experiments/run_solar_hull_uncertainty.py`, 4 s, deterministic; `experiments/results_solar_hull_uncertainty.txt`)
- **(1) held:** the best constant speed spans 0.3 m/s across the 27 hulls in the 5 m boat at 4 of 5 sites (1.3 to 1.6 Equator, 1.4 to 1.7 Florida, 1.3 to 1.6 Gulf of Maine, 1.2 to 1.5 North Sea; 1.2 to 1.4 Gulf of Alaska) and 0.2 to 0.3 m/s in the 4 m boat (1.0 to 1.3). The hull box moves the best speed by about as much as 5i's whole "best constant vs 1.0 m/s" correction.
- **(2) refuted:** running the central hull's best speed on another hull in the box costs 5% or more in 3 of 10 cases: 9.1% (5 m Equator), 5.8% (4 m Equator), 5.5% (5 m Florida). Elsewhere 1.4 to 4.9%. The loss is largest where the pack is never the limit (Equator, Florida), because there the boat simply cruises faster the less drag it has, and the box's drag range is wide.
- **(3) refuted:** the minimax speed (the one speed with the least worst-case loss over the box) still has worst-case regret of 3% or more in 6 of 10 cases, worst 6.5% (5 m Equator, 1.5 m/s). At the energy-limited high-latitude sites the central speed is already minimax (regret 1.4 to 4.9%).
- Absolute range moves far more than the best speed: at each hull's own best speed the 5 m Equator boat covers 109 to 133 km/day, Gulf of Maine 78 to 95, North Sea 61 to 74. So the unsourced hull box is worth about +-10% on km/day; the ordering of sites is unaffected, the absolute km/day is not trustworthy to better than that until milestone 3.
- Reading: "cruise at 1.1 to 1.5 m/s, not 1.0" (5i) is robust in direction; the exact best speed is not known to better than 0.3 m/s without a sourced hull. Both 5i's surge gain (under 3%) and the hull box's regret (up to 9%) say the same thing: the hull constants matter more than the speed policy.
- Limits: as planned. The box is the milestone-1 range, not a measurement; wave scale 0 is unphysical at Fr above 0.25, so the Equator/Florida 5 m worst cases lean on the optimistic corner of the box; no joint sweep with hotel load or panel efficiency.
- Next: milestone 3 (Holtrop-Mennen or a published small-hull curve) to narrow the box; a sensitivity ranking of the six inputs (hull constants, hotel, eta_pv, derate) on best speed.

## 2026-10-29 · Milestone 5i: is 5h's surge gain a property of the rule, or of the best constant speed it was compared with? (log written after the one run; no prediction was committed before it)
- Why: 5h's "Next" and a doubt about its own comparison: it reported the surge rule against *fixed 1.0 m/s*, but the best constant speed was already 1.1 to 1.4 m/s. Plan (`experiments/run_solar_surge_holdout.py`, same boat, pack and daily NASA POWER series, 4 m and 5 m, five sites, no source changes): split the decade into A (2015 to 2019) and B (2020 to 2024); on one half fit the best constant speed (0.6 to 1.7 m/s) and the best surge rule (base 0.8 or 1.0, fast 1.2 to 2.8 m/s, level 30 to 99%; 5h's grid stopped at 1.6 and 70%); score both on the other half, each starting from a full pack; swap halves (20 fits).
- Disclosure: no predictions were committed first. My unrecorded expectation was that a held-out surge rule would still beat the best constant speed by several percent in the 5 m boat.

### Outcome of milestone 5i (`experiments/run_solar_surge_holdout.py`, a few seconds, deterministic; `experiments/results_solar_surge_holdout.txt`)
- **Most of 5h's "surge gain" was the constant speed, not the rule.** Held out, the surge rule beats the best constant speed fitted on the same half by under 3% in all 20 fits (0 of 20 reach +3%; best +3.0%, rounded, at 5 m Gulf of Maine in both directions). The 4 m boat gain is -0.3 to +0.8%, i.e. nothing. At 5 m: +2.1/+1.9% Florida, +3.0/+3.0% Gulf of Maine, +2.9/+2.6% North Sea, +1.3/+1.6% Gulf of Alaska, about 0% at the Equator (where the pack is never the limit).
- Against fixed 1.0 m/s the gains are large (5 m: 86.4 to 116 to 119 km/day at the Equator, 73 to 88 at Gulf of Maine) but nearly all of it is just cruising at 1.3 to 1.5 m/s. 5h's +20/+17/+11/+32% should be read as "about 17/14/9/30 points from the faster constant speed, the rest from surging".
- **Out of sample, the rule transfers:** regret against a same-half oracle rule is 0.0 to 0.6% in every fit; the fitted parameters are the same in both halves for most cases (5 m Gulf of Maine: base 1.0, fast 1.6 above 70% both ways). The 5 m rules at Florida and Maine have a fast speed (1.6 m/s) well inside the widened 1.2 to 2.8 grid, so 5h's edge worry does not apply there; the `[edge]` flags in the output are mostly cases where the fitted rule is degenerate (fast speed 1.2 m/s, or a level of 30% or 99%) and reduces to a constant speed. 6 of 20 fits are fully interior.
- Reading: the useful policy for a deck-solar boat is "pick the right constant speed (1.1 to 1.5 m/s here, not 1.0)"; a pack-level surge rule adds at most about 3% on top, and only in a 5 m boat above 25 N. It is a small refinement, not a headline. That corrects the emphasis of 5h.
- Limits: daily steps; two five-year halves of one decade at one 1-degree cell per site; the constant speed and rule are fitted by grid search; no round-trip loss, depth-of-discharge limit or forecast; wave-making at 1.5+ m/s (Fr about 0.3 at 5 m) is only approximated by the Michell scale 1 term, so the speeds above 1.4 m/s are the least trustworthy part of the result; a 3% margin is inside the model-form uncertainty of the hull constants.
- Next: milestone 3 (Holtrop-Mennen against tank data) is still the blocker for trusting absolute speeds; the hull-constant uncertainty should be propagated through the best-constant-speed result.

## 2026-10-28 · Milestone 5h: is 5g's "slowing never helps" a property of the thresholds I picked, or of the boat? (planned before running)
- Why: 5g's "Next". It tried one threshold (30%) and two slow speeds (0.5, hold), so the result could be an artefact of those. Plan (`experiments/run_solar_policy_bound.py`, same boat, pack and daily NASA POWER series as 5g, 4 m and 5 m, five sites, 3,653 days, no source changes): (a) a constant-speed sweep 0.4 to 1.4 m/s; (b) the 5g threshold policy swept over threshold {10, 20, 30, 50}% x slow speed {0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 0.9} m/s; (c) a perfect-forecast upper bound on distance for ANY schedule. The bound: distance per day is 86.4 v while the day's energy is 24 P(v), P convex in v, so distance is concave in energy and (Jensen) the best schedule pooled over the decade spends (initial pack + total harvest) at one constant speed; ignoring pack capacity and the daily timing only loosens it, so it is an upper bound, not an achievable policy. Speed capped at 3 m/s.
- **Predictions (before running):** (1) no threshold policy in (b) beats the best constant speed in (a) by more than 1% in any of the 10 site-boat cases (prior 80%). (2) In cases where fixed 1.0 m/s leaves the pack empty on 5% or more of days, the perfect-forecast bound exceeds fixed 1.0 by under 10% (prior 75%): there the boat is energy-limited and 1 m/s is near the best-range speed. Refuted: (1) any case more than 1%; (2) any such case 10% or more.
- Limits known in advance: daily steps; the bound ignores pack capacity (a surplus in summer cannot be stored to winter) so it is loose where seasons differ; no round-trip loss or depth-of-discharge limit; one cell per site.

### Outcome of milestone 5h (`experiments/run_solar_policy_bound.py`, under 3 s, deterministic; `experiments/results_solar_policy_bound.txt`)
- **Correction made after seeing the first run (disclosed):** my first "perfect-forecast bound" (one constant speed spending pack plus total harvest, by Jensen) came out *below* simulated policies in four cases, so it was not a bound. The reason is the simulator's stall rule: a stopped boat draws nothing, so duty-cycling at the best-range speed beats any constant speed when energy-limited. The reported bound is the upper concave hull of the origin and the (energy, distance) curve at the pooled energy per day; the script now also checks that no simulated policy exceeds it (none does). Prediction 2 below was judged against this corrected bound.
- **(1) held:** no threshold policy (10 to 50% x 0.3 to 0.9 m/s) beats the best constant speed by more than 1% in any of the 10 cases. The best threshold policy is always the mildest (below 10%, slow to 0.9 m/s) and still sits 0.3 to 1.0 km/day under fixed 1.0 m/s in the energy-limited cases. So 5g's "slowing never raises distance" is not an artefact of the 30% and 0.5 m/s I picked.
- **(2) refuted, in the 5 m boat:** the corrected bound exceeds fixed 1.0 by 32% (Gulf of Maine), 25% (North Sea) and 16% (Gulf of Alaska) where the pack is empty on 29 to 53% of days. The 4 m boat cases are within 0 to 7% as predicted. My reasoning that an energy-limited boat sits at the best-range speed ignored that the 5 m boat has a summer surplus the 10 kWh pack cannot hold, so it is energy-limited in winter and energy-rich in summer.
- **Where the headroom is (exploratory, added after seeing the results, not pre-registered):** the best constant speed is 1.1 to 1.4 m/s, not 1.0, and a surge rule (1.0 m/s normally, a faster speed while the pack is above a level) reaches 87.5 km/day against 73.0 for fixed 1.0 at 5 m Gulf of Maine (+20%), 67.6 against 58.0 at 5 m North Sea (+17%), 58.8 against 52.9 at 5 m Gulf of Alaska (+11%), and 113.8 against 86.4 at 5 m Florida (+32%). For the 4 m boat the gain is 0 to 6%. Several best cases sit at the grid edge (1.6 m/s, 70%), so these are lower bounds on the rule's value. This reverses 5g's reading for the 5 m boat: the useful policy is not slowing when low but spending the surplus when full, which the clipped pack otherwise wastes.
- Limits: daily steps; a daily-mean harvest means the surge level is a pack level, not a clock rule; no round-trip loss or depth-of-discharge limit; faster speeds add wave-making the Michell scale 1 term only approximates (speeds up to 1.6 m/s, Fr about 0.26 at 5 m); the bound ignores pack capacity so it is loose; one 1-degree cell per site and one decade.
- Next: widen the surge grid past its edges and fit the rule on one half of the decade, test it on the other; milestone 3 still blocked on tank data.

## 2026-10-28 · Milestone 5g: does slowing down when the pack is low keep a deck-solar boat moving, or only keep it alive? (log written after the one run; no prediction was committed before it)
- Why: 5f's "Next": its empty-pack days assume the boat holds 1 m/s regardless. Plan (`experiments/run_solar_policy.py`, same boat, pack and NASA POWER daily series as 5f, 4 m and 5 m, all 3,653 days, year-round, start with a full pack): three policies, fixed 1.0 m/s; 1.0 m/s but 0.5 m/s while the pack is under 30%; 1.0 m/s but hold station (hotel load only) under 30%. Per policy: km/day, share of days the pack hits zero, share of days slowed. A day that would end below zero runs only the fraction its energy lasts.
- Disclosure: I did not commit predictions first. My unrecorded expectation was that slowing would raise distance by avoiding stalls; the data went the other way, so nothing here was tuned to a prior.

### Outcome of milestone 5g (`experiments/run_solar_policy.py`, under 1 s, deterministic; `experiments/results_solar_policy.txt`)
- **Slowing never raises distance.** Fixed 1 m/s covers the most km/day in all 10 site-boat cases (ties at the 5 m Equator, where nothing binds): e.g. 4 m Gulf-of-Maine 61.7 km/day fixed, 53.9 slowed, 39.5 held; 5 m North-Sea 58.0, 53.2, 44.1. The 30 W hotel load dominates at 0.5 m/s, so a slow boat spends nearly as much energy per day and covers half the distance; holding station is worse still.
- **What slowing buys is fewer dead days, not more distance.** Pack-empty days at 4 m Florida fall from 25.4% (fixed) to 4.2% (slow) and 1.5% (hold); 4 m Equator from 8.5% to 0.0%. Above 42 N slowing cuts empty days only modestly (5 m North-Sea 45.7% to 40.1%) because the deficit is seasonal, not a dull spell.
- The 5 m boat is nearly unaffected at 25 N and below (empty days 0.0 to 0.2%); at 42 N and above it is stalled 29 to 53% of days when fixed, so the pack, not the policy, is the limit in winter, matching 5f's year-round need of 41 kWh at 42 N.
- Reading: the speed policy is a reliability feature (keep the pack off zero, keep the survey recoverable), not a range feature, in a hotel-load-dominated boat; a policy that would raise distance has to be an optimal schedule over the forecast, not a threshold, which is not tried here.
- Check: the 5 m Equator case, where harvest exceeds load every day, returns exactly 86.4 km/day (1 m/s x 86,400 s) under every policy.
- Limits: threshold (30%) and the two slow speeds not swept; daily steps (no night or within-day shape); no round-trip loss or depth-of-discharge limit; one cell per site, one decade; the partial-day rule is an energy-proportional approximation; no forecast.
- Next: sweep the threshold and slow speed, and an optimal-schedule bound against a perfect forecast; milestone 3 still blocked.

## 2026-10-28 · Milestone 5f: do daily cloud runs break the monthly-mean "energy-neutral" verdict, and how big a pack bridges a year? (planned before running)
- Why: 5e called a boat neutral in a month when the monthly-mean harvest covers the load, but a battery bridges only so many overcast days. NASA POWER daily ALLSKY_SFC_SW_DWN (same cells as 5e, 2015-01-01 to 2024-12-31, 3,653 days per site, kept as fetched in `experiments/data/daily_<site>.json`) lets the check be run rather than assumed. Daily Wh = A eta_pv f I_day x 24 against load x 24, with I_day = kWh/m2/day x 1000/24.
- Plan (`experiments/run_solar_storm.py`, imports `solar.py` and `hull.py` unchanged): central boat of 5e (c_s 3.0, form 0.25, wave 1, 30 W hotel, eta_pv 0.20, derate 0.85, 200 kg, 1 m/s) at 4 m and 5 m, pack = 25% of mass at 200 Wh/kg = 10,000 Wh (panel mass ignored, as in 5e). (a) Operate only in months that 5e's monthly mean calls neutral; start each contiguous run of such days with a full pack; report days the pack hits empty, the worst drawdown in Wh (and as days of load), over the 10 years. (b) Year-round: per calendar year, the maximum drawdown from a running peak, which is the pack that bridges that year without the boat stopping; report the median and worst of the 10 years against 10,000 Wh.
- **Predictions (before running):** (1) in the monthly-neutral windows the 10 kWh pack never empties at any site for the 5 m boat (prior 80%). (2) For the 4 m boat at least one site-window does empty or draw down past 50% of the pack (prior 60%): its neutral months sit near the edge, so a run of dull days drains it. (3) Year-round at 42 N the 5 m boat needs more than 10 kWh in the median year (prior 85%); at the Equator it needs less than 10 kWh in every year (prior 85%). Refuted: (1) any empty day for 5 m; (2) all windows stay above 50% for 4 m; (3) either half fails.
- Limits known in advance: horizontal irradiance on a 1 degree cell, daily means (no within-day shape, so a pack that covers the night is assumed), ten years is a sample of weather not a return period, no battery round-trip loss or depth-of-discharge limit (a usable fraction below 100% would shrink the pack), panel mass ignored, one hull and one panel set.

### Outcome of milestone 5f (`experiments/run_solar_storm.py`, under 1 s, deterministic; `experiments/results_solar_storm.txt`; data as fetched in `experiments/data/daily_*.json`, 3,653 days per site)
- **(1) refuted, at one site:** the 5 m boat that 5e called neutral in all 12 months at Florida-Straits (25 N) empties the 10 kWh pack on 9 days in ten years, all in January (one day in 2016, eight consecutive days 14 to 22 January 2024); worst drawdown 11.2 kWh, 10.6 days of load. At the other four sites the 5 m boat in its monthly-neutral window never empties (worst drawdown 7% of the pack at the Equator, 29 to 70% elsewhere, 70% = 6.5 days of load at the Gulf of Alaska).
- **(2) held:** the 4 m boat draws the pack down 37 to 54% in its neutral windows (54% at the Equator and Florida, 5.0 to 5.2 days of load), never to empty. It crosses my 50% line at three sites; it is far less comfortable than 5 m but is not broken by ten years of weather.
- **(3) held:** year-round at 42 N the 5 m boat needs a median 41 kWh per year (4.1 times the pack; no year within 10 kWh); at the Equator it needs a median 0.45 kWh and at most 0.66 kWh. Year-round at 55 N and 60 N the need is 80 to 114 kWh (5 m) and 174 to 205 kWh (4 m), i.e. solar cannot carry a winter, only a summer.
- Reading: the monthly-mean verdict of 5e survives ten years of daily weather for a boat with margin (5 m, 42 N and above, summer window; 5 m at the Equator), but not where the monthly mean sits close to the load: Florida in January has a mean harvest that clears the load, and a dull week still emptied the pack. The pack the worst decade needs (11.2 kWh at 25 N, against 10 kWh fitted) is the figure to read, not the monthly neutrality flag. Equator 4 m is "neutral" in 7 months but needs a median 14.8 kWh year-round, so those months are not contiguous.
- Limits: as listed in the plan; additionally one 1-degree cell per site, one decade, no depth-of-discharge or round-trip losses (these only make the pack need bigger), the empty-day count is for a boat that holds 1 m/s regardless (a real controller would slow down, which this does not model).
- Next: let the controller slow down when the pack is low (a speed policy against the same daily series); storm-duration statistics beyond one decade; milestone 3 still blocked.

## 2026-10-28 · Milestone 5e: with sourced insolation at five named sites, in which months can the 5 m monohull sustain 1 m/s on deck solar? (planned before running)
- Why: 5c and 5d swept insolation 120 to 240 W/m2 because every irradiance host was refused. NASA POWER (`power.larc.nasa.gov`, climatology API, parameter ALLSKY_SFC_SW_DWN, all-sky horizontal shortwave, 25-year monthly means 2001 to 2025, SYN1DEG 1 degree cells) answered 200 this session. Files kept as fetched in `experiments/data/power_<site>.json`. Sites (chosen to span latitude, ocean survey areas): Equator-Pacific (0, -90), Florida-Straits (25, -80), Gulf-of-Maine (42, -70), North-Sea (55, 5), Gulf-of-Alaska (60, -145). Daily mean W/m2 = kWh/m2/day x 1000 / 24.
- Plan (`experiments/run_solar_sites.py`, imports `solar.py` unchanged): per site and month, same boat as 5c at central hull constants (c_s 3.0, form 0.25, wave 1), length 3/4/5 m, hotel 20/30/40 W, eta_pv 0.15/0.20/0.23, derate 0.70/0.85, 200 kg, 25% battery at 200 Wh/kg. Report (a) share of the 54 boat cases energy-neutral at 1 m/s per site-month, (b) for the central boat (30 W, 0.20, 0.85) the count of months neutral per site, at 4 m and 5 m, (c) whether the annual-mean insolation gives the same verdict as the worst month.
- **Predictions (before running):** (1) the 5 m central boat is neutral at 1 m/s in all 12 months at the Equator site (prior 80%) and in at most 6 months at the Gulf of Alaska (prior 80%). (2) At 42 N the annual-mean insolation calls the 5 m central boat neutral while December does not (prior 90%): a mean-based verdict overstates year-round neutrality. (3) The 4 m central boat is neutral in at most 3 months at every site (prior 65%). Refuted: (1) either half fails; (2) annual and December verdicts agree; (3) any site has 4 or more neutral months.
- Limits known in advance: horizontal-plane irradiance (a tilted or sea-state-pitching panel differs), monthly means (no cloud runs or storms: a battery covers days, not a week of overcast), one-degree cells, 25-year climatology rather than a given year, panel mass ignored here (5d: costs about a fifth of solar's range benefit at 10 kg/m2), hull constants fixed at central values.

### Outcome of milestone 5e (`experiments/run_solar_sites.py`, under 1 s, deterministic; `experiments/results_solar_sites.txt`; data as fetched in `experiments/data/`)
- **(1) held:** the 5 m central boat is neutral at 1 m/s in 12 of 12 months at the Equator site (August, the worst, still clears: 200 W/m2 gives about 51 W against a 44 W load) and in 5 months at the Gulf of Alaska (needed at most 6).
- **(2) held:** at 42 N the annual mean (161 W/m2) calls the 5 m central boat neutral (49 W against 44 W) but December (53 W/m2) does not, and neither do Nov to Mar: it is neutral in 7 months. Same at 55 N and 60 N, where even the annual mean fails. A verdict from annual-mean insolation overstates year-round neutrality at every site above 25 N for 4 m and above 42 N for 5 m.
- **(3) refuted:** the 4 m central boat is neutral in 7 months at the Equator site, 6 at 25 N, 4 at 42 N, 3 at 55 N and 0 at 60 N. I had put the sustained-speed 4 m boat further from the edge than the data does; 5c's swept 180 W/m2 central case was just short, and the sourced low-latitude months are 200 to 280 W/m2.
- Share of the 54 boats neutral by site-month ranges from 56% (Equator, March) down to 0% for the winter half of the year at 42 N and above. Seasonal swing, not the annual mean, sets the answer: the Gulf of Alaska has a 7 to 220 W/m2 range (a factor 31).
- Reading: a 5 m deck-solar monohull can hold survey speed all year within about 25 degrees of the equator and for a summer half-year near 42 N, but a year-round high-latitude survey needs the battery to bridge the winter, not just the night. Carries to the programme's H5/range claims only as a boundary on when solar replaces the pack.
- Limits: horizontal irradiance, monthly means (a battery bridges nights, not a stormy week), 1 degree cells, no panel mass or tilt, central hull constants, hull and panel inputs as in 5c; five sites is a sample, not a map.
- Next: panel mass inside the monthly picture (5d at each site); a storm-duration check on pack size; milestone 3 still blocked.

## 2026-10-28 · Milestone 5d: does charging panel mass to the battery change the solar verdict, and is the full deck the best array? (planned before running)
- Why: 5c's limits list "panel mass not charged to the 200 kg". A panel of area A weighs a_m A, taken from the 50 kg battery allowance (total mass stays 200 kg, so the hull is unchanged). Sourced irradiance is still unreachable (NASA POWER and PVGIS return 403 through the egress proxy this session), so insolation stays swept. Plan (`src/marine_hull/solar.py` gains `battery_after_panels`; `experiments/run_solar_mass.py`): same grid as 5c plus areal mass a_m 2, 5, 10 kg/m2 (flexible marine laminate to framed module; not sourced, swept) and array area 0, 25, 50, 75, 100% of the deck. Per case: best-speed range with solar (inf if neutral at any speed) for each area fraction, the area fraction that maximises it (ties to the smaller array), and the gain over no array.
- **Predictions (before running):** (1) at 5 kg/m2 the full deck is the best array in at least 80% of cases (prior 80%: 1.15 m2 at 4 m weighs under 6 kg of a 50 kg pack). (2) at 10 kg/m2 the full deck is still best in at least 50% (prior 55%). (3) the share of cases energy-neutral at 1 m/s drops by less than 3 points from 5c's 25.1% at 5 kg/m2 (prior 70%; mass does not change the load, so neutrality only depends on area and the load, and panel mass only matters through range where it is not neutral). Refuted: (1) under 80%; (2) under 50%; (3) 3 points or more.
- Limits known in advance: areal mass is swept, not sourced; mounting hardware and wiring ignored; panel mass displaces only battery (a hull sized to 200 kg total); daily-mean harvest as in 5c.

### Outcome of milestone 5d (`experiments/run_solar_mass.py`, under 10 s, deterministic; `experiments/results_solar_mass.txt`; 4,374 cases per areal mass; 46 tests pass)
- **(1) refuted as I wrote the metric, for a bookkeeping reason:** the full deck is the best array in 73.0% of cases (needed 80%), identical at 2, 5 and 10 kg/m2. The other 27% are not cases where panels hurt: a 50 or 75% array is already energy-neutral there, so unbounded range ties with the full deck and the tie rule picks the smaller one. Among the 2,388 cases with finite range the full deck is best in **100%** at every areal mass. My metric mixed those two groups; the finite-range reading is the meaningful one.
- **(2) held:** 73.0% at 10 kg/m2 (needed 50%), same caveat.
- **(3) held, and by construction:** neutral-at-1-m/s share with the full deck stays 25.1% at every areal mass, because neutrality compares harvest with load and neither depends on pack mass. The prediction could not have failed; it was not a test.
- What mass does change is the range multiplier where the array cannot carry the boat: median best-array range / no-array range is 1.95, 1.83 and 1.64 at 2, 5 and 10 kg/m2 (5c, no mass: 2.01); the 10th percentile falls from 1.28 to 1.13. So a heavy framed module (10 kg/m2, about 12 kg on a 4 m deck, a quarter of the pack at 50 kg) costs about a fifth of solar's range benefit; the full deck is still the best layout.
- Limits: areal mass swept and not sourced (no datasheet fetched); mounting hardware and wiring ignored; panels displace only battery; daily-mean harvest and swept insolation as in 5c; the 25% area steps are coarse.
- Next: a sourced irradiance table for a named latitude and month (NASA POWER, PVGIS and the Global Solar Atlas are all refused by the proxy this session; try a GitHub-hosted dataset via `add_repo`); milestone 3 still blocked.

## 2026-10-28 · Milestone 5c: how much deck-mounted solar makes the 4 m monohull energy-neutral at survey speed? (planned before running)
- Why: milestone 5 asks for battery and solar rungs. Solar changes the question from "range per charge" to "is there a speed at which the boat never needs one". Plan (`src/marine_hull/solar.py`, `experiments/run_solar.py`): daily-mean harvest P_s = A eta_pv f I, with panel area A set by deck area (0.6 L B, beam B = 0.12 L for the Wigley form of milestone 2, so about 1.15 m2 at 4 m), eta_pv 0.15/0.20/0.23 (commercial crystalline-silicon modules), derate f 0.7/0.85 (mounting angle, soiling, spray), daily-mean insolation I 120/180/240 W/m2 (swept: the global-mean surface value is roughly 185 W/m2 from memory, not re-fetched; low latitudes and clear summer days are higher, high latitudes and winter lower). Hull: the milestone-1 ITTC-plus-form-factor model (c_s 2.6/3.0/3.4, k_form 0.10/0.25/0.40, wave scale 0/0.5/1, length 3/4/5 m, hotel 20/30/40 W, same efficiencies), 200 kg. Outputs per case: (a) is net power at 1 m/s <= 0 (energy-neutral, unbounded range on a daily average), (b) the highest energy-neutral speed over 0.5 to 3 m/s, (c) the panel area that makes 1 m/s neutral.
- **Predictions (before running):** (1) energy-neutral at 1 m/s in between 30% and 70% of cases (prior 50%: at the central case the load is about 45 W and the harvest about 40 W, so the answer sits on the edge). (2) The highest neutral speed is 1.5 m/s or more in under 10% of cases (prior 85%: resistance grows with v^2 on top of the hotel load, so a few more m/s needs hundreds of watts). (3) Where it is not neutral, solar still lifts best-speed range by a median factor above 1.5 (prior 60%). Refuted: (1) outside 30 to 70%; (2) 10% or more; (3) 1.5 or below.
- Limits known in advance: daily mean, so no night (the battery covers it; a 25% pack of 200 Wh/kg is 10 kWh against a daily load near 1 kWh); no clouds or sea state in the harvest; no panel weight (the 200 kg is fixed, panels would displace battery); insolation is swept, not sourced.

### Outcome of milestone 5c (`experiments/run_solar.py`, under 5 s, deterministic; `experiments/results_solar.txt`; 4,374 cases; 45 tests pass)
- **(1) refuted, narrowly low:** energy-neutral at 1 m/s in **25.1%** of cases (needed 30 to 70%). It is set by length, which sets deck area (about L^2): 0% at 3 m, 20% at 4 m, 56% at 5 m; then by insolation (5%, 25%, 45% at 120, 180, 240 W/m2), hotel load (40%, 23%, 13% at 20, 30, 40 W) and panel efficiency. Hull-form constants and the wave term barely move it (22 to 28%; the wave term not at all at Fr 0.16). My prior put the central case on the edge; the central case (4 m, 30 W, 0.20, 0.85, 180) is just short.
- **(2) held:** the highest energy-neutral speed is 1.5 m/s or more in 3.2% of cases and 2.0 m/s in none (max 1.85). Median 0 means that in most cases even 0.5 m/s is not neutral.
- **(3) held:** where the array cannot carry the boat at its best speed (2,388 cases), solar multiplies range by a median 2.01 (10th to 90th 1.34 to 7.63).
- Panel area for neutral 1 m/s: median 1.67 m2 (10th to 90th 0.99 to 2.84), against a usable deck of 0.6 L B, so the deck is too small in 74.9% of cases.
- Reading: deck solar can make a 5 m survey boat self-sustaining at 1 m/s in good sun, but for a 4 m boat it roughly doubles range rather than removing the battery, and no case sustains 2 m/s. Hotel load is as important as the array: halving it from 40 to 20 W triples the neutral share.
- Limits: daily-mean harvest (no night, cloud or sea state); panel mass not charged to the 200 kg; insolation recalled (global mean about 185 W/m2), not re-fetched, so swept 120 to 240; efficiencies are typical module figures, not a datasheet; Wigley beam ratio 0.12 and fill 0.6 are choices.
- Next: a sourced irradiance table for a named latitude and month (PVGIS or NASA POWER if reachable); panel mass traded against battery; milestone 3 still blocked.

## 2026-10-28 · Milestone 5b: does a SWATH (two submerged torpedoes on surface-piercing struts) out-range the 4 m monohull at 200 kg? (predictions fixed before reading the output; this entry was written after the first run, which was the only run)
- Why: the H5 rungs left SWATH undone (hydrofoil was milestone 5). A SWATH hides its displacement below the free surface, so wave drag should fall; the cost is wetted area. New in `src/marine_hull/michell.py`: `wave_resistance_torpedo` (Michell thin-ship integral for a fully submerged parabolic torpedo y = b(1-xi^2)(1-zeta^2) at axis depth h; x part is the Wigley closed form, z part a Simpson sum), `torpedo_volume`, `torpedo_wetted`. Plan (`experiments/run_swath.py`): two torpedoes carry all 200 kg (fineness set by length), one Wigley-form strut each (chord f L, thickness 0.12 chord) with Michell wave drag and ITTC friction on both faces, Havelock two-hull factor on both wave terms. Grid: hull length 3/4/5 m, axis depth 0.4/0.6/0.8 m, hull spacing s/L 0.15/0.25/0.40, strut chord 0.3/0.5 L, SWATH form factor 0.05/0.10/0.20, monohull (4 m Wigley, milestone 2) form factor 0.10/0.25/0.40, Michell scale 0.5/1/1.5, battery 150/200/250 Wh/kg: 4,374 pairs. Same 25% battery fraction, 30 W hotel load and efficiencies as milestones 1 and 2.
- **Predictions:** (1) the SWATH out-ranges the monohull at its own best speed in under 10% of pairs (prior 85%: best speed is near 1 m/s, Fr 0.16, where the monohull's wave drag is already a few percent of friction, so the SWATH's extra wetted area cannot pay back). (2) At a required 3 m/s, the SWATH out-ranges the monohull in over half of the pairs (prior 55%). Refuted: (1) 10% or more; (2) half or fewer.
- Limits known in advance: struts are Wigley forms (parabolic in depth), not foil sections; torpedo is a parabolic body, not a body of revolution; thin-ship theory applied to a submerged body of half-beam 0.12 m at 0.4 to 0.8 m depth; strut buoyancy and the strut-hull junction drag are ignored; no stability or seakeeping, which are the reason SWATHs exist.

### Outcome of milestone 5b (`experiments/run_swath.py`, 25 s, deterministic; `experiments/results_swath.txt`; 4,374 pairs)
- **(1) held, at the extreme:** the SWATH out-ranges the monohull in 0 of 4,374 pairs at its best speed. Best-range ratio SWATH/monohull: median 0.69 (10th to 90th 0.61 to 0.76, max 0.83). SWATH best speed median 0.75 m/s, range median 569 km against 824 km.
- **(2) refuted:** at a fixed 3 m/s the SWATH wins in 0 of 4,374 pairs (range ratio median 0.46; at 2 m/s median 0.27), and in no slice of any axis (length, depth, spacing, strut chord, wave scale). I expected submerging the displacement to pay once the monohull's wave drag hump arrives; it does not, because the struts' own wave drag (central case: SWATH 327 N at 3 m/s, of which 180 N is wave, against 101 N for the monohull) and the larger wetted area (central case: two torpedoes 2.4 m2 each plus 3.8 m2 of strut faces, against about 3 m2 for the monohull) outweigh the torpedoes' small wave drag.
- Reading: at 200 kg, 4 to 5 m of length and 0.4 to 0.8 m of axis depth, a SWATH is not a range-efficient way to carry a survey payload in this model. Its case would rest on what the model leaves out: stability, deck area and motion in waves. Together with milestones 1 and 2 (catamaran) and the hydrofoil rung this closes the three displacement-hull alternatives to the monohull on energy per kilometre in calm water.
